## OF ECCLESIASTICAL WRITERS
# A Literary History
### From the Birth of Christ until the 14th Century. <note>original: "Sæculum XIV."</note>
->Arranged in an easy method.<-

->WHEREIN<-

->Their lives and deeds, their sect, dogmas, praises, and style; their genuine, doubtful, forged, unpublished, and lost writings, and fragments; and the various editions of their works are clearly treated.<-

->TO WHICH ARE ADDED<-

## **PAGAN WRITERS,** <note>original: "Gentiles," referring here to non-Christian classical authors who interacted with or opposed Christianity.</note>
->Opponents of the Christian Religion; and a **SUMMARY** <note>original: "Breviarium," a concise overview of historical events.</note> of every Century.<-

->Inserted in their proper places are several small works and fragments of the Ancients, both Greek and Latin, hitherto unpublished.<-

->Finally, a **PREFACE** <note>original: "Prolegomena," introductory treatises providing the necessary scholarly background for the study of antiquity.</note> is provided, in which many matters concerning the study of Church Antiquity are presented.<-

->*A work equipped with the necessary indexes.*<-

->*B Y  T H E  A U T H O R*<-

## **WILLIAM CAVE**, Professor of Sacred Theology,
->Canon of Windsor.<-

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page is the formal dedication of the work, following the title page. The author, William Cave, is addressing the Church of England (Ecclesia Anglicana) as a maternal figure, positioning his scholarly history of early Christian writers as a defense of Anglican tradition, which he argues is the true successor to the "primitive" early church.</meta>

## TO THE MOST HOLY AND DEAREST
# MOTHER,
### THE CHURCH OF ENGLAND,

->*May this be happy and auspicious.*<-

Mother, I send out into the public this work, born under your protection <note>original: "Auspiciis," referring to favorable guidance or patronage.</note>, crafted specifically for the use of your sons, and therefore justly and deservedly dedicated to your Name. Certainly, all those who are not led by an unfair partisan zeal honor and revere you as the teacher of true piety, the champion of Universal <term>Catholic; here meaning the "whole" or "universal" church of the first centuries, rather than the Roman Catholic denomination</term> doctrine, and the most beautiful model of early Antiquity. Your faith is the same as that which was once delivered to the Saints; the teachings <note>original: "dogmata"</note> of the Church Fathers and the decrees of the Councils stand with you. From you come all the Creeds <note>original: "Symbola"</note> of the Ancient Church, neither painted with artificial colors nor expanded or altered with any new Articles of Faith. Within your archives, the divine Oracles <note>A common scholarly term for the Holy Scriptures.</note> are preserved, not hidden in secret <note>original: "en tois apokryphois," a play on the word "Apocrypha," suggesting hidden or non-canonical spaces.</note>, nor kept behind bars and screens, nor known only to the learned and those who speak many languages <note>original: "polyglōttois"</note>, but set forth in the common tongue, read in churches, homes, and workplaces, known to everyone and accessible everywhere. *For all Scripture is inspired by God and useful, and was written by the Holy Spirit for this one reason* (as St. Basil notes in his Preface to the Psalms, p. 125): *so that, as if in a common hospital for souls* <note>original: "koinō tōn psychōn iatreiō"</note>, *all people might each select the remedy for their own particular suffering* <note>original: "to iama tou oikeiou pathous"</note>; so that from the Scriptures, just as from a public workshop for the healing of souls, everyone might be able to choose a healthy and appropriate cure for their own illness. With you, the Lord's Sacraments retain their proper dignity, reverence, and unblemished integrity; I speak of the venerable mysteries of the Christian religion—not swollen to the number of seven <note>A reference to the Anglican rejection of the five "Roman" sacraments in favor of the two "Gospel" sacraments: Baptism and the Eucharist.</note>, not heavy with monstrous dogmas, nor burdened with frivolous and senseless rites, but pure, sincere, and simple, just as they were once established by the Lord and handed down by the Apostles. Your churches are pure, dignified, and beautiful; neither stained by filth,

<meta>catchword: neither</meta>

<summary>A formal dedication to the Church of England, where the author praises the Anglican Church for maintaining the purity of the early Christian faith, the accessibility of the Bible, and the simplicity of the sacraments.</summary>
<keywords>Church of England, Dedication, Church Fathers, Creeds, Holy Scripture, Sacraments, St. Basil, Primitive Antiquity</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the dedication to the Church of England. In the previous section, the author praised the Church for making the Scriptures accessible to all. Here, he contrasts the simplicity and order of the Anglican Church with the perceived excesses of Roman Catholicism and affirms its continuity with the early Church Fathers.</meta>

neither is it constructed and adorned for theatrical display; not swollen with boxes of relics <note>original: "Reliquiarum thecis." This refers to the ornate containers used to hold physical remains of saints, a practice the author is criticizing as excessive.</note> scraped together from everywhere, nor glowing with the immense brilliance of crystal lamps; it is not proud of images of the Saints, polished with every art and expense—which Epiphanius <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403 AD) was a Church Father who famously tore down a curtain depicting a saint, arguing it was contrary to Christian teaching.</note> once clearly affirmed could not be retained in the Church of Christ *against the authority of the Scriptures and our Religion*. Every duty of your Liturgy, the solemn formulas of Prayer, and the Rites and Ceremonies used in sacred things—few in number, grave in their institution, and useful in their observation—testify that your public worship is truly primitive, universal <term>Catholic; used here in its original sense of "universal" or "orthodox," referring to the shared traditions of the early church before later divisions</term>, pious, and worthy of God. In you, we admire and proclaim likewise the ancient government received from the Apostles themselves: the Orders of Bishops, Priests, and Deacons succeeding one another in turn. You restrain the power granted to you by the Lord within its proper limits; you render to *Caesar the things that are Caesar’s* in full, and to *God the things that are God’s*. In your house, if anywhere else, the sacred majesty of Kings is preserved inviolate, and enjoys its privileges with no one envying and no one tearing them away. With you, the ancient Canons <note>The "Canons" are the historical laws and regulations of the Church.</note> hold their honor, the better part of which you have transcribed into your own Synodical Code; to these, as much as modern times will allow, you conform yourself entirely, and you earnestly desire that the vigor of the original discipline be restored to its wholeness. To be brief: in you we behold the simplicity of the age of Cyprian <note>St. Cyprian (c. 210–258 AD), a Bishop of Carthage known for his focus on church unity and discipline during times of Roman persecution.</note> renewed, the zeal and purity of the age of Constantine, and the dignity and good order <note>original: εὐταξίαν (*eutaxian*)</note> of the age of Theodosius—would that I could also add, their good fortune. May the ancient faith, virtue, and simplicity flourish in you more and more; may your Laity be blameless in their character, firm and brave in their faith; may your Priests be clothed with justice and adorned with learning; and may the *British Clergy be*, as they have been until now, the *wonder of the world* for all time. May you find the most serene King, and all those placed in high positions, to be favorable toward you, so that you may be protected by the defense of those for whose safety you pour out prayers to God daily, and so that all your people may lead a quiet and tranquil life, with all piety and gravity. So vows, wishes, and prays from the heart,

->Given in the College of Windsor,<- \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ ->The least of your sons,<-
->October 4, 1688.<-
\ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ \ ->**WILLIAM CAVE.**<-

<summary>William Cave concludes his dedication by praising the Church of England's "primitive" simplicity, its apostolic hierarchy of bishops, and its loyalty to the monarchy, famously calling the British clergy the "wonder of the world."</summary>
<keywords>William Cave, Church of England, Anglicanism, Epiphanius, Apostolic Succession, Bishops, Clergy, Monarchy, Church History, 1688</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>catchword: TABLE OF CONTENTS. This page contains a dedicatory poem written in Greek by Abednego Seller, addressed to William Cave. It follows a prose dedication to the Church of England. While the previous page praised the institution of the Church, this poem praises Cave as a modern scholar who has restored the history of the Church Fathers, placing him in a lineage of great bibliographers and historians.</meta>

## To the most esteemed Theologian William Cave, who has truly attained
## every kind of learning, and the first place in sacred criticism.

**H**ail, man thrice-longed-for, you are heard as a friend by the blessed ones,
And you are a friend to mortals, as you are a friend to God;
Many men of old, indeed, were gentle and very learned,
Setting in order the records of the Fathers and their fair testimonies,
Distinguishing the Scriptures—which are spurious and which are genuine—
And the wretched doctrines of the heretics.
First among them was the very famous Bishop Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD), often called the "Father of Church History."</note>, and then after him,
The tireless voice of the presbyter Spyridon <note>original: "Σπυριδωνός." Likely referring to a historian or perhaps a corruption in the text regarding Gennadius of Marseilles, who continued the work of Jerome and Eusebius.</note>,
Or Gennadius <note>Gennadius of Marseilles (5th century), author of *De Viris Illustribus* (On Illustrious Men).</note>, or "Massiliensis" <note>original: "Μαασπιλιὸς." Likely a corruption of Gennadius's title as being from Marseilles.</note> wrestling briefly
With the authors for the sake of public benefit.
These things flourished when Justice <note>original: "Θέμις" (Themis).</note> and Apollo were as one,
And the Graces recognized a venerable fatherland.
Later, the Sacred Ship <note>A common metaphor for the Church.</note> descended into an inescapable surge of evils,
Suffering a terrible and shivering disturbance;
She was mixed with the swell of Hades, her sails loosened in the waters,
And barbarism prevailed, giving contrary signs.
Greece was taken, and venerable Rome
Became a stone to the northern portents <note>A reference to the "Dark Ages" and the invasions that disrupted classical and patristic learning.</note>;
And then there were Isidore <note>Isidore of Seville (c. 560–636 AD).</note>, Honorius <note>Honorius Augustodunensis (12th century).</note>, and Ildefonsus <note>Ildefonsus of Toledo (607–667 AD).</note>,
(Who were then the "phoenixes" <note>Unique or rare scholars in a period of decline.</note> of Spanish theology),
And Sigebert, a monk of the land of Gembloux <note>Sigebert of Gembloux (c. 1030–1112), a medieval chronicler.</note>,
And Trithemius <note>Johannes Trithemius (1462–1516), author of the first comprehensive bibliography of ecclesiastical writers.</note>, appearing later,
(Of whose works many are not noble, but mixed with many wretched things).
Lambertius <note>Possibly Lambert of St. Omer (c. 1061–1125).</note> shone for a short time in that dark age.
Forgive me, blessed man, joined among the saints,
O Photius <note>Photius the Great (c. 810–893 AD), Patriarch of Constantinople, whose *Bibliotheca* is a foundational work of literary history.</note>, you who are the glory of all the East—
Photius, though struck by the profane thunderbolts of the Romans,
You are all the more a friend to the heavenly ones;
You truly attained every kind of learning,
Both of the Romans and of the Greeks.
It is right to gather flowers for your crown of hymns,
Though it is like a small lamp trying to illuminate the sun.
But now, with the awakening of the Muses and of piety,
Not a few men record the lives of the Fathers:
Sixtus <note>Sixtus Senensis (1520–1569), a Dominican who wrote the *Bibliotheca Sancta*.</note>, Possevino <note>Antonio Possevino (1533–1611), a Jesuit bibliographer.</note>, Aubertus Miraeus <note>Aubert le Mire (1573–1640), an ecclesiastical historian.</note>,
Gesner <note>Conrad Gessner (1516–1565), the "father of modern bibliography."</note>, Rivet <note>André Rivet (1572–1651), a French Huguenot theologian.</note>, and our own Robert Cooke <note>original: "Κόκῳ ἡμεδαπῷ." Robert Cooke (1550–1615), an English theologian who wrote on the detection of forged patristic texts.</note>,
And Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), the famous Jesuit cardinal and controversialist.</note> (a great wonder!), though an Appelline judge <note>original: "ἀπελλινῆς... δικάζων." A reference to his role as a critic/judge of ecclesiastical literature.</note>;
At other times he judged the writings of the Greeks with over-critical eyes.
Then came Labbe <note>Philippe Labbe (1607–1667), a Jesuit known for his massive work on the Church Councils.</note>, a formidable and very bitter judge,
To the Greeks a stranger, but to his own, fitting.
Oudin <note>Casimir Oudin (1638–1717), who wrote a history of ecclesiastical writers.</note> followed, very stealthily, for in his book
He pulled away as much from others' books as he had himself,
A title-page for printers, a sign ✝ for booksellers,
Full of errors and trifles, like no others.
Even a key has been placed upon his own tongue <note>A metaphor suggesting Oudin's work is either silenced by its own poor quality or he is being accused of plagiarism.</note>,
He who drew so much water from the wells of others.
But you, noble man, at the end of your labor in "Sparta" <note>original: "Σπάρτιῳ." Likely a metaphor for Cave's disciplined, rigorous scholarly life (referencing the proverb "You have obtained Sparta, now adorn it").</note>,
Having once been a *Chartophylax* <term>Archivist; literally a "guard of papers," an official in the Greek church responsible for records</term>, and now an *Oikonomos* <term>Steward; a high-ranking church administrator</term>,
Revealing the hidden treasures of the libraries,
Discerning correctly the dogmas of the Church and of the heretics,
The ancient decrees, the laws of kings,
The letters of bishops, their sayings, and the councils.
Hail, glorious man, may you be vigorous in your deep old age,
And may all things be unbending and a possession for you.
Grow old in a long life like the thrice-aged Pylian <note>Nestor, the wise king of Pylos in Homer's *Iliad*, known for his longevity and sweet speech.</note>,
From whose tongue flowed speech sweeter than honey.

->Yours worthily,<-

->ABEDNEGO SELLER.<-

<summary>A Greek encomium by Abednego Seller praising William Cave for his monumental work in ecclesiastical bibliography and history. The poem places Cave at the pinnacle of a long tradition of scholars—from Eusebius to Bellarmine—who cataloged the lives and writings of the Church Fathers.</summary>
<keywords>William Cave, Abednego Seller, Church Fathers, Bibliography, Ecclesiastical History, Photius, Eusebius, Sigebert of Gembloux, Bellarmine, Patristics</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the formal dedication to the Church of England by William Cave, shifting from the availability of Scripture to a defense of Anglican liturgy, governance, and its relationship to the state, contrasting it with the perceived excesses of the Roman Catholic Church.</meta>

...nor are you built and decorated for theatrical display; you are not swollen with caskets of **Relics** <term>Reliquiarum; physical remains of saints or objects they touched, which Cave suggests were gathered unscrupulously from everywhere</term> scraped together from every side, nor are you gleaming with the immense splendor of crystal lamps. You are not proud of most elegant **Images of Saints**, refined by every art and at great price, which **Epiphanius** <note>Epiphanius of Salamis (c. 310–403 AD) was a Church Father famous for his opposition to icons and images in churches.</note> once clearly affirmed *could not be retained in the Church of Christ except against the authority of the Scriptures and our Religion*. Every office of your **Liturgy**, your solemn formulas of Prayer, and the Rites and Ceremonies used in your sacred services—few in number, grave in their institution, and useful in their observation—all testify that your public worship is truly primitive, universal <term>catholicum; used here in the sense of "universal" or "orthodox" rather than "Roman Catholic"</term>, pious, and worthy of God. In you, we admire and proclaim equally that ancient government received from the Apostles themselves: the Orders of Bishops, Priests, and Deacons succeeding one another. The power granted to you by the Lord you restrain within its proper limits; what belongs to **Caesar**, you give fully to **Caesar**, and what is **God's**, to **God**. <note>A reference to Matthew 22:21, emphasizing the Anglican doctrine of Royal Supremacy and loyalty to the monarch.</note> Within your walls, if anywhere else, the sacrosanct majesty of Kings is preserved inviolate, and each enjoys their privileges with no one envying and no one tearing them away. The ancient Canons hold their due honor with you, the better part of which you have transcribed into your **Synodal Code** <term>Codicem Synodicum; the collected laws and canons governing the Church</term>. To these, as much as modern times will allow, you entirely conform yourself, and you earnestly desire that the vigor of the former discipline be restored to its wholeness. To be brief: in you we behold the simplicity of the **Cyprianic** age renewed, the zeal and purity of the **Constantinian** era, and the beauty and **good order** <gloss>original: ευταξίαν (eutaxian)</gloss> of the **Theodosian** <note>Cave refers to the eras of St. Cyprian (3rd century), Constantine the Great, and Theodosius I (4th century) as the gold standards of Christian history.</note>—would that I could also add, its happiness. May the ancient faith, virtue, and simplicity flourish more and more within you; may your Laity be blameless in their morals and firm and brave in their faith; may your Priests be clothed in justice and adorned with learning, and may the **British Clergy** <term>Clerus Britannus; Cave famously described the English clergy as "stupor mundi," the wonder of the world, for their scholarship.</term> be, for all time as they have been until now, the wonder of the world. May you find the most serene King and all those placed in high authority to be favorable toward you, so that you may be protected by the defense of those for whose safety you daily pour out prayers to God, and that all your people may lead a quiet and tranquil life in all piety and gravity. So vows, wishes, and prays from the heart,

Given in the College
at Windsor, October 4,
1688.

->The least of your sons,<-

->**WILLIAM CAVE.**<-

<summary>In this concluding dedication, Cave praises the Church of England for its "primitive" simplicity, apostolic governance, and loyalty to the Crown, contrasting it with the "theatrical" excesses of Rome. He concludes with a famous tribute to the learning of the British clergy.</summary>
<keywords>Epiphanius, liturgy, apostolic succession, Royal Supremacy, Church Canons, St. Cyprian, Constantine the Great, British Clergy (stupor mundi), Windsor</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page transitions from a Latin dedication addressed to the Church of England to a Greek encomium (a poem of high praise) dedicated to the scholar William Cave. The previous page emphasized the Anglican Church's continuity with the primitive church and its rejection of "theatrical" Roman Catholic practices. This poem, written by Abednego Seller, praises Cave for his monumental work in cataloging and defending the writings of the Early Church Fathers.</meta>

## To the most highly valued Theologian, William Cave, who has truly achieved 
## all learning, and holds the first rank in sacred criticism.

Greetings, man of renown! You are known as a friend to the blessed saints; you are a friend to mortals, and you are a friend to God. 

Many good men of old, deep in learning, provided the defense of the Fathers and excellent testimonies. They discerned which writings were spurious and which were genuine, and they separated out the mournful dogmas of the heretics. There was Eusebius, the very famous Bishop, and then after him... <note>original: "Σπυριδωνὸς χολὶς" (Spyridon's gall/zeal). This is a difficult phrase, likely a poetic reference to the historian Socrates Scholasticus or a specific anecdote involving Saint Spyridon's defense of orthodoxy.</note> 

Then Gennadius and Matalius, chirping slightly <note>original: "πελαρίζων" – acting like a stork or chirping; a playful reference to their scholarly commentary.</note> as authors for those who follow. These things occurred when divine Justice and the brightness of Apollo and the Graces were known to the venerable fathers. 

But did the sacred ship later descend into an inescapable wave of evils, a wretched and fearful turmoil? The sky was mixed with the sea, the stars dissolved in the waters, and a barbarity hostile to life prevailed with blood. Greece was captured—even Greece!—and mistress Rome became the prey of the northern plunderers <note>This refers to the "Dark Ages" following the fall of the Roman Empire and the subsequent loss of classical learning.</note>.

And then Isidore, Honorius, and Ildefonsus appeared (who were then the Phoenixes among Spanish Theologians); then Sigebert, a monk of the land of Gembloux, and Trithemius, a treasurer of the archives. (Of their works, many were excellent, yet mixed with many things that were mournful.) They brightened that dark age only a little.

Forgive me, blessed man, united with the saints—I speak of Photius <note>Photius the Great (c. 810–893 AD), Patriarch of Constantinople, whose 'Bibliotheca' is a primary source for ancient Greek literature.</note>, the glory of all the East! Photius, though struck by the thunderbolts of the Romans as if he were profane, is yet even more a friend to those in heaven. You, Cave, have truly attained all kinds of learning, both Roman and Greek. 

I have come to crown you with flowers of hymns... but not a few others have also chronicled the lives of the fathers: Hessus, Possevino, Aubertus Miraeus, Gesner, Rivera, and our own Coke <note>James Coke (1563–1644), an English scholar who wrote on the authenticity of the Fathers.</note>. 

There was Bellarmine, who—though he knew no Greek (a great wonder!)—judged the foreign writings of the Greeks with excessive criticism. Labbe followed him, a formidable judge, very bitter, a stranger to Greece but possessing its spirit. Finally, there is Oudin <note>Casimir Oudin (1638–1717), a contemporary bibliographer whom the author accuses of plagiarism.</note>, quite thievish, who took as much from the books of others as his own book contains—even the titles from printers and the marks of booksellers; his errors are like no others.

But you, noble man, having labored in your own Sparta <note>A reference to the Greek proverb "Spartam nactus es, hanc exorna" (You have found Sparta, now adorn it), meaning to do one's duty in the place one is assigned.</note>, did not merely draw a little water from the surface of the well. 

Once a *Chartophylax* <term>Keeper of the Records; specifically the title of Cave's previous work, 'Chartophylax Ecclesiasticus' (1685)</term>, now you have become an *Oeconomus* <term>Steward or Manager; a play on Cave's role in organizing the "household" of church history</term>, revealing the hidden treasures of libraries. You correctly diagnose the orthodox and the heretical, the primary dogmas, the ancient foundations of kings, short writings, songs, and synods.

Hail, famous man! May you be strong in deep old age; may God give you unbending strength and sturdy limbs. Grow old like the long-lived Nestor of Pylos <note>A legendary king from Homer's Iliad known for his wisdom and great age.</note>, from whose tongue flowed speech sweeter than any honey.

->Yours in holiness,<-

->AB. SELLER<-

<summary>A Greek poem by Abednego Seller praising William Cave's scholarly achievements in ecclesiastical history, contrasting his accuracy with the errors and biases of previous scholars like Bellarmine and Oudin.</summary>
<keywords>William Cave, Abednego Seller, Photius, Bellarmine, ecclesiastical history, Patrology, bibliographical criticism, Greek encomium</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>continuity: Following the Greek dedicatory poem to William Cave, this page serves as the Table of Contents (ELENCHUS) for his "Historia Literaria" (Literary History). It organizes the history of the Church into specific "centuries" named after the dominant theological movement or figure of each era.</meta>

# TABLE OF THE CENTURIES.

I. **A**POSTOLIC Century. Page 1. <note>The 1st Century AD, covering the lives of the Apostles and their immediate successors.</note>
Contains 25 Ecclesiastical Writers.
1 Pagan.

II. **G**NOSTIC Century. p. 25. <note>The 2nd Century, named for the rise of Gnosticism, a diverse movement of mystical religious ideas that the early Church Fathers fought to define as heresy.</note>
66 Ecclesiastical Writers.
7 Pagans.

III. **N**OVATIAN Century. p. 63. <note>The 3rd Century, named after Novatian, a scholar and priest who founded a rigorist sect over the issue of how to treat Christians who had lapsed during Roman persecutions.</note>
42 Ecclesiastical Writers.
2 Pagans.

IV. **A**RIAN Century. p. 110. <note>The 4th Century, dominated by the controversy surrounding Arius, who taught that Jesus was a created being rather than co-eternal with God the Father.</note>
124 Ecclesiastical Writers.
8 Pagans.

V. **N**ESTORIAN Century. p. 281. <note>The 5th Century, named after Nestorius, whose views on the dual nature of Christ (human and divine) led to the Council of Ephesus.</note>
201 Ecclesiastical Writers.
2 Pagans.

VI. **E**UTYCHIAN Century. p. 381. <note>The 6th Century, named for Eutyches, a monk whose teachings on Christ's single nature sparked the Monophysite controversy.</note>
120 Ecclesiastical Writers.
2 Pagans.

VII. **M**ONOTHELITE Century. P. 440. <note>The 7th Century, named for the Monothelite doctrine, which proposed that Christ had two natures but only one "will" or "energy."</note>
85 Writers.

VIII. **I**CONOCLASTIC Century. P. 471. <note>The 8th Century, defined by the struggle over the use of religious icons in worship, which led to widespread destruction of art and intense theological debate.</note>
47 Writers.

IX. **P**HOTIAN Century. P. 503. <note>The 9th Century, named for Photius the Great, Patriarch of Constantinople, whose conflict with the Pope was a major step toward the Great Schism between East and West.</note>
125 Writers.

X. **O**BSCURE Century. P. 571. <note>The 10th Century, historically termed the *Saeculum Obscurum* because of a perceived lack of prominent theological writers and the low moral state of the papacy at the time.</note>
45 Writers.

XI. **H**ILDEBRANDINE Century. P. 595. <note>The 11th Century, named after Hildebrand (Pope Gregory VII), known for his sweeping reforms and the struggle for power between the Church and the State.</note>
92 Writers.

XII. **W**ALDENSIAN Century. P. 632. <note>The 12th Century, named for the Waldensians, a pre-Protestant movement founded by Peter Waldo that emphasized poverty and preaching in the common tongue.</note>
169 Writers.

XIII. **S**CHOLASTIC Century. p. 695. <note>The 13th Century, the era of the Great Universities and the development of Scholasticism, a method of critical thought used by figures like Thomas Aquinas.</note>
122 Writers.

## APPENDIX.

XIV. **W**YCLIFFITE Century. p. 1. <note>The 14th Century, named for John Wycliffe, the "Morning Star of the Reformation," who translated the Bible into English and challenged papal authority.</note>
162 Writers.

XV. **S**YNODAL Century. P. 75. <note>The 15th Century, marked by the era of the "Great Councils" (synods) such as Constance and Basel, which attempted to reform the Church from within.</note>
189 Writers.

XVI. **R**EFORMED Century. p. 191. <note>The 16th Century, encompassing the Protestant Reformation led by Luther, Calvin, and others.</note>

<summary>This page provides a chronological table of contents for a history of Christian literature, dividing time into thematic "centuries" named after the most influential theologians or religious movements of each era. It lists the number of Christian (ecclesiastical) and secular (pagan) authors discussed in each section.</summary>
<keywords>Apostolic, Gnostic, Arian, Nestorian, Iconoclastic, Scholastic, Wycliffe, Reformation, ecclesiastical history, bibliography</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of the "Prolegomena" or Preface. The previous page concluded a list of typographical corrections for the volume. The author, William Cave, is now introducing the history and motivations behind his massive undertaking of cataloging Christian writers.</meta>

# PREFACE. <note>original: "PROLEGOMENA"</note>

It comes forth at last, soon enough if well enough, the *Literary History of Ecclesiastical Writers*. Regarding its origin, progress, material, method, means, and aids, and many things pertaining to the knowledge of Ecclesiastical Antiquity, it will perhaps interest you as much as me, candid Reader, that I deal with a few things here in advance.

---

## SECTION I.

### *On the Origin and Progress of this Work.*

Into what an immense mass things often grow from a small beginning, I have learned more than enough from this very work. It is now the fourteenth year since I committed to the press the *Tables of Ecclesiastical Writers*; which were afterwards reprinted at Hamburg in the year 1676, entirely without my knowledge. At that time, a thought of further promoting this matter <gloss>not even in a dream</gloss> <note>original Greek: "ἐπ’ ὄναρ ἔστ’"</note> entered my mind even once. But after a few years, many friends, men of no common sort, earnestly requested and more sharply urged me to comment more accurately still on the Ecclesiastical Writers, and to supply what seemed to be missing in others' books on this subject, and to build a work that would serve the common advantage of studies. To those persuading such things, I turned a deaf ear, and being overcome not so much by arguments as by repeated prayers, I finally surrendered; and having sought out from all sides what seemed likely to be an aid as well as an ornament to the future work, I laid the foundations of this *literary history*. 

But I immediately experienced that I had taken up a hard task, and that my shoulders were not equal to so heavy a burden; that it was a great business, full of labor and danger, and not a work of one year. For it is not permitted to us, who labor daily "under the sun and dust" <note>A classical metaphor for the heat and toil of active public life or hard work.</note>, to be so blessed as to devote our effort and mind to a single business, free from other cares. Daily cares and anxieties of life call us elsewhere, as do mostly tedious <note>literally "cold" (frigidæ), implying uninspiring or spirit-dampening chores</note> occupations, which at once distract and diminish the mind; elsewhere the services performed for friends; elsewhere the differing system of studies at times; and finally, elsewhere the duties of our sacred office; each of which might occupy a man entirely. 

Notwithstanding these things, I had progressed in arranging this History almost to the end of the Fourth Century several years ago, when first the wavering state of my affairs, then a stubborn and almost despaired-of health, and the cadaverous mass <note>The author refers to his own failing, sickly body as a "corpse-like mass."</note> by which I am weighed down, shook the begun work from my hands. In the meantime, so that I might not seem to have done nothing at all, in the year 1685 I published the *Ecclesiastical Archive-Keeper* <note>original: "Chartophylacem Ecclesiasticum"</note>, in which nearly 1500 writers and their writings were reviewed. Since that time, two French writers have treated the same subject in part; namely, Louis Ellies du Pin, a theologian of the Sorbonne faculty, who [has begun] a new bi- <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "bliothecam," referring to a "New Library of Ecclesiastical Authors."</note>

<summary>In this preface, the author describes the evolution of his work from a simple set of tables into a massive literary history, detailing the personal illness and professional distractions that delayed its completion.</summary>
<keywords>ecclesiastical writers, literary history, Hamburg, Chartophylax Ecclesiasticus, Louis Ellies du Pin, Sorbonne, scholarly labor, bibliography</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's narrative regarding the origins of his work. On the previous page, Cave discussed his initial reluctance to expand his "Tables" of ecclesiastical writers. Here, he evaluates the bibliographical works of his contemporaries, Louis Ellies Du Pin and Casimir Oudin, before justifying the need for his own massive "Literary History" as a necessary expansion of his earlier, briefer "Chartophylax."</meta>

[he] published a bibliography <note>original: "Bibliothecam"</note> in the French language at *Paris, 1686, in octavo.* A man (as much as can be discerned from those things which he published in Latin, for I do not understand French well enough) learned and sincere, and a most diligent scholar <note>original: "cultor," literally "cultivator"</note> of ecclesiastical antiquity. The other, *Casimir Oudin*, a Premonstratensian priest, who in the same place and time published a *Supplement to Bellarmine on Ecclesiastical Writers*. In which work, I wish he had applied the same diligence and equal judgment as the illustrious Du Pin: he would not have entangled himself in so many foul errors, nor transcribed word-for-word so great a part of his work from Miraeus, Labbe, Vossius, and others (often without making even the slightest mention of those from whom he took his material); he would have earned no small praise for sincerity and for diligence not uselessly spent.

But I return to the *Chartophylax* <note>The *Chartophylax Ecclesiasticus* (1685) was Cave’s shorter predecessor to this current work.</note>. Since friends everywhere complained that the little work was too brief and meager to satisfy the needs of students, they urged me both in person and by letter to bring back to the anvil <note>A classical metaphor for refining or reworking a piece of writing.</note> the work which I had abandoned to the cockroaches and moths; and thus they added spurs for me to finish weaving the web I had begun. I did, therefore, what I could, and at last brought the work to this form and size you see, not without immense labor (if I may say so without boasting). As long as the learned judge it fairly, that will be enough; as for what the critics or the "big-nosed" <note>Metaphor for those who are overly critical or arrogant.</note> think, Hippoclides doesn't care. <note>original Greek: "οὐ φροντὶς Ἱπποκλείδῃ" — a famous ancient proverb expressing indifference to the disapproval of others.</note>

Perhaps someone will say that I am doing what has already been done <note>original: "actum agere"</note>, and that to engage in this undertaking after Sixtus of Siena, Possevino, Bellarmine, Miraeus, Labbe, Gesner, Simler, Perkins, Scultetus, etc., is nothing other than to write the *Iliad* after Homer. I confess the truth of the matter: there are many writers in this genre, and far be it from me to wish that their late-night labors <note>original: "lucubrationes"</note> be shaken from the hands of students. The books of others have their uses; perhaps mine has its own as well. For those running in the same race, the manner of running is not the same for all, nor is the same path to be trodden, nor are the aids for successfully finishing the course the same; nor do those who walk in a pleasant and spacious garden always pluck the same little flowers. The field of Ecclesiastical Writers lies open very wide, in which many can exercise themselves without harm to others. What others who deal with Ecclesiastical Writers have achieved, and what I myself have provided in this work, let that be for others to judge; I would prefer that the reader become aware of it from a comparison of the work itself, rather than from my own proclamation. Without boasting, I hope it can be said that, besides those things I have in common with others, very many things are found here that would be sought elsewhere in vain. Be that as it may, I may even bring in that well-known saying of Saint Augustine:

<margin>Augustine, *On the Trinity*, book 1, chapter 3. Volume 3, page 211.</margin>
> *For not everything written by everyone reaches the hands of everyone. And it can happen that some, who are even capable of understanding these works of ours, may not find those clearer books, and might at least stumble upon these. Therefore, it is useful for many books to be produced by many authors in different styles—though not with a different faith—even about the same questions, so that the matter itself might reach as many as possible, to some in one way, and to others in another.*

### SECTION [CONTINUED]

<sig>d</sig>

<summary>William Cave defends the publication of his extensive "Literary History" against claims of redundancy, citing the precedent of varied scholarly approaches and quoting Saint Augustine to justify the existence of multiple works on the same subject. He critiques the plagiarism of Casimir Oudin while praising the diligence of Louis Ellies Du Pin.</summary>

<sig>b 2</sig>

## SECTION II.

### On the Subject Matter and Method of this Work.

It remains now for me to discuss a little more explicitly the writers themselves whom we present here, their writings, the editions of their works, and finally the method and order which we have employed. The writers themselves are not all of one kind. They can be conveniently divided into four classes: for they are either Major or Minor; and either Heretics or Pagans <note>original: "Gentiles." In this context, it refers to non-Christian classical or secular authors whose works intersect with Church history.</note>. I call those "Major" who not only surpass the others in age and authority—such as the Apostles and the Apostolic men, <term>those who held the first rank in the succession of the Apostles</term> <note>original Greek: "οἱ τὴν πρώτην τάξιν τῆς τῶν ἀποστόλων ἐπέχοντες διαδοχῆς." Cited from Eusebius, *Ecclesiastical History*, Book 3, Chapter 37.</note>—but also those who, with untiring zeal and immense labor, produced several volumes for the benefit of both the Church and the <term>Republic of Letters</term> <note>original: "Reipublicæ literariæ." A common early modern term for the international community of scholars and the collective body of human knowledge.</note>. In this category, I hope I have overlooked no one.

The writers of the "Minor" groups are those who published only a few small treatises or transmitted a single notable letter to posterity. These also I have sought out from everywhere and included in the census. To do this with greater profit, I have not only diligently examined the great volumes of the Church Fathers, but I have also leafed through the massive volumes of the "Libraries of the Fathers," the Councils, the Miscellanies, and as many volumes of such "Collected Works" as have come into my hands, dragging into the light many writers lying hidden therein. In this class of writers, the first place is held by—for I am not speaking now of those who composed full volumes—nearly all the Roman Pontiffs <note>The Popes.</note>. Since their Acts, Letters, Decrees, and <term>Diplomas</term> <note>Official charters or documents.</note> are especially necessary for the knowledge of ecclesiastical matters, I do not know for what reason they should not be replaced in the census of Ecclesiastical Writers, although this is not usually done by common custom.

That I have included Heretics was done because the knowledge of them, no less than of others, contributes significantly to illustrating the history of the Church. Since they are a significant part of ecclesiastical antiquity, and it matters greatly to know who they were and where they came from—what their sects and opinions were, what books they produced to defend their dogmas, and on what occasion they spread their heresies—it is necessary that an account of them be taken in a work of this kind. Yet, those who present "Ecclesiastical Writers" to us usually either pass over them entirely or care for them quite negligently. For the same reason, I have also taken account of those whose works do not survive today, because it is of great interest to the student of antiquity to be led to a knowledge of them.

Writers of political history I have included in my commentaries here and there, though sparingly. For who is unaware that in the later times of the Church, there were very few who handed down ecclesiastical matters separately? Rather, they were accustomed to commit civil and ecclesiastical business to writing in one and the same work. No one should wonder that many writers have been omitted by me. For who, even more sharp-sighted than Bernard, sees everything? <note>A reference to the medieval proverb *Bernardus non videt omnia* ("Bernard does not see everything"), suggesting that even the most learned authorities have blind spots.</note> Furthermore, there are many anonymous writers whose names do not come into the count, and yet we have noted these also among the ancients for the most part. Many are of an entirely uncertain time, whose age I could fish out with no hook of even likely conjecture; many are well enough known, but of no reputation, since...

<meta>catchword: quippe (since / in fact)</meta>

<summary>In this section of the preface, William Cave explains his criteria for selecting authors, categorizing them into Major and Minor writers, Popes, Heretics, and even those whose works are lost, arguing that a complete Church history requires understanding all these groups.</summary>

<keywords>bibliography, classification of writers, Apostolic succession, Roman Pontiffs, Heretics, lost works, secular historians, Republic of Letters, Eusebius, Bernard of Clairvaux</keywords>

--- Page 30 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's explanation of his editorial method. He is transitioning from describing the "Major" and "Minor" Christian authors to discussing his criteria for inclusion and his decision to include Pagan critics of the Church.</meta>

->PROLEGOMENA.<-

...being indeed trivial and common, and therefore only worthy of giving way to others. For since there is an infinite harvest of writers in the later centuries of the church—namely the <term>Scholastics</term> <note>Medieval theologians who used logic and philosophy to explain Christian doctrine.</note>, Chronologers, Biographers, etc.—a choice had to be made among them at times, so that by passing over obscure and worthless writers <note>original: "farfari foliis," literally "leaves of coltsfoot," a metaphor for cheap or useless paper.</note>, room might be made for those more famous and worthy of the memory of the ages. However, I fear it may be held against me as a fault that I have mixed in more of these "minor scribblers" <note>original: "scriptorulos." A diminutive, dismissive term for writers of little merit.</note> than was proper. In the earlier centuries, we applied the greatest diligence possible; to this end, we have drawn from almost all of Eusebius, Jerome, Gennadius, Photius, Suidas, and other ancients, as far as pertains to our purpose. Under each writer, I have first noted the most memorable events, especially those pertaining to the state of the Church and the <term>State</term> <note>original: "Reipublicæ." Here referring to the civil government or the Roman Empire.</note>, drawn from all the best authors. For I had often observed that this was what was most lacking in others who preceded us in this subject: they deal too sparingly with their writers, and do not usually provide a sufficiently full historical account of them, which is especially necessary for younger students. In this matter, however, I have kept to a middle course, so as to say neither nothing nor everything. Yet there are few of the more illustrious chapters of Church Antiquity whose essence cannot be sought from one place or another. The writer's life is followed by a <term>Praise</term> <note>original: "Elogium." A formal tribute or testimonial to the author's character and work.</note> and a critique of their style; these are mostly drawn from the ancients, and where the ancients fail, from the moderns—but only men of the first rank, such as Erasmus, Scaliger, Casaubon, etc.; and not infrequently, I have also interposed my own judgment, for whatever it may be worth.

At the end of each century, I have added the Gentile <note>original: "Gentiles." In this context, referring to Pagan or non-Christian classical authors.</note> writers who attacked our most holy faith and religion in their time. I admit their number is slim and small compared to the span of so many centuries. But the names of many have perished along with their writings. For the first Christian Emperors, out of a fervent (should I also say praiseworthy?) zeal, condemned the books of the Pagans against the Christian faith to the flames. Regarding <term>Porphyry</term> <note>A Neoplatonist philosopher (c. 234–305 AD) famous for his work "Against the Christians," which was targeted for destruction by the early Church.</note>, Constantine the Great says in his Letter to the Bishops and the People, found in Socrates Scholasticus, Book 1, Chapter 9:

> <note>Original Greek followed by Latin translation in source.</note> *Porphyry, the enemy of true piety, having composed lawless works against religion, has found a fitting reward: that he himself should be infamous to future ages and covered with the greatest disgrace, and that his impious writings should be destroyed.*

Behold also the Law of Theodosius the Great in the Code, "On the Supreme Trinity," Title 1, law 3:

> *We judge it fitting for our empire to remind our subjects of Religion... We therefore decree that all things whatsoever that Porphyry, driven by his own madness, or anyone else has written against the religious worship of Christians, shall be committed to the fire wherever they are found. For we do not wish any writings that provoke God to anger and offend pious minds to even reach the ears of men.*

Hence it happens that even the names of many who wrote against the Christians are not known to us, let alone that their complete books should have survived to our time.

# PREFACE.

->PROLEGOMENA.<-

<meta>catchword: reat.</meta>

Regarding the writings themselves, I have assigned each to its proper category; I have added an index of all works, whether published together in collections or separately; those hiding under false names I have restored to their true authors; I have searched for traces of lost works and pointed out, as if with a finger, which fragments survive today and where they may be found. I have warned the reader which works are of doubtful reliability, carefully distinguishing the genuine from the **forged**, and where necessary, I have added my reasons—briefly indeed, and in "scarcely three words," unless the matter itself demanded a fuller critique. For it did not seem right to me to bring out long lines of arguments to prove the falsity of those minor works which are already rejected as spurious by the much larger and more learned part of the <term>Roman Catholics</term> <note>original: "Pontificiorum." Referring to scholars of the Roman Catholic Church.</note> themselves. Nor, truly, was it necessary when I was writing this: for there had not yet arisen among us those new <term>defenders</term> <note>original: "hyperaspistis," from the Greek for "shield-bearers" or "protectors."</note> of the Roman cause who serve us "rehashed cabbage" <note>original: "cramben centiès coctam," a Latin idiom referring to a boring, repetitive argument that has been used many times before.</note>—arguments a hundred times refuted—and who obtrude upon us, with such shameless audacity, the minor works of the ancients which have been held as forgeries by almost everyone until now, acting as if no one had ever challenged them. I have not always noted the *Constitutions* and *Synodal Acts*. I had certainly intended to attach the Councils and their Acts and letters to each century, but I turned away from this plan so that the book would not grow into a larger mass than was proper. May someone eventually arise who will deliver the Councils to us using this or a similar method. Yet, although my first and primary care was for published writings, I have nevertheless investigated <term>unpublished</term> <note>original: "ἀνέκδοτα" (anekdota). Literally "not given out," referring to works still in manuscript form.</note> works and those which have remained hidden until now with all the diligence I could muster; in this, however, I fear I may not have satisfied the curious Reader. It would surely benefit the study of Antiquity if the locked doors of Libraries were broken open, the innermost secrets of the Archives unbolted, and the full treasury of so many hidden monuments of the Church laid open to us. But indeed, what **Atlas** would be sufficient for this burden? What **Hercules** could be found equal to enduring this labor? Meanwhile, I celebrate the good fortune of this age, which sees so many monuments of the Ancients rescued from darkness and snatched from the empire of cockroaches and moths; and that our time has produced so many scholars such as Sirmond, Valois, Holstenius, Allatius, Labbe, Cotelier, Garnier, Baluze, Mabillon, D'Achery, Huet, Lambeck, and Wetstein <note>This list includes the greatest European scholars, librarians, and historians of the 17th century who specialized in early Christian texts.</note> (to say nothing of our own countrymen flourishing today).

I have listed the editions of the ecclesiastical writers—if not all of them, then certainly almost all, and especially the most excellent ones. I have diligently reviewed those short works published separately and those which are missing from the collected works. I have usually joined older editions to the more recent ones, as the older ones are deservedly valued for reasons to be mentioned below. In tracing the editions of the many books that appeared in the early days of <term>printing</term> <note>original: "nascentis typographiæ," referring to incunabula, or books printed before 1501.</note>, I have followed the best guides I could find. Nor have I neglected catalogs of better repute, both from public libraries—such as those of **Oxford**, **Leiden**, etc.—and private ones, such as the **De Thou**, **Heinsius**, and **Arundel** collections, etc. <note>These were famous 17th-century library catalogs used by scholars to locate rare manuscripts and early editions.</note>, partly to compare various editions with one another, and partly so that those works not available everywhere might be more easily tracked down. But we shall deal more extensively with the editions of the **Holy Fathers** in the proper place.

Let us come finally to the order and method we have used. First, I have added a specific title to each century, so that it may be more easily distinguished from the rest and remain more firmly fixed in the reader's memory.

...would flourish. Next, I have provided a Summary [Breviary] of that century, in which the sources of ecclesiastical and literary history are opened up; however, I was quite brief in this matter, since the most memorable events are reviewed under the individual writers. In arranging the writers, I have observed a chronological order, as it is more in harmony with nature and leads us by the hand through the reading of the Church Fathers in the most convenient way. I have noted the year in which each flourished, especially the final year in which he died, which is surely the most certain indicator of his era; where a certain date cannot be found, I have used conjectures, though not entirely devoid of reason. Given the time of death of this or that writer, I have placed the year in which he was famous ten years prior, unless some specific reason suggested otherwise. In establishing the eras of Emperors, Bishops, and Abbots, we have derived the <term>Epoch</term> <note>The starting point of their reign or tenure.</note> from the year in which they were raised to their duties and dignities, wherever that could be obtained. Regarding the succession of the Roman Pontiffs and the notation of time from the fourth century, I have followed the catalog which <term>Papebroch</term> <note>Daniel Papebroch (1628–1714), a Jesuit hagiographer and member of the Bollandists known for his work on the lives of saints.</note> published not long ago, just as, in reviewing their deeds, we have mostly followed the authors from whom <term>Baronius</term> <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607), a Cardinal and historian whose "Annales Ecclesiastici" were a foundational work of Catholic history.</note> took his own information. The century is closed by the <term>Gentiles</term> <note>Referring to non-Christian or "pagan" writers.</note> who attacked the most holy religion of Christ in their writings; but these end in the sixth century, after which the entire race of pagan philosophers and learned men was almost everywhere extinguished.

### SECTION III.

->*On the means and aids employed in composing this work.*<-

Long ago, Gaius Pliny rightly said in the Preface to his *Natural History* addressed to Vespasian: *“It is, I think, a mark of a gracious spirit and full of noble modesty to acknowledge those through whom you have made progress—unlike most of those I have touched upon. For know that while comparing authors, I have discovered that the ancients were transcribed word for word by the most sworn and recent authors, and yet were not named.”*

---

It is truly the mark of a subservient soul and an unhappy talent to prefer being caught in a theft than to return what was borrowed, especially since the principal increases through the interest. Therefore, after declaring what has been performed by me in this work, it is only fair that I next explain the resources with which I was equipped and the help of friends by which I was supported to move it forward. And first, I will say quite confidently that I have given each man his due: I have harmed no one; I have suppressed no one's fame, name, or writings; I have not plundered anyone's private papers; I have always sought the sources themselves where possible; but whenever more recent authors had to be called to my aid, I have candidly and faithfully acknowledged both the name and the location of the Author. The books I used are available everywhere and open to anyone; accordingly, I have prefixed a list of them to these <term>Prolegomena</term> <note>Introductory remarks or a formal preface to a scholarly work.</note>. Nor were friends lacking on their part to lend helping hands to me as I labored in this arena: some I have noted in the course of the work itself, and a few I have reserved for this place. Among those from abroad, the first place is held by <term>L. Otto Mencken</term> <note>Leonhard Otto Mencke (1644–1707), the founder of the first major scholarly journal in Germany.</note>, a public professor at the University of Leipzig and the most worthy President of the college that produces the *Acts of the Learned* <note>original: "Acta eruditorum."</note>; to him I am bound by more than one account, and he is to be celebrated by me in this place because he provided some small Greek works from the <term>Pauline Library of Leipzig</term> <note>The library of the University of Leipzig, located in the former Pauline monastery.</note> from manuscript codices...

...he took care to have transcribed for my use, one or two of which I have inserted into this work. **Henry Dodwell** of Dublin, the most celebrated professor of history at Oxford University, was a kind supporter of my work; when I more than once pulled my shoulders back from this burden <note>A metaphor for nearly giving up on the massive task of writing this book.</note>, he added strength and courage, and steadied me when I wavered. If I truly needed anything, he willingly brought it forth from both his heart and his library. Some notes were provided by **Abednego Seller** of Devon, a man worthy of a better position and perhaps a better fate. But when I saw the work growing to an almost insane size, I used what I received from friends more sparingly. 

There is one thing yet that must be discussed a little more specifically. **James Ussher**, Archbishop of Armagh, from the very cradle of his theological studies, contemplated a certain *Theological Library* (for he had formed the title of the work himself), which has been long and much desired by the public prayers of the learned. <margin>Friedrich Spanheim, *Dedicatory Epistle to the Evangelical Doubts*, part 3. Geneva, 1639.</margin> *“Your **Theological Library**,”* says the illustrious Spanheim, *“and your critique of ancient ecclesiastical writers are expected with the greatest desire by all students of genuine antiquity because of your most refined judgment.”* To this end, Ussher studiously read the Church Fathers through every century and collected a great deal of material for the future work from everywhere. But alas! The work was planned rather than truly begun, and was nothing but an <term>unrefined and indigestible mass</term> <note>Latin: *rudis indigestaque moles* — a famous phrase from the poet Ovid describing the chaos before the creation of the world.</note>. Occupied by more serious business, and tossed about by the storm of the civil war that raged among us for several years, he progressed hardly beyond some confused collections. 

**Edward Stillingfleet**, Dean of St. Paul's in London—an ornament of both our nation and our century—shared the manuscript of that most learned prelate with me out of his great kindness. But since it contained nothing but some torn papers, miserably worn out, poorly written, and arranged in no order, the excellent hope I had conceived of drawing from it for my use was largely frustrated; I excerpted a very few things which, preserving for the most part Ussher's own words, are inserted in their places. Lest what Ussher had collected with such great diligence should perish entirely, he commended them while dying to the care of **Gerard Langbaine**, Provost of Queen’s College, Oxford. Nor did Langbaine fail the wishes of his most upright friend; he took care to have Ussher’s collections transcribed, put them into what order he could, filled the gaps, and restored the citations that had been worn away in the margins. However, while he was pressing on with this work with more ardent zeal than was healthy in the public library during an unusually harsh winter, he died at his task in the year 1657. Langbaine’s transcript is kept in the archives of the **Bodleian Library**, where I remember seeing it once.

However (to return to the friends from whom I have digressed), **Henry Wharton**, Master of Arts, must not be deprived of his praise. He is a man of excellent character and, in almost every kind of higher literature, learned far beyond his years. To relieve the weakness of my eyes, he worked faithfully with me for seven or eight months in searching for the locations of authors, comparing editions, making translations, and collecting the deeds of many Roman Popes. And later, while occupied with other things, as he searched the shelves of various libraries, he pointed out more than one writer and more than one fragment to me, as I have noted in their places. Finally, while I myself, too intent on the work, was hastening to the end of the thirteenth century, behold a most serious...

Next, I have provided a Summary of that century, through which the sources of Church and literary history are opened; however, I have been quite brief in this matter, since the most memorable events are reviewed under each individual writer. In arranging the writers, I have observed the sequence of time, as it is more in harmony with nature and leads us by the hand in a very convenient order when reading the <term>Church Fathers</term> <note>The influential early Christian theologians and bishops whose writings established the foundations of Christian doctrine.</note>. The pagans, who attacked the most holy Religion of Christ in their writings, close out the century; but these end in the sixth century, as the entire race of pagan philosophers and scholars was almost everywhere wiped out after that time.

## SECTION III.

->On the means and assistance used in creating this work.<-

Pliny the Elder once rightly said in the Preface to his *Natural History* dedicated to the Emperor Vespasian: 
> *I believe it is both kind and full of noble modesty to acknowledge those through whom you have profited; not as most of those I have touched upon have done. For you should know that in comparing authors, I have discovered that the ancients were transcribed word-for-word by those who were most sworn to them and closest to them, yet without being named.*

---

> *It is surely the mark of a submissive mind and a miserable talent to prefer being caught in a theft than to repay a loan, especially since the principal grows with interest.*

After I have declared what I have provided in this work, it is only fair that I next explain the supports I was equipped with and the help of friends by which I advanced it. First, I will say quite confidently that I have given each person their due: I have harmed no one; I have suppressed no one's fame, name, or writings; I have not plundered anyone’s desk. I have always sought the sources themselves whenever possible; but whenever modern scholars had to be called upon for help, I have candidly and faithfully acknowledged both the author’s name and the specific passage. The books I used are available everywhere and open to anyone; accordingly, I have placed a list of them at the beginning of these <term>Introductory Remarks</term> <note>original: "Prolegomenis."</note>. Nor were friends lacking in doing their part to lend a helping hand to me as I labored in this arena: some I have noted in the course of the work itself, and a few I have reserved for this place. Among those from abroad, the first place is held by **L. Otto Mencken**, a public professor at the University of Leipzig and the most worthy president of the college that produces the *Acts of the Learned* <note>original: "Acta eruditorum," the first scientific journal of the German-speaking lands, published in Latin.</note>; to whom I am indebted on more than one account, and who must be celebrated by me here because [he provided] certain small Greek works from manuscript codices in the <term>Paulina Library of Leipzig</term> <note>The library of the University of Leipzig, which held significant manuscript collections.</note>...

**James Ussher, Archbishop of Armagh**, meditated upon a certain *Theological Library* <note>original: *Bibliothecam quandam Theologicam*. In this context, a "Library" refers to a systematic bibliography or catalog of authors rather than a physical building.</note> (for so he had titled the work himself) from the very cradle of his theological studies; it was long and greatly desired by the public prayers of the learned. *All those devoted to genuine antiquity,* says the famous **Spanheim** <note>Friedrich Spanheim (1600–1649), a Calvinist theologian and professor at Geneva and Leiden.</note> (in his dedicatory epistle to the third part of the *Evangelical Doubts*, Geneva, 1639), *await with the greatest desire your "Theological Library" and your critique of ancient ecclesiastical writers, based on your most refined judgment.* For this purpose, he studiously read the Church Fathers through the passing centuries, and from all sides he searched out many materials for his future work.

Langbaine’s transcript is kept in the archives of the Bodleian Library, where I remember seeing it once.

## SECTION IV.

### *Critical Rules Necessary for Distinguishing the Genuine Works of the Holy Fathers.*

<margin>* Augustine, *Against Faustus the Manichaean*, Book 22, Chapter 79.</margin>
<margin>† Jerome, *On Illustrious Men*, Chapter 7.</margin>
Since in the primary controversies of faith it is customary to appeal to the "Tribunal of the Fathers" from all sides, and since the Roman Catholics <note>Original: *Pontificii*</note> mostly plead their cause against us from the fictional cabinets of forged antiquity, it cannot be hidden from anyone—except someone completely blind in Church matters—how much it concerns students of Church Antiquity to be able to separate the genuine works of the Holy Fathers from the spurious ones. In every century, there have been rascally forgers who not only laid violent hands on the writings of the Fathers and foully corrupted them—partly by adding and partly by lopping text off—but also impudently substituted entire books of their own invention under the Fathers' names. Hence so many Gospels, Constitutions, Traditions, Revelations, and Itineraries were once sold under the names of the Apostles themselves—written by "I know not what cobblers of fables" <note>A dismissive term for amateur or uneducated writers inventing stories</note> under the Apostolic name, as * Augustine says regarding the Apocrypha of the Manichæans. Hence the *Journeys of Paul and Thecla*, stuffed with foul fables, were forged by a certain Asian Priest out of <gloss>goodwill</gloss> <note>Original Greek: *εὐνοια* (eunoia)</note> for Paul; he confessed the fraud to the Apostle Saint John and was for that reason removed from the rank of the priesthood, as † Jerome records from Tertullian. And if they did not even spare the Apostles themselves, what wonder if this unbridled license for forgery raged against their successors? Who does not know the spurious works circulated under the names of Clement of Rome, Linus, Prochorus, Martial, Melito, Justin Martyr, Tertullian, Cyprian, Dorotheus, Athanasius, Jerome, Augustine, Chrysostom, and others? These make up nearly every other page in the following work. The monks of later centuries imitated the frauds and impostures of the ancients in this regard: to them we owe so many Charters, Letters, Sermons, Lives and Passions of Saints, and six hundred other things of that kind, marked with the illustrious names of the ancients. It is enough just to have mentioned this. I confess I have always tolerated this class of men with a very resentful mind—men born indeed for the destruction of the literary world, and therefore worthy of the whip and the mill; or, to speak in the words of the playwright Plautus, *“to be banished to the islands of the whip-beatings and the iron-clankings, where dead oxen rush upon living men.”* <note>A joke from Plautus's play *Asinaria*: "dead oxen" refers to whips made of cowhide.</note> To establish a more successful critique of these matters, so that the true may be separated from the false, the most famous theologians of the Reformed tradition have handed down various rules and aphorisms to us: Hyperius in *The Method of Theological Study, Book 4, Chapter 9, Observation 3, page 688*; Scultetus in *Aphorisms on the Fruitful Reading of the Fathers*...

<meta>catchword: Marrow [Medullæ]</meta>

It highlights historical examples of forgeries, such as the Acts of Paul and Thecla, and condemns the "rascally forgers" of later centuries.</summary>

<meta>The author is continuing his introductory discourse (Prolegomena), transitioning from the administrative history of his work to the methodological rules for identifying forged ecclesiastical texts. He cites several prominent scholars—Rivet, Holdsworth, and Spanheim—to establish his academic lineage.</meta>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

Having premised the core components <note>original: "Medullæ"</note>; as seen in Andreas Rivet's *Treatise on the Authority of the Fathers*, chapter 14, page 88; Richard Holdsworth (formerly Professor of Sacred Theology at Gresham College) in Lecture 37, page 328; and Friedrich Spanheim, the currently renowned Professor of Theology at the University of Leiden, in his *Introduction to the History of the New Testament*, page 120. Following in their footsteps, I shall outline a few standards <note>original: "Canons"</note> and bring them forward into the light, illustrated with notes and examples.

I. **In any work where mention occurs of things or persons more recent than the age of the writer whose name the book bears, it is a most certain sign of falsehood.** As far as persons are concerned, I will demonstrate this with a few examples. In the *Apostolic History* (Book 5) of Abdias of Babylon—supposedly one of the seventy disciples—Hegesippus is praised <note>Hegesippus lived in the 2nd century, long after an "Apostolic" disciple would have died</note>. In the *Questions and Responses to the Orthodox* under the name of Justin Martyr (Question 115), Irenaeus is mentioned; in Question 82 and 86, Origen; and in Question 127, the Manichaeans; all these are recorded despite being far more recent than Justin. The book *On the Trinity*, long attributed to Tertullian, mentions the Sabellians, who only arose after Tertullian's time. In the commentaries on Luke by Titus of Bostra, Gregory Nazianzen, Nyssen, Chrysostom, Isidore of Pelusium, and Cyril of Alexandria are cited more than once. In Athanasius's *Questions to Antiochus*, the names of Nyssen, Chrysostom, and other more recent figures appear. The book *On the Spirit and the Soul* is held among the works of Augustine; however, as Erasmus <note>Desiderius Erasmus (1466–1536), the famous Renaissance humanist and editor of the Church Fathers</note> noted, although it is the work of a man of varied and extensive reading who delved deeply into Aristotelian philosophy, it is clearly not by Augustine. This is evident from the fact that, among other things, Boethius is cited in Chapter 37—a man who lived almost an entire century after Augustine's death. By the term "things," I primarily mean rites, ceremonies, controversies, and heresies. In the *Apostolic Constitutions*, in the works of Dionysius the Areopagite, and in the *Pseudo-Decretal Epistles*, there occur everywhere hundreds of passages concerning temples, altars, consecrations, vestments, public feasts, fasts, masses, the timing and manner of administering baptism, exorcism, penance, and monasticism—infinite things not yet born in the Apostolic age. In the *Apostolic Constitutions* (Book 6, Chapter 15), the baptism of heretics is discussed and the controversy is accurately defined, even though the dispute on this matter did not arise until the third century. There exist three books of *Commentaries on Job* under the name of Origen, in which, nevertheless, mention is made of the Manichaeans, and the Arian heresy is expressly defended. Similarly, the *Commentaries on the Psalms* that bear the name of Arnobius the African mention Photinus and his heresy, which was devised many years after Arnobius. To this also belongs the fact that in certain small works ascribed to Cyprian, not a few passages of Holy Scripture are cited from Jerome’s version <note>The "Vulgate" translation</note>. This is something that could perhaps be noted in several others as well.

II. **New words, or words with new meanings, and those which clearly belong to a later age, plainly show the spuriousness <note>original: "νόθειον" (notheion)</note> of the book in which they are found.** By this indicator, the so-called *Decretal Epistles* betray themselves everywhere. In every passage, titles such as Primates, Patriarchs, Metropolitans, Archbishops, Apocrisiarii <note>Envoys or legates of the high clergy</note>, Patricians, Counts, "formatted letters" <note>original: "literarum formatarum" - formal certificates or letters of recommendation for clergy</note>, and so on, present themselves to us—names of which the first centuries of the Christian Church did not even dream.

The author provides numerous examples of famous texts falsely attributed to early Church Fathers like Justin Martyr, Augustine, and Origen.</summary>

He is moving from Rule I (anachronistic persons) to Rule III (unreliable fables).</meta>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

By this same rule, the mask is stripped from the various Liturgies and Apostolic Constitutions. In these works, one frequently encounters Popes, Patriarchs, Archbishops, and Monks, along with their Sub-deacons, Readers, and Cantors. They mention Incense, Diptychs <note>Tablets used in churches to record names for prayer</note>, the *Trisagion* <note>A "thrice-holy" hymn; original: "Trifagium"</note>, the Alleluia, Altars, and so forth. All of these were as entirely unknown to the Apostolic Age as the New World was to the ancients. That the *Life of Saint John*, attributed to his disciple Prochorus, is of a much later date is sufficiently proven by the use of terms like <term>consubstantial</term> <note>original: "homoousios"</note>, <term>Trinity</term> <note>original: "Trias"</note>, and <term>person</term> <note>original: "hypostasis"</note>. This is no less true than the *Gospel of Nicodemus*, which uses the vocabulary of the "Sacred Trinity" and "original sin"—terms that were only introduced into ecclesiastical usage after several centuries had passed.

<margin>* Heresy 26, Number 12.</margin>
III. Empty fables and the most foolish trifles—which are completely inconsistent with the character and gravity of the Apostolic age or any ancient writer—shout that the book containing them is a forgery, no matter whose name it bears. At the very least, they provide a just reason for doubt. For this reason, the following are rightly classified as spurious: the *Travels of Paul and Thecla* (mentioned by Tertullian in his book *On Baptism* and by Jerome in *On Illustrious Men*, chapter 7); and the *Acts of Saint Thecla the Virgin* that survive today, which are utterly defiled with fables. On these, see the lengthy discussion by Baronius in his *Notes on the Roman Martyrology for September 23*. Scarcely better are the two books *On the Passion of Peter and Paul* held under the name of *Linus*, which recount so many uncertain, false, and frivolous things.

To this same pile of rubbish <note>original: "farinæ cumulum" — literally a "heap of the same flour," meaning "of the same sort"</note> we must banish the *Proto-Evangelium* <note>The "First Gospel," an apocryphal book about the infancy of Mary and Jesus</note> attributed to James. It overflows with more-than-Jewish nonsense and inventions regarding Joachim and Anna (the parents of the Blessed Virgin), and about Mary's birth, education, marriage, and childbirth. It was concocted by some Judaizing scoundrel or someone from the Gnostic sect and shamelessly imposed upon the credulity of men under the venerable and sacred name of James the Apostle. Thus, it should be numbered among those apocryphal gospels which Lucianus is said to have forged in the Pseudo-Gelasian Decree <note>A famous early medieval list of authentic and rejected books</note>. In that decree, perhaps the name *Lucius* or *Leucius* (whom Gelasius discusses shortly after) should be restored in place of *Lucianus*.

From this, the compiler of the *Commentary on the Six Days of Creation* <note>original: "Hexaemeron"</note> under the name of Eustathius of Antioch clearly reveals his own deception. He carelessly poured almost this entire history—which was perhaps unknown in Eustathius’s time and certainly unworthy of his judgment—into his commentary, as I have noted in the appropriate place. Of exactly the same low quality is the short book on the *Birth of Saint Mary* found among the works of Jerome and likewise condemned by Gelasius. The imposter pretends that Jerome translated it from Hebrew and claims—if you can believe it!—that the book was first written in Hebrew by Saint Matthew, then kept secret for a long time, and finally brought to light by a certain Seleucus, a disciple of Mani. I believe it is generally agreed that this Seleucus is the same as Leucius or Lucius (whom the Pseudo-Gelasian Decree calls a "disciple of the devil"). No one can doubt that both little works—the *Pseudo-Gospel* and the *Book on the Birth of Mary*—came from the same Gnostic workshop. Indeed, as Epiphanius testifies*, among <term>ten thousand fabrications of the same kind</term> <note>original: "myria tēs autēs peplasmena genna"</note>, the Gnostics possessed a book entitled *On the Lineage of Mary* that was full of monstrous fables. The Manichaeans took these fables from the Gnostics and refined them more fully, especially Leucius, or Seleucus; hence...

<summary>The author argues that many early church texts are forgeries because they contain theological terms and ritual practices that were unknown in the Apostolic age. He identifies several apocryphal works about Mary and the Apostles as Gnostic or Manichaean fabrications designed to trick the faithful.</summary>
