# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

<margin>* Letter 3 to Exuperius, chapter 7.</margin>
Innocent I <note>Pope Innocent I, reigned 401–417 AD</note> determined that those works written under the names of Matthew (some read Matthias), James the Less, Peter, and John—which were composed by a certain Leucius—were not only to be rejected but also condemned. The book by Melito of Sardis on the *Passing of the Blessed Mary*, and the *History of the Passion of the Image of Christ in Beirut* (which is found among the works of Saint Athanasius), are obvious to everyone; they are more worthy of laughter than of faith, to say nothing of infinite other examples.

<margin>* To Vincent, Letter 48, Column 184.</margin>
IV. The style and the context of the entire discourse, when far removed from that character of speaking which the author used in his genuine works, provide a manifest argument of forgery. In this criterion (to say nothing of <term>external evidence</term> <note>original: "tōn exōthen"; referring to evidence from outside the text itself, such as historical context</note>), the ecclesiastical critics agree: Eusebius, Jerome, Gennadius, Photius, and all the more recent ones. When a controversy once arose concerning Cyprian's opinion on the baptism of heretics, and some shouted that his letters on this matter had been forged, Augustine judged them to be Cyprian's based on the texture of the speech, because, he said, "his style has a certain proper face by which it can be recognized." It is rarely otherwise: for even if a skilled master of speaking can sometimes mimic the style of another quite cleverly, one is rarely found who can sustain this, and hardly one in a thousand. Nor, after all effort, even if diligently applied, will he prevent a great lack of similarity from appearing to an attentive reader, whether in the polish of the words, the form of the elocution, or the artifice of the arrangement. Erasmus of Rotterdam—the most sagacious and beyond controversy the greatest judge of ecclesiastical Latin—long ago demonstrated this with six hundred examples in the works of Cyprian, Hilary, Ambrose, Jerome, Augustine, etc., so that it is not necessary to note individual writers in this matter. I confess it is not everyone's task to judge style aptly: but by a certain fate it happens to imposters that they not only fall below the proper and native kind of speaking of the true author, but they also mostly betray their childishness, stammering, and barbarism; so that you may easily recognize the lion by its claw <note>A proverb meaning one can judge the whole from a small part</note>, or rather, the donkey by its ears.

<margin>* Volume 2, folio 28, section C.</margin>
<margin>† Volume 4, folio 150, section G.</margin>
V. A book is rightly condemned as <term>spurious</term> <note>original: "nothos"; a Greek term for "illegitimate," often used for forged texts</note> in which doctrines are found that are clearly contrary to those which the true author handed down in his genuine and undoubted works. For it is abhorrent to every appearance of truth that one and the same writer—prudent, grave, and learned—would contradict himself and interweave propositions into his books that are sometimes diametrically opposed. For when someone later revokes and reshapes opinions they once promoted—as Augustine did in his books of *Retractions* written near the end of his life—it is a mark of the highest prudence and candor. I would not want this to be taken in the sense that one and the same writer never contradicted himself anywhere, especially in matters of lesser importance (for examples of that kind can be found, especially in <term>prolific writers</term> <note>original: "polygraphois"</note>, whether it happened by a failure of memory or the heat of debate), but rather that it rarely happens, and not in those <term>unfailing matters</term> <note>original: "aptaistois"; fundamental truths or certainties</note> which are unlikely to be misstated by a learned and wise man. Who would believe that Origen taught in his *Commentaries on Job* that Christ preached for three and a half years from his baptism until his passion, when in his work *On First Principles* <note>original: "Peri Archōn"</note>, book 4, chapter 1, † he affirmed that He preached for only a single year and a few months—

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

By this same rule, the mask is also pulled away from the <term>Liturgies</term><note>Traditional orders of public worship attributed to early figures like James or Mark</note> and the <term>Apostolic Constitutions</term><note>A collection of early Christian instructions on church law and prayer, falsely attributed to the Apostles</note>. In these works, we frequently encounter Pontiffs, Patriarchs, Archbishops, and Monks, along with their Sub-deacons, Readers, and Cantors; we see mention of Incense, <term>Diptychs</term><note>Lists of names read aloud during the liturgy</note>, the <term>Trisagion</term><note>The "Thrice-Holy" hymn: Holy, Holy, Holy</note>, the Hallelujah, Altars, and so on. All of these were as entirely unknown to the Apostolic age as the "New World" <note>The Americas</note> was to the ancients. Furthermore, the *Life of Saint John*—attributed to his disciple Prochorus—is proven to be of a much later date by the use of terms such as <term>consubstantial</term><note>original Greek: "homoousios"; the technical term used in the 4th-century Nicene Creed to describe Christ’s nature</note>, <term>Trinity</term><note>original Greek: "Triados"</note>, and <term>personhood</term><note>original Greek: "hypostasis"; a philosophical term for the distinct persons of the Godhead</note>. The same is true of the *Gospel of Nicodemus*, which uses the vocabulary of the Holy Trinity and "original sin"—terms that only entered common ecclesiastical usage after several centuries had passed.

III. Vain fables and the most foolish trifles, which are utterly inconsistent with the character and gravity of the Apostolic age or any ancient writer of substance, cry out that the book containing them is a forgery, regardless of whose name it bears. At the very least, they provide a just reason to doubt the work. By this standard, those "Circuits" of Paul and Thecla are rightly dismissed as spurious, as mentioned by Tertullian in his book *On Baptism* and Jerome in his *On Illustrious Men* (chapter 7). The same applies to the *Acts of Saint Thecla the Virgin* which survive today; they are thoroughly defiled by fables, about which one may see Baronius arguing at length in his *Notes on the Martyrology for September 23rd*. Of no better quality are the two books *On the Passion of Peter and Paul* circulating under the name of Linus <note>The traditional second Pope</note>, in which so many uncertain, false, and frivolous things are narrated. 

To this same heap of "flour from the same mill" we must relegate the *Infancy Gospel of James* <note>original: "Proto-Evangelium"</note>. It is overflowing with trifles and stories more Jewish than Christian concerning Joachim and Anna (the parents of the Blessed Virgin), and about Mary's birth, education, marriage, and childbirth. It was fabricated by some Judaizing rogue or someone from the Gnostic herd, and shamelessly forced upon the credulity of men under the venerable and sacred name of James the Apostle. Thus, it should be numbered among those apocryphal gospels which Lucianus is said to have corrupted in the <term>Pseudo-Gelasian Decree</term><note>A famous early medieval list of authorized and prohibited books, once attributed to Pope Gelasius</note>. In that text, perhaps, the name *Lucianus* should be replaced with *Lucius* or *Leucius* (whom Gelasius discusses shortly after). 

<margin>* Epiphanius, *Against Heresies*, Book 26, Number 12.</margin>

Furthermore, the compiler of the *Commentaries on the Six Days of Creation* <note>original: "Hexaemeron"</note> under the name of Eustathius of Antioch clearly reveals his own imposture; he carelessly poured this entire history into his commentary—a history likely unknown in Eustathius's time and certainly unworthy of his judgment—as I have noted in the appropriate place. Of the very same bran is the little book *On the Nativity of Saint Mary* found among the works of Jerome and likewise condemned by the Decree of Gelasius. In this case, the imposter pretends that Jerome translated it from the Hebrew, and (if the gods permit such nonsense!) that the book was first written in Hebrew letters by Saint Matthew, then hidden away in secret for a long time, and finally published by a certain Seleucus, a disciple of the heretic Manichaeus. I believe it is generally confessed by everyone that this Seleucus is the same as the Leucius (or Lucius) whom the Pseudo-Gelasian decree calls a "disciple of the devil." There can be no doubt that both short works—the *Infancy Gospel* and the *Book on the Nativity*—emerged from the same Gnostic workshop. Indeed, Epiphanius testifies* that among the <term>myriad of things fashioned by their own labor</term><note>original Greek: "myria tēs autois pep lasmena geōrgia"</note>, the Gnostics possessed a book entitled *The Lineage of Mary*, which was full of monstrous fables. These fables, inherited from the Gnostics, were more fully refined by the Manichaeans, and especially by Leucius, or Seleucus; from which...

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

<margin>* Letter 3 to Exuperius, chapter 7.</margin>
Innocent I * determined that those works written by a certain Leucius under the names of Matthew (others read *Matthias*), James the Less, Peter, and John, are not only to be rejected but also to be condemned. The book by Melito of Sardis titled *On the Departure of the Blessed Mary*, and the *History of the Passion of the Image of Christ* in Beirut (which is found among the works of Saint Athanasius), are known to everyone to be more worthy of laughter than of faith—to say nothing of countless other examples.

IV. The style and the context of the entire discourse, if it is far different from the character of speech used by the author in his genuine works, provides manifest evidence of forgery. On this criterion (to say nothing of <term>secular authors</term> <note>original: "τοῖς ἔξωθεν"; referring to non-Christian or pagan writers</note>), the ecclesiastical critics agree: Eusebius, Jerome, Gennadius, Photius, and all the more recent scholars. When a controversy once arose regarding Cyprian’s opinion on the baptism of heretics, and some claimed that his letters on this matter had been fabricated, Augustine <margin>* To Vincent, Letter 48. Column 184.</margin> judged them to be Cyprian’s own by the texture of the speech, saying, "because his style has a certain unique face by which it can be recognized." And it rarely happens otherwise; for even if a skilled master of rhetoric can sometimes quite cleverly imitate another’s style, it is rarely found that anyone can truly sustain this, and hardly one in a thousand succeeds. Nor, after all their efforts—even if most diligently applied—will they prevent a great dissimilarity from appearing to an attentive reader, whether in the elegance of the words, the form of the expression, or the artfulness of the arrangement. Erasmus of Rotterdam, that most sagacious and beyond controversy the greatest judge of ecclesiastical Latinity, has long since demonstrated this with hundreds of examples in the works of Cyprian, Hilary, Ambrose, Jerome, Augustine, and others; thus, it is not necessary to note every individual writer in this regard. I confess it is not within everyone’s power to judge style aptly; but by a certain fate it happens to impostors that they not only fall below the proper and native speaking style of the true author, but they also mostly betray their own simplicity, stammering, and barbarism. Thus, you may easily "recognize the lion by its claw," or rather, the ass by its ears.

V. A book is rightly condemned as <term>spurious</term> <note>original: "νόθος"; literally "bastard," used for forged books</note> if it contains doctrines plainly contrary to those the true author handed down in his genuine and undisputed works. For it is utterly inconsistent with any appearance of truth that one and the same writer—prudent, grave, and learned—would contradict himself and weave into his books propositions that are sometimes diametrically opposed. Now, if someone should later revoke and reshape opinions they once promoted (as Augustine did in his books of *Retractions* written near the end of his life), that is a mark of the highest prudence and candor. I would not want these things to be taken in the sense that one and the same writer never contradicts himself anywhere, especially in matters of lighter weight; for of that kind, especially in <term>prolific writers</term> <note>original: "πολυγράφοις"</note>, many examples can be given, whether it happened through a fault of memory or the heat of debate. Rather, I mean that it rarely happens, and not in the kind of <term>inconsistency</term> <note>original: "ἀνωμαλίαν"</note> that would hardly befall a learned and wise man. Who would believe that Origen—who in his *Commentary on Job* <margin>* Volume 2. folio 28. C.</margin> taught that Christ preached for three and a half years, from his baptism until his passion—would have clearly affirmed in his work *On First Principles* <note>original: "περὶ ἀρχῶν"</note>, Book 4, Chapter 1, <margin>† Volume 4. folio 150. G.</margin> † that He preached for only a single year and a few months—

<meta>Continuity check: This page continues the list of rules for identifying forged ecclesiastical texts. The previous page ended with Rule IV, discussing stylistic consistency. This page begins by concluding Rule V regarding doctrinal consistency, then introduces Rule VI about linguistic anomalies (Greek authors using Latin), and Rule VII regarding the testimony of ancient historians.</meta>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

<margin>Page 281.</margin> ...had asserted. Justin Martyr <note>An early Christian apologist, c. 100–165 AD</note>, in his *Dialogue with Trypho*, claims that the angel speaking with Jacob was uncreated, and asserts not only that he should *be called God*, but that he *is God* <note>original Greek: "theon kaleisthai... theon einai"</note>. Yet in the *Questions to the Orthodox* attributed to him (*Question 142*), he writes that the angel obtained *the rank and the authority of God* <note>original Greek: "tēn taxin kai tēn hēgemonian tou Theou"</note> only because of the duty entrusted to him, and in no other sense than that in which humans are called *gods*. Once that duty is finished, those who received the name of God because of some function cease to be called *gods*. Who could believe that the *Homily on the Mother of God* <note>Latin: "de Deipara"</note>, in which he teaches that the Blessed Virgin should be invoked, belongs to Athanasius? In his undisputed sermons against the Arians <note>A group who denied the full divinity of Christ</note>, Athanasius clearly demonstrates and explicitly affirms that it is entirely unlawful for any creature to adore another creature. The books of the *Hypognosticon* <note>A five-part work on grace and free will</note> are rightly stripped from Augustine's name, as they openly teach Pelagian dogmas <note>The beliefs of Pelagius, which Augustine famously spent his later life refuting</note>. Among his indubitable works, no one attacked or pursued the dogmas of Origen more strongly than Cyril of Alexandria; this is a most certain argument that the *Homilies on Leviticus* are not his. In those homilies, there are many testimonies concerning the pre-existence of souls, and the idea that the punishments of the wicked—and even of Demons—will eventually have an end, along with other Origenist dreams of that kind.

VI. *If in any work claiming a Greek author, there occurs a judgment or explanation of Latin words, it raises a most deserved suspicion of falsehood or fraud.* As our own Holdsworth <note>Richard Holdsworth (1590–1649), an English theologian</note> rightly notes, this is completely foreign to the writing habits of the Greek Fathers. This rule is especially applicable in weeding out various small works of Origen. Regarding the *Commentaries on Job, the Song of Songs, and Psalms 36, 37, and 38*, and the *Homilies on various passages of Matthew*, after Erasmus and Sixtus of Siena, scholars like Cooke, Rivet, and others have observed this; it is so clear that these are either the works of a Latin man or have been significantly <term>interpolated</term> <note>altered by a later editor or translator</note> by a translator. The same must be said of the commentaries on Leviticus by Hesychius of Jerusalem; in these, to remain silent on other issues, he uses Jerome’s version and even calls it "our translation." To this class also belong various treatises which, although they falsely claim Greek parents, are almost certainly Latin offspring. Of such a sort are Melito’s book *On the Passing of the Blessed Mary*, Origen’s *Lament*, and several *Homilies*; Athanasius’s *Letters to Mark, Liberius, and Felix*, and so on. Nor does the mask less deservedly fall from an imposter when a Greek author is alleged to have written in Latin. By this evidence, the *Synopsis on the Life and Death of the Prophets and Apostles* by Dorotheus loses its standing; an anonymous Greek author, whose work I have now brought to light for the first time, pretends that Dorotheus wrote in Latin. For who could ever be persuaded that Dorotheus—a man of Syrian birth and language, or at least Greek, and a bishop of Tyre among the Phoenicians—would have recorded his monuments in the Latin tongue?

VII. *Whatever small works the Ancients condemned as spurious should be admitted with great difficulty, and only with the clearest and most powerful arguments; especially if the testimony of those who would have an interest in them being genuine is added.* What judgment Eusebius, Epiphanius, Jerome, Gennadius, and Photius passed on various writings circulated under the names of the ancients is not something that needs to be explained at length to the learned...
<meta>catchword: I will deal with [agam];</meta>

<summary>This page outlines two major criteria for identifying forged religious texts: doctrinal inconsistency (where a work contradicts an author's known theology) and linguistic/cultural errors (such as a Greek author using Latin sources). The author cites numerous examples, including works falsely attributed to Justin Martyr, Athanasius, and Origen.</summary>
<keywords>textual criticism, forgeries, Church Fathers, Justin Martyr, Athanasius, Origen, Augustine, Pelagianism, Greek vs Latin authors, patristics</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on identifying authentic texts, concluding the previous section on Roman Catholic scholars' efforts to distinguish genuine from spurious works, and transitions into Section V, which outlines practical rules for students on how to select reliable printed editions of the Church Fathers.</meta>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

I shall proceed; since the books of all these authors are handled daily by everyone. The decree of Pope Gelasius <note>Gelasius I, Pope from 492–496 AD; the decree mentioned is the *Decretum Gelasianum*</note> *concerning apocryphal and condemned books*, although we shall prove it to be a forgery in its proper place, nevertheless maintains its authority intact among the Roman Catholics; thus, whatever the Pseudo-Gelasius has banished to the status of forgeries, they must consider as counterfeit. Driven indeed by the force of truth, there have not been lacking even in the midst of the papacy more sensible <note>Latin: *cordatiores*</note> Roman Catholics who have struck many writings from the register of genuine <note>Greek: *gnēsiōn*</note> Fathers, and relegated them either to the doubtful or the apocryphal. In everyone's mouths and hands are Sixtus of Siena, Possevino, Bellarmine, Sirmond, Petau, Labbe, Miré, Valois, Fronton du Duc, De Marca, Baluze, Mabillon, Du Pin, and others. Baronius <note>Cesare Baronius (1538–1607), a cardinal and historian known for his *Annales Ecclesiastici*</note> is almost the only one who is accustomed to defending flimsy and fabulous writers with all the diligence he can; and when they can be defended by no art, he is still not ashamed to extract historical monuments from *them*. <margin>* For the year 52, no. 10.</margin> *Lest anything*, as he himself admits, *should seem to have been withdrawn from the greedy minds of readers.* We state these things briefly; others have said more. For it was not my intention to gather everything that could be said on this matter.

---

## SECTION V.

### General Rules to be applied in selecting editions of the Holy Fathers.

There is almost nothing in the whole circle of literature into which younger men, and those less practiced in the theological course, are more accustomed to stumble than in procuring for their use bad or less convenient editions of the Holy Fathers; while, with no care and no selection made, they seize whatever is at hand, whatever offers itself to them at first sight. Thus it happens—not without a heavy loss of expense, time, and what is even more precious, truth itself—that they daily wear out editions that are imperfect, corrupt, teeming with faults and errors, which deceive readers and not rarely lead them away from the right path of truth. It is indeed surprising that among so many cultivators of ecclesiastical antiquity whom this or the previous age has produced, no one is found who has written on this matter specifically <note>Latin: *ex professo*</note>. Many have pointed out editions of the Fathers here and there as the occasion arose; but no one, as far as I know, has brought light to this matter by a dedicated work or even some slight little dissertation. Until, therefore, someone arises who might roll this stone <note>A classical metaphor for undertaking a difficult task</note> more diligently, I will perform this duty for a little while for the sake of youth who are lovers of antiquity <note>Greek: *philarchaias*</note>, and I will briefly deliver in this place some observations reduced to general rules and instructions. Let others add more, or let them not disdain to add their own to ours.

I. *The older the editions of the Holy Fathers are, the more faithful they are for the most part*; which proceeds from a double cause. 1. Because the editions that were produced in the very cradle of printing <note>The author uses the term *incunabulis*, referring to the earliest stage of the printing press, typically books printed before 1501</note> were printed from the best possible manuscripts <note>Latin: *MSS*</note>, so that since many of these are now lost...

<summary>The author identifies scholars who have critiqued forged texts and introduces a new section providing practical guidance for students on selecting the most accurate and reliable printed editions of early Christian writings.</summary>
<keywords>Gelasius, Baronius, textual criticism, patristics, printing history, incunabula, bibliography, ecclesiastical antiquity</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the "Prolegomena" or Preliminary Discourses. The author is transitioning from a discussion of identifying genuine works to Section V, Rule I, which argues that the earliest printed editions of the Church Fathers (incunabula) are the most reliable because they were produced before the religious controversies of the Reformation led to textual censorship and corruption. The catchword "omni" from the previous page's metadata connects to the (implied) start of the discussion on the reliability of early editions.</meta>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

...in most Manuscript Books <note>original: *Codicibus MSS.*</note>, those [early] editions can deservedly take the place of manuscripts themselves. 2. Because the controversies regarding the reform of the Church had not yet been born, and the disagreement between the Romans and the Reformed over the articles of faith and the corruptions of the Roman Church had not yet arisen, there could be no sufficiently suitable reason for them to use fraud in printing the works of the Fathers. There was no "Hannibal at the gates" yet <note>a classical expression meaning a dangerous enemy is at the door; here referring to the Protestant reformers who would later use the Fathers' words against the Catholic Church</note>; there was almost no one to move camp against them or to bring forward the frequent ranks of the Fathers into the battle-line. Hence, they presented the Ecclesiastical Writers to us without deceit or pretense, passing down their own doctrines in their own words. And would that it had never been done otherwise! But alas! by so many shameful examples it is clearer than the sun that the Fathers have been foully corrupted; the older editions, as far as was possible, were suppressed; the following ones were truncated or interpolated; indeed, they broke out into such madness that, when reduced to desperation <note>literally "reduced to the moves," a metaphor for being checkmated or cornered in a game</note>, they do not blush to deny that this or that edition ever existed. This is what I noted in its proper place concerning Christian Druthmar’s Commentaries on Matthew <note>Christian Druthmar was a 9th-century monk whose literal interpretations of the Eucharist were later suppressed by those favoring a more mystical/transubstantiation view</note>, and perhaps more instances of that kind can be noted. To these points it may be added that the first managers of printing brought out their works with great purity, not leaving the care of correction to ignorant, inept, or common <note>literally "sought from the crossroads"</note> *correctors*, as they call them. 

For this reason, editions issued from the invention of printing—that is, from around the year 1442, or rather 1450, or perhaps 1460, when it began to be brought to a slightly more perfect form—up until the reformation of religion begun by Luther in the year 1517, ought to be valued as worth more than gold. Those which appeared from that time until the Council of Trent <note>1545–1563</note> come closest to them in value. For a long time, learned men have lamented the rarity of the old editions; the first champions <note>literally *Antesignani*, the elite front-line soldiers</note> of the reformed religion labored with an immense abundance of them, which their successors—enticed by shinier typefaces and more numerous additions—ignored to our great loss. Hence, having been either destroyed or suppressed, they are now sought almost in vain and no longer appear, except very rarely, like *swimmers in a vast whirlpool after a shipwreck* <note>a quote from Virgil's *Aeneid*, describing the survivors of a storm</note>. It would certainly be desirable that as many as survive today be placed in public libraries, where they would have a safer home and a more convenient opportunity for anyone to inspect them. Until this happens, I wish there were someone who would take this labor upon himself: to seek out editions from all sides, diligently search the shelves of libraries, and make for us an accurate catalog of them and indicate where they lie hidden. 

Philippe Labbe <note>a Jesuit scholar and prolific bibliographer (1607–1667)</note>, who has deserved well of the Republic of Letters on more than one account, performed something in this regard and reviewed the old editions from the year 1459 to 1500 which are preserved in the Royal Library of Paris, in his *Supplement to the New Library of Manuscripts*, volume 9, page 337. Just as these things were being sent to the press, I happened upon Cornelis van Beughem's *Cradle of Typography* <note>Original: *Incunabula Typographiae*; "incunabula" is a technical term for books printed before 1501</note>, or *A Catalog of books and writings in any language published in the years closest to the invention of printing up to the year of Christ 1500 inclusive*, Amsterdam, 1688, in duodecimo format.

II. *The works of the Holy Fathers which appeared in Greek from the workshop of Robert Estienne* <note>Robertus Stephanus (1503–1559), a famous Parisian printer and scholar who published the first critical editions of the Greek New Testament and the Fathers</note> *easily surpass other editions in the care of their correction and the beauty of their type.* When literature first began to flourish again, the study of the Greek language was vigorously promoted. Hence, so many Greek writers...

<summary>This page argues that printed editions of the Church Fathers produced between 1450 and 1517 are uniquely reliable because they predated the theological censorship of the Reformation and Counter-Reformation. The author laments the loss of these "incunabula" and praises early scholarly printers like Robert Estienne for their accuracy.</summary>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

...the directors of the first printers <note>original Greek: *τυπογραφέων*; refers to the master printers of the Renaissance who acted as both publishers and scholars</note> produced editions destitute of any [Latin] version, not even thinking in their dreams of the ignorance and laziness of later generations, who would only consult Latin translations. Among these, the Stephanus workshop <note>the famous Estienne family of Paris and Geneva</note> beyond all doubt held the first place. Regarding Henri I Stephanus, the father of Robert; and Henri II, the son of Robert (known to all scholars), and other branches of the Stephanus family, most famous in the art of printing, it is not our task nor do we have the leisure to discuss them at present. Theodore Jansson, Doctor of Medicine, did this with much praise in his *Epistolary Dissertation on the Lives of the Stephani, Amsterdam 1683, octavo.* 

What concerns our Robert, we will dispatch in a few words. He was a man highly learned in Latin, Greek, and Hebrew letters; he studiously surveyed not only his own France, but also foreign Academies and Libraries. So many books published from his workshop, so many written by his own effort, testify to his erudition; especially that distinguished *Thesaurus of the Latin Language*; truly a Treasury, such as no previous age saw, and no future age will produce. While still a young man, he took care of the printing house of Simon de Colines, his stepfather; then, having built his own workshop, he diligently managed his affairs: finally, he was appointed Royal Printer by Francis I, that great patron <note>original: *Maecenate*; a reference to Gaius Maecenas, the Roman patron of the arts</note> of letters. 

In this office, with what care and diligence he conducted himself, his son Henri teaches us in the *preface to Gellius*: namely, that his father had a kind of literary "ten-man commission" <note>original: *Decemviratum*; a term borrowed from Roman government to describe a specialized board of experts</note> at home, gathered from almost all nations. He employed these ten men for various parts of promoting literary affairs; chief among them were those who performed the duty of correctors. Yet he did not entrust this matter entirely to the eyes of others; but he reread almost everything with his own eyes, with such zeal and accurate diligence that, as the illustrious Jansson notes, the story went that he was accustomed to hang individual sheets to be read through here and there in certain places through the streets, with a reward attached for anyone who detected certain errors—even his own—prizes according to how enormous the faults were. 

To these he added the highest beauty of typefaces, a plainly admirable elegance, so much so that he left all printers up to this day at a long distance behind him and provoked envy. Witnesses are—to set aside external writers <note>original Greek: *τὰς ἔξωθεν*; referring to non-Christian or secular authors</note>—the *New Testament, the works of Justin Martyr, Eusebius of Caesarea, the Ecclesiastical History of Socrates, Sozomen, Theodoret, Theodorus, Evagrius, etc.*, printed so splendidly, neatly, and correctly that the mere luster of the types alone would allure and invite us to read them. 

But look, finally, at the reward for virtue, erudition, and so many labors exhausted for the benefit of the literary republic. The theologians of the Sorbonne, offended by certain annotations which he had added under the name of Vatable <note>François Vatable, a renowned Hebrew scholar whose notes Robert edited</note> to the Latin Bibles he published, accused the man of heresy, and did not cease to cause him perpetual trouble for many years; so much so that after the death of King Francis, weary of vexations and injuries, he found it necessary to change his soil <note>meaning he went into exile</note>; indeed, even after his departure, his effigy was burned by the public hand of the executioner. 

Therefore, around the year 1551, he moved his household gods <note>original: *Penates*; the Roman deities of the home, used here to mean his family and property</note> and his entire bookstore and workshop to Geneva, where, diligently applying himself to his art, he happily departed from human affairs on the 7th of the Ides of September <note>September 7th</note> in the year 1559, at age 56. <margin>* Book 33, year 1559, page 708.</margin> The most distinguished Thuanus <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou, a famous historian</note> says: *He surpassed Aldus of Venice and Froben of Basel by a long distance, with sharp judgment, accurate diligence, and the elegance of the art itself; for which not only France, but the whole Christian world...*

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

...in almost all manuscript Codices, those [early] editions can, not without merit, take the place of the Manuscripts themselves. 2. Because the controversies regarding the reform of the Church had not yet been born, and the discord between the Romans <note>the Roman Catholic Church</note> and the Reformers <note>Protestant Reformers</note> regarding articles of faith and the corruptions of the Roman Church had not yet arisen, there could be no sufficiently suitable reason for them to use fraud in printing the works of the Fathers. There was no "Hannibal at the gates" <note>original: *Hannibal ad portas*; a Roman proverb meaning an immediate, life-threatening crisis</note> yet; there was almost no one to move camp against them, or to bring forward the frequent ranks of the Fathers into the battle-line. Hence, they presented the ecclesiastical writers to us without fraud or artificial coloring, handing down their own dogmas in their own words. And if only it had never been done otherwise! 

But alas! It is clearer than the sun, by so many shameful examples, that the Fathers have been foully corrupted. The older editions were suppressed as much as possible; the following ones were truncated or interpolated <note>altered by inserting new, often false, material</note>; indeed, they have broken out into such madness that, reduced to desperation, they do not blush to deny that this or that edition ever existed. I have noted this in its proper place regarding the *Commentaries on Matthew* by Christian Druthmar <note>a 9th-century Benedictine monk; his work was controversial because it was cited in debates over transubstantiation</note>, and perhaps more of that kind can be noted. To these points it can be added that the first caretakers of the art of printing sent out their works with as much correction as possible; they did not leave the care of emending to ignorant, inept people sought from the "crossroads" <note>original: *è trivio petitis*; meaning common, uneducated, or low-quality workers</note>, whom they call *correctors*. 

For this reason, editions published from the invention of typography—that is, from around the year 1442, or rather 1450, perhaps 1460 when it began to be brought to a slightly more perfect form—up until the reformation of religion begun by Luther in the year 1517, ought to be valued as worth more than gold <note>original: *auro contra non charae*; literally "not expensive even when weighed against gold"</note>. Those which appeared from that time until the Council of Trent <note>1545–1563</note> come closest to these in value. 

For a long time now, learned men have deplored the rarity of these ancient editions. The first leaders of the reformed religion worked with an immense supply of them; but their successors, enticed by cleaner fonts and heaped-up additions, disregarded them to our great loss. Hence, they are now either destroyed or suppressed and are sought almost in vain; they appear no more, except very rarely, like survivors of a shipwreck swimming in the vast deep <note>original: *rari nantes in gurgite vasto*; a famous quote from Virgil’s Aeneid describing sailors lost at sea</note>. It would be truly desirable that however many survive today should be placed in public libraries, where they would have a safer home and everyone would have a more convenient ability to inspect them. Until this happens, I wish there were someone to take up this labor: to search everywhere for these editions, diligently shake out the shelves of libraries, and produce an accurate catalog of them for us, indicating where they lie hidden. 

Philippe Labbe <note>a famous French Jesuit bibliographer</note> performed something in this regard and reviewed the old editions from the year 1459 to 1500 which are preserved in the Royal Library of Paris; he deserves credit from the Republic of Letters <note>the international community of scholars</note> for more than one reason in his *Supplement to the New Collection of Manuscripts*, volume 9, page 337. Just as these pages were being sent to the press, I came across Cornelius à Beughem’s *The Cradle of Typography, or a Catalog of books and writings published in any language in the years nearest to the invention of Typography, up to and including the Year of Christ 1500. Amsterdam, 1688, duodecimo.*

II. *The works of the Holy Fathers which came from the workshop of Robert Stephanus in Greek easily surpass other editions in the care of their emendation and the beauty of their type.* When the study of literature first began to flourish again, the study of the Greek language was vigorously promoted. Hence so many Greek writers...

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES

...the curators of the first printers produced [editions] destitute of any [alternate] version, never even dreaming in their sleep of the ignorance and laziness of those who came after, who would consult only Latin translations. Among these, the Estienne workshop <note>The "Officina Stephanea," the famous printing house of the Estienne family in Paris</note> held the first rank beyond all doubt. To speak at present of Henry I Estienne, the father of Robert; or of Henry II, the son of Robert (known to all learned men); and of the other branches of the Estienne family who were particularly famous in the art of printing, is neither the purpose of our work nor within our leisure. Theodore Jansson, M.D., did this not without praise in his *Epistolary Dissertation on the Lives of the Estiennes*, Amsterdam, 1683, in octavo. We shall briefly dispatch what concerns our Robert.

He was a man highly learned in Latin, Greek, and Hebrew literature; he studiously surveyed the academies and libraries not only of his own France but also those abroad. His erudition is attested by so many books issued from his workshop, and so many written by his own effort; especially that remarkable *Thesaurus of the Latin Language*; truly a "Treasure," the likes of which no previous age saw, and no future age will produce. While still a young man, he managed the printing business of his stepfather, Simon de Colines; later, having built his own workshop, he diligently managed his own affairs. Finally, he was appointed King's Printer by Francis I, that great patron of letters. How much care and sedulity he displayed in this office, his son Henry tells us in the *preface to Agellius*: namely, that his father maintained a kind of "literary committee of ten" <note>original: *Decemviratum*; a reference to the Roman board of ten men, here meaning a council of experts</note> at home, assembled from almost every nation. He employed these ten men in various parts of promoting literary affairs; chief among them were those who performed the duty of correctors. Nor did he entrust this matter entirely to the eyes of others; rather, he re-read almost everything with his own eyes, with such great zeal and accurate diligence that, as the illustrious Jansson notes, the story went that he was accustomed to hang up individual sheets to be read here and there through the streets at certain places, attaching a reward for anyone who detected defects—even his own—according to how significant they were. To this he added the highest beauty of typefaces and a clearly admirable elegance, so much so that he has left all printers to this day a long distance behind him and provoked their envy. 

<margin>secular [writers]</margin> <note>original Greek: *τὰς ἔξωθεν*; literally "those from outside," referring to non-Christian or secular authors</note> Witness to this—to pass over secular writers—are the New Testament, the works of Justin Martyr, Eusebius of Caesarea, and the *Ecclesiastical Histories* of Socrates, Sozomen, Theodoret, Theodorus, Evagrius, etc., printed so splendidly, neatly, and correctly that the mere brilliance of the typefaces allures and invites us to read them. 

But see at last the reward for his virtue, erudition, and so many labors exhausted for the benefit of the literary republic. Certain theologians of the Sorbonne <note>The faculty of theology at the University of Paris, known for its strict orthodoxy</note>, offended by some annotations he had added to the Latin Bibles he published under the name of Vatable <note>François Vatable, a humanist scholar whose notes were suspected of Protestant leanings</note>, accused the man of heresy. They did not cease to cause him perpetual trouble for many years; so much so that after the death of King Francis, weary of harassments and injuries, he found it necessary to "change his soil" <note>original: *solum vertere*; a Latin idiom for going into exile</note>. Indeed, after his departure, his effigy was publicly burned by the hand of the executioner. Therefore, around the year 1551, he moved his household gods and his entire printing workshop to Geneva, where, applying himself industriously to his art, he happily departed from human affairs on the 7th of the Ides of September <note>September 7th</note> in the year 1559, at the age of 56. *He surpassed Aldus of Venice and Froben of Basel by a long distance*, says the most distinguished Thuanus <margin>\* Book 33, for the year 1559, p. 708.</margin> <note>Jacques Auguste de Thou, a famous French historian</note>, *in keen judgment, accurate diligence, and the elegance of the art itself; for which reason not only France, but the entire Christian world...*

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES

<margin>Portrait in R. Stephanus under the letter Y. ii.</margin>
...he owes more to him than has ever been owed to any of the bravest military leaders for expanding the borders of the fatherland; and a greater honor and never-perishing glory redounded to Francis <note>King Francis I of France, known as a patron of the arts and letters</note> from his industry alone than from so many things famously achieved in war and peace. A twin praise to this is given by **Theodore Beza**: *As much as those others (Aldus, Froben, and others) excelled the rest, so much greater glory must be granted to you, Robert Estienne, who has surpassed all your predecessors far and wide, as even envy itself grumbling confesses. For while some others may contend with you regarding the elegance and beauty of certain printed books, no one could be compared with you in the faithfulness and diligence of correction, even in the most laborious works. We owe to you the Hebrew language greatly illustrated, the supreme elegance of Greek characters, etc.* Finally, there were several more famous printers around these times who remarkably advanced Greek letters, both sacred and profane, with their presses: at Venice, **Aldus Manutius** and his son **Paulus**, both in his father’s workshop and later at Rome; at Paris, besides the Estiennes, **Adrien Turnèbe**, Professor of Languages and Philosophy and Royal Printer—for at that time, the most celebrated Professors did not disdain the printer's office. This is still witnessed by **Johannes Oporinus** and **Franciscus Raphelengius**; the latter a Professor and Printer of the Hebrew language in Leiden, the former of Greek in the Academy of Basel. At Geneva first, then at Heidelberg, **Hieronymus Commelinus**, a grave and learned man who gave us many monuments of the **Greek Fathers** <note>The early influential theologians and writers of the Christian Church who wrote in Greek</note> correctly edited, and dying in the year 1597, handed down that same care to his sons as if by hereditary right. I pass over others almost infinite, especially more recent ones, about whom **Bernhard von Mallinckrodt** wrote in a special chapter of his *Historical Dissertation on the Art of Typography*, chapter 14, page 91.

III. The Frobenian editions of the **Latin Fathers** are rightly and deservedly held in great esteem, as they were produced with the highest faithfulness and with no less beauty for that age. Concerning the Frobenian printing house, **Erasmus** says this:
<margin>* To Leo X, book 2, Epistle 1.</margin>
*There is no other workshop more accurate than the Frobenian, or from which more good books issue, especially those pertaining to sacred literature.* Nor otherwise does
<margin>† Ibid. book 11, Epistle 13.</margin>
**Nicolaus Beraldus** † write to Erasmus: *I wish it might happen that all those things were seen shortly printed in the most beautiful types, namely the Frobenian ones, than which I think nothing can be made more polished, more elegant, or more pleasant.* **Johann Froben**, from the house of Hammelburg, the first founder of the workshop, easily the prince among printers—if not of all, at least of the Germans—fixed his household at Basel; a man of remarkable probity, faith, erudition, industry, and a clearly incredible zeal for assisting literary matters. He spared no vigils, no labors, and finally no expenses, so that he might adorn excellent authors and send them out into the light, polished and faithfully prepared. *He had at his home*, says Erasmus, *a man most learned in both literatures <note>Referring to expertise in both Latin and Greek</note>, and endowed with equal faithfulness, which I certainly count among the foremost things.*
<margin>* Book 19, Epistle 8.</margin>
He designates, I believe, the man appointed to the office of <term>corrector</term> <note>An editor responsible for checking proofs against original manuscripts for accuracy</note>. In which place he had **Lachner**, carried off by the plague a few years ago, the father-in-law of Froben himself and the chief of the Frobenian workshop, whose death Erasmus laments elsewhere †.
<margin>† Book 3, Epistle 20.</margin>
He also had sons nurtured in good literature, who strenuously busied themselves with their father's business to the best of their ability. But the greatest glory of the Frobenian workshop was that great **Erasmus**, the ornament of his century, whom Froben always had as a patron, and very often as a guest in his own home for several months...

<meta>This page continues the "Prolegomena" (Preliminary Discourses) from the previous discussion of Robert Estienne. It shifts focus to the famous Basel printer Johannes Froben and his collaboration with Erasmus of Rotterdam in the restoration of ancient Christian texts.</meta>

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES

Frobenius <note>Johannes Froben (c. 1460–1527), a famous printer in Basel known for his scholarly editions.</note>; by whose truly astounding care and untiring diligence the works of Irenaeus, Cyprian, Hilary, Jerome, Augustine, and others were sought out from every side, accurately reviewed, and published under favorable auspices <note>original: "bonis avibus," an idiom referring to good omens</note>, and repeated there more than once in subsequent editions. How many difficulties, how much sweat, and how many troubles Erasmus underwent in this matter, you may learn from the single edition of Jerome; regarding which he says in a Letter to William Warham, Archbishop of Canterbury: *“This one thing, let me say truly and boldly: I believe it cost Jerome less to compose his books than it cost us to restore them; and they were born with fewer sleepless nights for him than they were reborn for us. Having despised all difficulties with a certain Herculean spirit, I entered upon a most laborious but truly beautiful province, intending to defeat, almost alone, so many monstrous errors; for I do not think Hercules exhausted as much labor in taming a few monsters as I did in removing so many thousands of errors.”* Johannes Froben died, seized by a sudden paralysis, in the year 1527. For this most upright friend, who deserved so well of the <term>Republic of Letters</term> <note>The "Republic of Letters" (Respublica literaria) was the long-distance intellectual community of scholars in the late 17th and 18th centuries.</note> for so many distinguished monuments of such great men, Erasmus placed an Epitaph, which indeed I will not be reluctant to add in this place:

> ->*This stone covers the dry bones of Johannes Froben,*<-
> ->*His fame, which knows not how to die, flourishes in the whole world.*<-
> ->*He earned this by his pure character and by helping studies:*<-
> ->*Which now lie mournful, orphaned of their parent.*<-
> ->*He brought back and adorned the monuments of the ancient wise men,*<-
> ->*With art, hand, care, money, favor, and faith.*<-
> ->*Grant him perennial life in the heavens, just deities;*<-
> ->*Through us on earth his fame shall be perennial.*<-

Intent rather on the benefit of others than his own, he increased the scholarly world more than his own family estate, and left to his heirs more of an honorable reputation than of money. Johannes Hervagius succeeded to the office as well as the marriage-bed of the deceased <note>Hervagius married Froben's widow and took over the printing business.</note>; he was a man of good faith and not unlearned, who, in promoting and adorning literature and in restoring good authors, employed a care and diligence if not equal to Froben, then certainly nearly equal. And with Erasmus especially providing help and counsel, he printed those same Fathers, diligently reviewed and most correctly amended, and called others back from the darkness into the light. Concerning the editions from Cologne, Louvain, Lyon, Antwerp, and Rome, there is no reason for me to delay the reader with many words, since it is known to anyone that they fall below the Basel editions by an immense distance, at least in faithfulness and correction. The ecclesiastical writers whom Rigaltius, Sirmond, Garner, Baluze, and most recently, the Benedictine Monks <note>The Maurists, a French Benedictine congregation famous for their high-quality scholarly editions of the Church Fathers.</note>, have sent forth from the Parisian presses, far surpass all other more recent editions.

IV. The works of the Holy Fathers, which the Parisians have published in Greek and Latin, snatch the prize <note>original: "palmam praeripiunt," meaning to take the first place or victory.</note> from others in their kind. A significant argument is drawn from this: that for a long time among the restorers of reborn literature, the study of the Greek language held its dominion, because Greek writers at that time were published only in Greek. And although it could occasionally happen otherwise, it happened only rarely; nor was it in common use until the previous century was verging toward its end

<page-num>xvi</page-num>
<header>PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES</header>

<margin>Portrait of R. Estienne under the letter Y. ii.</margin>
[France] owes more twice over [to Robert Estienne] than it ever owed to any of the bravest military leaders for expanding the borders of the fatherland; greater honor and never-fading glory have flowed to Francis <note>King Francis I of France, a major patron of the Renaissance</note> from the industry of this one man than from so many illustrious deeds performed in war and peace. Similar to this is the eulogy of Theodore Beza: <note>Theodore Beza (1519–1605) was a French Protestant theologian and scholar who succeeded John Calvin</note> *By as much as those others (Aldus, Froben, and others) excelled the rest, by so much more glory must be attributed to you, Robert Estienne—having surpassed all your predecessors far and wide—as even grumbling Envy herself must confess. For though some others may compete with you regarding the elegance and beauty of certain printed books, no one, even in the most laborious works, can be compared to you in the faithfulness and diligence of your editing.* We owe to you the great illumination of the Hebrew language, the supreme elegance of Greek characters, and so on. Finally, there were many other famous printers around this time who significantly advanced Greek literature, both sacred and profane, with their presses: Aldus Manutius in Venice, and his son Paul, both in the paternal workshop and later in Rome; in Paris, besides the Estiennes, Adrien Turnèbe, a professor of languages and philosophy and a Royal Printer <note>The "Typographus Regius" was a prestigious position appointed by the French Crown</note>; for at that time, even the most celebrated professors did not disdain the office of printing. This is still attested by Johannes Oporinus and Franciscus Raphelengius—the latter a professor of Hebrew at Leiden, the former a professor of Greek at the University of Basel—who were both professors and printers simultaneously. In Geneva first, and then in Heidelberg, there was Hieronymus Commelinus, a grave and learned man, who gave us many accurate editions of the Greek Fathers <note>The "Greek Fathers" refers to the influential early Christian theologians who wrote in Greek</note> and, upon his death in 1597, left this same care to his sons as if by hereditary right. I remain silent about almost countless others, especially more recent ones, regarding whom Bernard von Mallinckrodt wrote a specific chapter in his *Historical Dissertation on the Art of Typography*, chapter 14, page 91.

---

<margin>
* To Leo X, book 2, letter 1.
† Ibid. book 11, letter 13.
</margin>
III. The Frobenian editions of the Latin Fathers <note>The "Latin Fathers" refers to early Christian theologians who wrote in Latin, such as Augustine or Jerome</note> are justly and deservedly held in high esteem, as they were produced with the greatest faithfulness and with no less beauty for that age. Erasmus said this of the Froben press: *, *There is no other workshop more accurate than the Frobenian, nor one from which more good books are issued, especially those pertaining to sacred literature.* Nor did Nicolas Bérault † think otherwise when he wrote to Erasmus, *I wish that it may happen soon that I see all those works printed with the most beautiful types—namely the Frobenian ones—than which I think nothing can be made cleaner, more elegant, or more pleasant.* Johannes Froben, originally from Hammelburg, the first founder of the workshop and easily the prince of printers (if not of all, at least of the Germans), established his home in Basel. He was a man of remarkable integrity, faith, erudition, industry, and a truly incredible zeal for aiding the world of letters; he spared no late nights, no labors, and finally no expenses, so that he might honor excellent authors and send them out into the light, elegantly and faithfully prepared. <margin>» Book 19, Letter 8.</margin> *He had in his house,* says Erasmus *, *a man most learned in both literatures <note>Meaning Latin and Greek</note> and endowed with equal faithfulness, which I certainly count as a primary asset.* He refers, I believe, to the man placed in charge of the duty of correcting <note>A "corrector" in an early modern press was a scholar who proofread and edited the text for accuracy</note>. In this position he had Wolfgang Lachner, who was taken by the plague a few years ago, the father-in-law of Froben himself and a leader of the Frobenian workshop; Erasmus mourns his death elsewhere ‡. <margin>‡ Book 3, Letter 20.</margin> He also had sons nourished in good literature, who worked strenuously for the paternal business to the best of their ability. But the greatest glory of the Frobenian workshop was the great Erasmus himself, the ornament of his century, whom Froben always had as a patron and very often as a guest in his home for many months...

# PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES

[Johann] Froben; by whose truly astounding care and tireless diligence the works of Irenaeus, Cyprian, Hilary, Jerome, Augustine, and others were sought from every corner, accurately revised, and—under favorable omens—published, being repeated there more than once in subsequent editions. You may learn how many difficulties, how much sweat, and how many troubles Erasmus underwent in this matter even from the single edition of Jerome; concerning which he says in a Letter to William Warham, the Archbishop of Canterbury:
*“This one thing I will say, truly and boldly: I believe it cost Jerome less to have composed his books than it cost us to restore them; and that they were born with fewer sleepless nights for him than they were reborn for us.”* <note>original: "Unum illud, ut verè dicam & audactèr, minoris arbitror Hieronymo suos constituisse libros conditos, quam nobis restitutos..."</note> — Having despised all difficulties, *“with a certain Herculean spirit, I approached a most laborious but truly beautiful province, alone about to do battle with so many portents of errors; for I do not think as much labor was exhausted by Hercules in taming a few monsters as by me in removing so many thousands of errors.”* <note>Erasmus often compared his editorial work to the "Labors of Hercules," particularly the cleaning of the Augean stables, due to the messy state of medieval manuscripts.</note> Johann Froben died, seized by sudden paralysis, in the year 1527. For his most upright friend, who deserved so well from the <term>republic of letters<gloss>The international community of scholars and intellectuals (res publica literaria).</gloss></term> for so many famous monuments of such great men, Erasmus placed an Epitaph, which indeed it will not be unpleasant to include here:

->This stone covers the dry bones of Johann Froben,<-
->In the whole world his fame flourishes, knowing not how to die.<-
->He earned this through pure morals and by aiding studies:<-
->Which now lie sad, deprived of their father.<-
->He restored and adorned the monuments of the ancient wise men,<-
->By art, hand, care, money, favor, and faith.<-
->To him, O just spirits, give eternal life in the heavens;<-
->Through us on earth, his fame shall be eternal.<-

Intent on the benefit of others rather than his own, he increased the literary estate more than his family's, and left more of an honest reputation to his heirs than money. Johann Herwagen <note>Johann Herwagen (Hervagius) took over Froben's press in 1528 and married Froben's widow, Gertrude Lachner.</note> succeeded to the workshop as well as the marriage-bed of the deceased; a man of good faith and not unlearned, who in promoting and adorning letters and restoring good authors applied care and diligence if not equal, then certainly nearly equal, to Froben. With Erasmus especially providing help and advice, he printed the same [Church] Fathers, diligently revised and most correctly edited, and called others back into the light from the shadows. Concerning the editions from Cologne, Louvain, Lyon, Antwerp, and Rome, there is no reason I should delay the reader with many words, since it is known to anyone that they sit at an immense distance below those of Basel in terms of faithfulness and correction. The ecclesiastical writers whom Rigault, Sirmond, Garnier, Baluze, and most recently, the Benedictine Monks, have released from the Parisian presses, far surpass all other more recent editions.

IV. **The works of the Holy Fathers, which the Parisians released in Greek and Latin, snatch the prize from others of their kind.** That the study of the Greek language held its dominion for a long time among the restorers of reborn literature is proven by the fact that Greek writers at that time were published only in Greek. And although it could have happened otherwise occasionally, it occurred rarely; and not until the previous century was turning toward its end did it come into common...

### XVIII PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

<margin>* History, Book 36, page 288.</margin>
It began to be common for Greek books to be published in both languages, with a Latin version added in a parallel column. Indeed, the cultivation of this most eloquent and divine language <note>Referring to Greek.</note> had already begun to grow cold, and scholars no longer knew how to "swim without a cork" <note>original: "sine cortice natare." A Latin idiom meaning to perform a task without assistance or support.</note>. Learned men disdained this laziness; hence Johannes Meursius, in the *Preface to his Cecropia*, giving the reason why he had not—contrary to his custom and personal inclination—added a translation to the cited passages of Greek authors, says: *“For this is the vice, or perhaps the misfortune, of this age: that while few are found to be students of the Greek language, among those few even more are found who love it rather than understand it.”* Consequently, Greek editions—no matter how beautiful—were looked upon with contempt almost everywhere if they lacked a Latin translation; they found few buyers and even fewer readers.

A shameful example of this is provided by that most noble Savile edition of Chrysostom <note>Sir Henry Savile (1549–1622) produced a magnificent eight-volume Greek edition of St. John Chrysostom at Eton in 1612. Despite its scholarly perfection, it was a financial failure compared to bilingual editions.</note>, than which the sun has never seen anything more beautiful or more perfect. It was produced at such immense expense, corrected with such fidelity and zeal, enlarged by such additions sought out from almost every corner of Europe, and illustrated with such learned prefaces, notes, and critiques. Finally, it was adorned throughout with such elegant beauty of type and purity of paper that it seems to reach the very pinnacle of perfection. Yet, because it was printed in Greek only, it is handled by only a few, being consigned to the cockroaches and moths <note>original: "ad blattas & tineas amandata," a common scholarly trope for neglected or unread books</note>; meanwhile, the Greek-Latin Parisian edition occupies a place in almost every library, both public and private.

Therefore, when learned men realized it was necessary to assist the laziness—or shall I say the ignorance?—of students in this matter, they devoted diligent effort to this task. Hieronymus Commelinus <note>A famous printer active in Heidelberg (c. 1550–1597).</note> conducted himself with great distinction in this duty; he gave us several of the Greek Fathers restored to their original luster, printed in Greek and Latin as correctly as possible. However, the care and diligence of the Parisian printers far surpassed all other industry and expense in printing the works of the Greek Fathers in both languages, both in the beauty of the characters and the perfection of the edition.

Among these, the **Morell** family leads the way, which produced Charles, Claude, Denis, and perhaps others. Indeed, they had begun to become famous in this field almost a full century earlier. For Guillaume Morel of Calais—according to de Thou*—was distinguished for his accurate skill in the Greek language and, around the year 1555, succeeded Adrien Turnèbe in the care of the Royal Greek Press. When he had adorned this position for nine whole years with the highest fidelity and diligence, he reached his final day on February 19, 1564. Closely following the Morells are the **Sonnius, Cramoisy, Vitré, and Petit** families, and others. To these we owe so many Greek Fathers speaking in both Greek and Latin, which appeared most elegantly under the guidance of Morel, Fronto Ducaeus, Petavius, Valesius, Cotelerius, and other outstanding men. The reader should only be warned of this: that the later Parisian editions fall far short of the earlier ones, at least in the brilliance of the type and paper. This can be seen at first glance in the works of Justin Martyr, Basil, Gregory of Nazianzus, Gregory of Nyssa, Chrysostom, and others.

V. *In discerning the editions of the Holy Fathers, it is of great importance to know who undertook the care of the edition; since the value of the edition itself depends entirely on the editor's fidelity, skill, and diligence.* Not everyone who acts as a "midwife" <note>The author uses the metaphor of midwifery (obstetricantur) for the act of editing and bringing a text to light.</note> in publishing the books of others is an Erasmus...

<summary>This page discusses the historical shift toward bilingual Greek-Latin editions of the Church Fathers, noting that even the most beautiful Greek-only editions (like Savile's) failed because scholars' Greek skills were declining. It highlights the prominence of the great Parisian printing dynasties and editors in making these texts accessible.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the history of scholarly editing, moving from the praise of great humanist editors and printers to a critique of those who prioritize profit over accuracy, and those who censor the Church Fathers to suit sectarian agendas.</meta>

### XIX PRELIMINARY DISCOURSES.

Erasmus, Rhenanus, Sylburg, Elmenhorst, Scaliger, Casaubon, Goldast, Sirmond, Rigault, Duceus, Huet, Garnier, and others <note>These represent the "princes" of humanist scholarship and patristic editing from the 16th and 17th centuries.</note>. These are men who, beyond all controversy, are to be venerated for their learning and integrity. Indeed, not even all printers can match or closely follow in the footsteps of the Manutii, the Frobens, the Amerbachs, the Stephenses, the Vascosans, the Oporini, the Commelini, and the Morels <note>A "who's who" of the great printing dynasties of Venice, Basel, Paris, and Geneva.</note>. Among printers today are found not a few who, gaping only for lucre and gain, thrust out into the literary world the works of the Ecclesiastical writers <note>The "Church Fathers," early influential Christian theologians.</note> without any careful revision, without any comparison to manuscript codices—much less a checking against earlier editions—and even (heaven help us!) without having been proofread or revised by their own eyes. These men are mere dullards and blocks of wood, who scarcely know how to tell a horse from a donkey. There are others who do indeed appoint supervisors and "Correctors" to their printing houses, claiming these men will watch over the books prepared for the press; but they are hired at a paltry price—ignorant and lazy monks who, as the saying goes, work with a light finger <note>original: "levi, quod aiunt, digito." Meaning they touch the work only superficially or carelessly.</note> and perform their tasks while yawning. Instead of removing a single flaw, they tend to increase the burden of errors in books immensely. I have heard men of no small reputation complaining most gravely of this regarding the presses of Lyon and several others.

But leaving the printers aside, let us turn to others. At all times there have been men of great erudition who strenuously applied their minds to bringing the works of the Holy Fathers to light; yet, led by a perverse zeal for defending the Papal faith and animated by an immoderate hatred for those they call "heretics," they attempted to drag the Fathers they edited into their own party—by the neck, as it were <note>original: "obtorto licet collo." A Latin idiom meaning to do something by force or against one's will.</note>—and by either adding or subtracting text, they forced the Fathers to speak in their favor. A case or two will make the matter clearer.

There exists under the name of Chrysostom <note>St. John Chrysostom (c. 347–407), a famous Greek preacher and Patriarch of Constantinople.</note> a work by a certainly not unlearned author titled *The Incomplete Work on Matthew*. In the forty-ninth homily, the author treats the unique abundance and authority of Holy Scripture against traditions, heresies, and schisms, stating:

> *That there is no other refuge for those Christians wishing to know the truth of the faith, no other possible proof of true Christianity, no other mark of the true Church of Christ in such great confusion of affairs; and therefore the Lord commanded that Christians wishing to receive the firmness of the true faith should flee to nothing except the Scriptures;* <note>original: "Nullum aliud esse Christianorum fidei veritatem cognoscere volentium refugium..." This passage was controversial because it seemed to support the Protestant doctrine of "Sola Scriptura" (Scripture alone).</note>

He pursues this point with many words, and those indeed most eloquent, and establishes it most firmly. Yet the Parisian editor, in the edition he prepared (printed by Odoenus Parvus at Paris, 1557, in octavo—to say nothing now of the Antwerp edition of 1545 and several others), entirely omits this whole passage, which is quite long. Even Bellarmine <note>Robert Bellarmine (1542–1621), a Cardinal and powerful defender of Catholic doctrine during the Counter-Reformation.</note> himself does not deny it,

<margin>* On the Word of God, book 4, chapter 11, page 199.</margin>

noting that this entire passage was removed from certain recently corrected codices on the grounds that it had been inserted by Arians <note>Arians were followers of Arius, who taught that Jesus Christ was not co-eternal with God the Father. In the 16th century, "Arianism" was often used as a catch-all for dangerous heresy.</note>. Furthermore, in the very entrance to the work, the editor warns that it has been diligently purged of the dregs of the Arians; and in the Preface, he claims that no stone was left unturned so that this work might henceforth be available more sincerely and (if possible) more brilliantly cleaned of all filth. But as for whether this passage we are discussing truly "smells entirely of Arian impiety," as they so often object—let fair judges of matters read it first, and then decide. See also what we have noted regarding this matter in the section on Chrysostom on page 268 of the following work.

In Homily XI, while discussing the sanctified vessels, the author says: *“in which the true body of Christ is not, but the mystery of his body is contained.”* <note>This phrase was controversial because it suggested a symbolic rather than literal presence of Christ in the Eucharist.</note> These words are sought in vain in that edition. Furthermore, in Homily XIX, because

<meta>This page continues the critique of textual corruption, shifting from the negligence of printers to the deliberate censorship and "purgation" of the Church Fathers by the Roman Catholic Church, specifically through the Office of the Inquisition and the Index of Forbidden Books.</meta>

<header>P R O L E G O M E N A.</header>

...which the Author had as *offering a sacrifice of bread and wine*, is changed into these words: *offering a sacrifice of the body and blood of Christ.* <note>The author is highlighting how editors changed early Christian texts to support the Catholic doctrine of the Real Presence in the Eucharist.</note> How often have our scholars <note>The "our" here refers to Protestant theologians and scholars.</note> reproached Pamelius of Antwerp and the Roman editors of Cyprian, charging that that most celebrated passage of Cyprian’s from the third chapter of the book *On the Unity of the Church* has been most foully corrupted by them. They argue that these words—*that Christ established one Chair; that primacy was given to Peter; and that he who deserts the Chair of Peter, upon which the Church was founded, strives against and resists the Church* <note>original: "Christum unam Cathedram constituisse; primatum Petro dato, Ecclesiæ renititur & resistit, qui Cathedram Petri super quam fundata est Ecclesia, deserit"</note>—were intruded into Cyprian’s very text against the evidence of almost all manuscript codices and all the oldest editions. Six hundred examples of this kind (to say nothing of the professional controversialists) are given to us by Cooke, Perkins, Rivet, Hottinger, Daillé, and above all others, our own Thomas James in his *Treatise on the Corruption of Scripture, the Fathers, and the Councils*, which he published in our native tongue <note>Thomas James (1573–1629) was the first librarian of the Bodleian Library; his work exposed what he saw as "pious frauds" in Roman Catholic editions of ancient texts.</note>. But more on these matters shortly.

VI. One must especially beware of editions of the Holy Fathers that are produced in those places where *the Office of the Holy Inquisition flourishes*. The reason is readily apparent: editions of that sort are revised precisely according to the rules of the *Expurgatory Indexes*. <term>Index Expurgatorius</term> <note>A list of books that were not banned entirely but were required to have specific passages deleted or altered before they could be read by Catholics.</note> For they are not content to swoop down upon the writings of more recent authors, but exercise—shall I call it authority or tyranny?—over the venerable Fathers themselves. Sixtus Senensis <note>A Jewish convert to Catholicism and a noted theologian.</note> candidly confessed this, and for that reason extols Pope Pius V with wonderful praises:

<margin>* In the Dedicatory Epistle prefixed to the "Library of the Saints."</margin>

> *First, you commanded that books of wicked doctrine be sought out throughout the whole Christian world and publicly burned. Next, you took care that all the writings of Catholic authors, and especially those of the ancient Fathers, should be expurgated and cleansed—writings which had been contaminated by the dregs and infected by the poisons of the heretics of our age. Finally, you ordered a Catalogue or Index to be published of all authors and books of heretical name, who were to be barred from the Ecclesiastical Library and cast away by all Christian readers.*

So that this may be a little clearer, I will briefly explain a matter perhaps not sufficiently known to everyone. Hardly had the reformation of religion begun by Luther raised its head, when Leo X forbade, under heavy penalty, anyone to print any book unless it was first diligently examined by the Master of the Sacred Palace, the Bishop of the diocese, or the Inquisitor of heretical depravity. Following this example, Bishops and Inquisitors issued various *Indexes* of pestilential and damnable books as the times required. And to defend what had been done, Gabriel Putherbeus wrote *Theotinum, or, On the Removal and Expunging of Bad Books*, in three books, published at Paris in 1549. But as the number of heretical writers grew daily, and as weapons were drawn from every side of the armory of Ecclesiastical Antiquity, the Council of Trent delegated several distinguished Prelates and selected Theologians from almost every nation to compile an Index of forbidden books. When they had made considerable progress in this business, the Council decided to refer the entire matter to the Apostolic See. Therefore, in the year 1564, Pope Pius IV revised the Index recently completed by the Roman Inquisitors and approved by the Fathers of Trent, and published it by his own authority with certain rules added. Later, Pius V, Gregory XIII, and Sixtus V returned this material to the forge, refining it and increasing it with several new additions. Finally, in the year 1595, Clement VIII, having chosen a college of seven Cardinals and other pious and learned...
