The previous page ended while discussing the work of Thomas James and others who exposed the "purgation" or corruption of ancient texts by Roman Catholic authorities. This page picks up with the formalization of these practices through the Council of Trent and the creation of various "Expurgatory Indices" across Europe.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA

...with learned men <note>The Latin text here begins mid-word, likely "eruditis viris," meaning "learned men."</note> having been summoned to the Council, he put the finishing touches on the work, adding not only rules but also instructions. In the chapter on the *Correction of Books*, Section 1, it was decreed that Bishops and Inquisitors <term>Inquisitors: officials of the Catholic Church tasked with identifying and suppressing heresy</term> should jointly have the power to purge any books according to the prescription of this Index, even in places usually exempt from such oversight. It was also mandated that this purging of books should only be entrusted to men distinguished for their learning and piety. In Section 2, among other things, the following were decreed to be purged by the Correctors: namely, propositions that are heretical, erroneous, savoring of heresy, scandalous, offensive to pious ears, or rash; and those which introduce any novelty against the rites and ceremonies of the Sacraments, or against the received use and custom of the **Roman Church**. Likewise, doubtful and ambiguous words which could lead the minds of readers away from the right and Catholic sense toward wicked opinions were to be removed.

And indeed, by now, not only Prohibitory Indices <note>The *Index Librorum Prohibitorum* listed books banned entirely.</note> but also Expurgatory Indices <note>An *Index Expurgatorius* listed specific passages to be deleted or altered in otherwise permitted books.</note> had become prevalent everywhere. The first to lead the way, as far as I know, was the *Belgian Index*, produced by the authority of *Philip*, King of the Spains, and published with a preface by *Arias Montanus* at *Antwerp, 1571, in quarto*. This was reprinted more than once with new prefaces added by *Junius*, *Pappus*, and others. The *Portuguese Index* was published by *Jorge de Almeida*, *Archbishop of Lisbon*, at *Lisbon, 1581, in quarto*. The *Spanish Index* was issued by *Gaspar Quiroga, Cardinal and Archbishop of Toledo and Inquisitor General of Spain*, at *Madrid, 1584, in quarto*. The *Neapolitan Index* was produced by *Gregorio Capuccino* under the title of the *Ecclesiastical Manual*, at *Venice, 1588, in octavo*. Finally, the *Roman Index* was published by *Giovanni Maria Brasichellano, Master of the Sacred Palace*, at *Rome, 1607, in octavo*—to say nothing of those more recent than these. 

The first architects of such an execrable institution, so injurious toward God and men, toward the Holy Fathers, and toward truth itself, could not help but feel some shame. Hence, at first, the business was conducted under a veil and with witnesses removed, known to few, and confined within the domestic walls of the Censors. For this reason, in King *Philip's* diploma <note>A "diploma" here refers to an official state charter or decree.</note> prefixed to the Belgian Index, it was cautioned that this Expurgatory Index should not be distributed, but given only to the censors; they were to keep the Expurgatory Index by them privately, with no one else knowing, and they were neither to communicate it to others nor give a copy of it to anyone. Instead, they were to take the utmost care only to search out, expunge, and restore the aforementioned passages. 

But after a copy of this Index fell into the hands of *Franciscus Junius* <note>Franciscus Junius (1545–1602) was a Reformed theologian who famously published the "secret" Belgian Index to expose the extent of Catholic censorship.</note>, the horrible secret leaked out to the public. And now, with what shield, with what apology could they defend themselves? Finally, the Jesuit *Jacob Gretser* entered the arena, publishing two books at *Ingolstadt, 1603, in quarto* concerning the right and custom of prohibiting, purging, and abolishing heretical and noxious books; to these, he added two parts by way of a supplement the following year. In all of these, with a certainly remarkable display of learning, but with a spirit and faith plainly Jesuitical—that is, crafty and deceitful—he pleads his cause.

<margin>Book 1, Chapter 1, Page 2.</margin>
He confesses that the *Protestants* always have this Index on their lips and cast it in the teeth of *Catholics* as a supreme crime; nevertheless, he defends it as worthy of praise, and its authors and executors as having deserved excellently of humankind. 

<margin>† Book 2, Chapter 10, Page 329.</margin>
And elsewhere †, he says: *This right is and always has been in the hands of the Church: that she may anxiously remove those things which bring harm to the flock; and if it is lawful to proscribe an entire book, it will also be lawful to proscribe a part of a book, whether large or small; by cutting it out, deleting it, obliterating it, or simply omitting it, and this for the benefit of the reader.* <note>original: "Jus hoc semper & est, & fuit, penes Ecclesiam, ut quæ gregi detrimentum adferunt, sollicitè amoliatur..."</note> Yet, in order to more plausibly answer the most serious objection of all...

<meta>catchword: amoliretur (remove/dispel)</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA

...he would get rid of, he distinctly denies either that the Fathers <note>The "Church Fathers" are the influential early Christian theologians whose writings established the foundations of Christian doctrine.</note> were purged by him, or that the authors of the Indices intended this. 
<margin>

</margin>
*He says ||, they lie impudently, as often as they affirm that anything in the books of the ancient Fathers is deleted by us, or prescribed to be deleted by the Expurgatory Index: We delete nothing in the Fathers, we obliterate nothing.* — 
<margin>
* Book 2, ch. 7, p. 298.
</margin>
* Name even one book of one of the aforementioned Fathers which is interdicted in any Prohibitory or Expurgatory Index. Not Augustine, not Cyprian, not Chrysostom, not Cyril, etc. 
<margin>
† Ibid. ch. 10, p. 324.
</margin>
— — † For neither the Belgian, nor any other Index was written for that purpose, to serve in cleansing and correcting the monuments <note>original: "monumentis," referring here to the surviving written works</note> of the Fathers; as we also testified above, and the matter is clear enough from the index itself. Is that so, good Gretser? <note>Jakob Gretser (1562–1625) was a Jesuit scholar and a fierce defender of Catholic practices against Protestant critics.</note> Come then, let us test for a little while whether it be so:

<margin>
|| Vol. 5, p. 87. Paris Edition, Morel.
</margin>
Chrysostom || in his fourth Homily on Lazarus, has these words: *“For you do not tell your sins to a man so that he may reproach you, nor do you confess to a fellow servant so that he may expose you; rather, you show your wounds to the Master, the Guardian, the Friend of mankind, the Physician.”* <note>original Greek: "μὴ γὰρ ἀνθρώπῳ λέγεις... τῷ ἰατρῷ τὰ τραύματα ἐπιδείκνυεις"</note> *For you do not tell your sins to a man, that he may bring shame upon you; nor do you confess to a fellow-servant, that he may make it public, but rather you show your wounds to him who is the Lord, to him who cares for you, to him who is kind, to him who is the Physician.* And yet, from the Index of Chrysostom’s works, the Spanish Index orders these words to be expunged. *“Let these words be deleted,” it says, “Sins are to be confessed to God, not to man.”*

<margin>
* Sermon on Pentecost, vol. 6, p. 233.
</margin>
* *“He did not say upon Peter,”* says the same Chrysostom, *“for he built his Church not upon a man, but upon faith—what then is ‘upon the rock’? Upon the confession, upon the words of piety.”* <note>original Greek: "Οὐκ εἶπεν ἐπὶ τῷ Πέτρῳ... ἐπὶ τοῖς ῥήμασιν"</note> *Upon this rock, he did not say upon Peter, for not upon a man, but upon faith he built his Church: what is "upon the rock," this is upon the confession, upon the sermons of piety.* The Expurgatory Index for this passage in the Index of Chrysostom states: *“Let these words be deleted: The Church is built not upon a man, but upon faith.”*

<margin>
† Against Adimantus, ch. 12, vol. 6, col. 187.
</margin>
Augustine † concerning the Eucharist says explicitly, *“For the Lord did not hesitate to say, ‘This is my body,’ when he was giving a sign of his body.”* And yet from the Index of Augustine, the curators of the Sandoval Index <note>Refers to the Spanish Index published under Cardinal Bernardo de Sandoval y Rojas in 1612.</note> want these words deleted: *“Christ gave a sign of his body.”*

<margin>
* Against the Arians, Oration 3, p. 204.
</margin>
Athanasius * on divine worship: *“For a creature does not worship creatures, but a servant worships a master, and a creature worships God—it is worthy of God alone to be worshipped.”* <note>original Greek: "κτίσματα γὰρ κτίσμασι οὐ προσκυνεῖ... τὸ προσκυνεῖσθαι"</note> *A creature does not adore a creature, but a servant a lord, and a creature God:—therefore it belongs to God alone to be adored.* And yet from his index the same Spanish Index removes these words: *“To be adored belongs to God alone.”*

<margin>
|| Heresies 69, no. 36, p. 759.
</margin>
Epiphanius || says almost the same things: *“The holy Church of God does not worship a creature.”* <note>original Greek: "ἡ ἁγία τοῦ Θεοῦ Ἐκκλησία οὐ κτίσμα προσκυνεῖ"</note> Nevertheless, from the annotations, the good Censors remove these words from their place: *“A creature is not to be adored.”*

<margin>
* Canon 27, Col. 521.
</margin>
Hilary on Matthew *: *“No one is to be helped by the works and merits of others, because it is necessary for each one to buy oil for his own lamp.”* They order these things to be deleted from the Index with these words: *“No one is helped by the merits and works of others.”* To these and six hundred similar places brought against us, Gretser promptly applies this "wise medicine": that the Indices <note>The alphabetical tables or reference guides at the end of books.</note> usually attached to the Works of the Fathers are only the commentaries of more recent men. A wretched excuse, by Hercules! For were not these Indices of the Fathers mostly composed by the most learned Catholic men? Is not the sense the same in both places? Are not the same words found everywhere? When he realized that he would by no means escape through this burrow, he fled to another no less weak; namely, according to the old saying: *“Often the same thing, when said by the same people, is not the same.”* The sayings of the Fathers, when they are used by heretics, can be the same if you look at the words, but far...

# PROLEGOMENA.

far different if one considers the sense and the mind. As if there were truly so vast a difference that what is considered orthodox in the main text becomes heretical when placed in an Index! When the very same sentence—with no false sense attached, distorted by no gloss—is found in both places in the same words, and almost the same number of syllables! What will you do with this sort of men? *By what knot can you hold them?* <note>original: "quo teneas nodo?" — a reference to Horace, Epistles 1.1.90, suggesting the difficulty of pinning down an elusive opponent.</note> I am truly sorry for those who are forced to blurt out things that are not true, nor perhaps what they themselves feel, but what the necessity of defending a wicked cause is accustomed to extort.

But setting the Indices aside, let us come to the Fathers themselves; they did not spare the authentic text of these men, nor did they withhold their violent hands from their very own words. Look at the Spanish Index, page 62; you will see the lords Inquisitors passing this sentence upon Cyril of Alexandria: <note>Cyril of Alexandria (c. 376–444) was a Patriarch and major theologian; the author is highlighting how even his venerable status did not protect his works from the censors.</note> *from the Commentaries of St. Cyril on Isaiah, Lawrence Humphrey being the translator, from chapter 1 let those words be deleted from the text:* "The grace of Faith, however, with those who are greatly defiled, etc." *In chapter 51, at the end, let those words be deleted from the text: "But where they do not have the fear of God in themselves, nor receive Jesus as an inhabitant through faith; for he dwells in our Hearts through faith, as it is written."* To these things Gretser <margin>* Book 2, ch. 7, p. 305.</margin> says: *Glosses added to Cyril by heretics, the notes of idle men, and interpretations are to be expunged and struck out.* Indeed, with his preceding words, as if he had already carried off the victory, he sings a Triumph. *But you,* he says to Junius, <note>Francis Junius (1545–1602), a Reformed theologian who wrote against the Indices.</note> *if you are not a liar, show me any expurgatory index that orders anything to be obliterated which pertains to the actual and genuine context of Cyril.* Are these things, then, not Cyril's? Let the Latin be compared, I pray, with the Greek. You will see them to be translated so accurately and faithfully that in almost all the editions of the papists, even in that newest Greek-Latin one (*Paris, 1638*), they are found today in the very same words. See what the Index notes on page 74 concerning the same man’s book *Against the Anthropomorphites*; <note>A work refuting the belief that God possesses a human physical form.</note> what it says of Eucherius’s *Commentaries on Genesis*; what the Roman Index says of Gelasius’s book *On the Two Natures in Christ*, page 146. I pass over infinite examples met with everywhere. Indeed, our own [Thomas] James <note>Thomas James (c. 1573–1629), the first librarian of the Bodleian Library, who wrote extensively on the "corruption" of ancient texts by Roman censors.</note> has not only dealt with this through his entire book on the *Corruption of the Fathers*, but has also appended to Part IV a catalog of 323 Ecclesiastical writers—partly ancient, partly recent—whose books are either prohibited by the expurgatory indices, ordered to be purged, or finally marked with an unfair censure. I do not mention here the entire books of Anthony the Great, Fortunatus, Bertram, and many others prohibited by the Index. On the occasion of Bertram's booklet, according to the judgment of the University of Douai, the Belgian Censors ingenuously confess: *that they tolerate many errors in the ancient Catholic writers, they extenuate them, they excuse them, they often deny them by a contrived commentary, and they affix a convenient sense to them when they are opposed in disputes or conflicts with adversaries.* Who will not cry out, *Great is the truth, and it will prevail*? <note>original: "Magna est veritas, & prævalebit." — a popular Latin proverb derived from the apocryphal book of 1 Esdras.</note> It remains finally for us to see whether this privilege of purging extends also to the manuscript codes (MSS)? And indeed it can be gathered so from <margin>* Select Library, book 1, ch. 48, p. 38.</margin> * Possevino; <note>Antonio Possevino (1533–1611), a Jesuit diplomat and bibliographer.</note> for while he warns that the greatest care must be taken lest Manuscript Books which are not permitted by the Church be open to everyone, he presently adds: *For the purging pertains to those also.* And on this occasion, let me be allowed to note what our James once reported: that in the Vatican Library there are not a few artists who daily...

# PROLEGOMENA.

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence: "[...every] day; whose task it was to transcribe the Acts of the Councils and the works of the ancient Fathers, doing so with letters that imitated the oldest manuscripts as closely as possible. With what intent this was done, they themselves know; certainly, he testifies that he heard of the matter from the mouth of a certain most noble Englishman, who had surveyed these things with his own eyes in the Vatican Library and voluntarily offered his own oath to confirm his reliability. But whatever becomes of the manuscript codices, it is most certain that a printing house <term>Typographia<note>The Typographia Vaticana, or Vatican Press, was established by Pope Sixtus V in 1587 specifically to publish authoritative editions of the Church Fathers and the Bible.</note></term> was long ago established in the Vatican Palace, intended as a most fortified citadel not only for defending orthodox truth but for propagating it.

<margin>* See the Preface to Gregory the Great, volumes 1 and 5, Rome, 1588.</margin>
And from this press (for so they promised us *) would come the most corrected monuments of the Holy Fathers, restored to their pristine splendor. From this printing house, they have given us the works of Cyprian, Ambrose, and Gregory the Great, to say nothing of others. In these works, the editors themselves do not deny that many things have been added, many cut away, and everything "corrected," purged, and restored. With how much negligence, or rather fraud, they treated Cyprian is noted on almost every page by Pamelius <note>Jacques de Pamele (1536–1587) was a Flemish theologian and editor of the Fathers; even he, a Catholic, found the editing work of others problematic.</note> himself. In the works of Ambrose, they have miserably disturbed the order of the letters and sermons; they have changed the inscriptions and titles; they have added many tracts, letters, and sermons never before printed, without even naming the manuscript codices from which they were drawn. In the works of Gregory the Great, to note nothing else, they inserted more than 140 letters—beyond the expositions on the books of *Kings and the Psalms* which were mentioned by none of the ancients and are not sufficiently supported by any manuscripts. These letters are missing from all our codices, according to the testimony of James <note>Thomas James (1573–1629) was the first librarian of the Bodleian and a fierce critic of Roman Catholic "corruption" of ancient texts.</note>, who deals copiously with the editions of Gregory and their collation with ancient manuscript copies in his *Vindiciae Gregorianae, Geneva 1625, quarto.* And lest I be infinite, the works of Augustine were published at *Venice, 1584, in 10 volumes, quarto.* What good the editors provided in this edition, they themselves frankly teach us at the very front of the work. *In which,* they say, *besides the restoration of many places according to the collation of older copies, we have taken care to remove all those things which could infect the minds of the faithful with heretical pravity <note>original: "heretica pravitate" — a standard term used by the Inquisition for the "wickedness" of heresy.</note>, or deviate from the Catholic and orthodox faith.* Consequently, these and similar editions should be handled by students not without the greatest caution.

## SECTION VI.

### Introductory books for the study of Ecclesiastical Antiquity.

I do not claim so much for myself here as to prescribe laws and methods for the study of Church matters to others. Far be it from me; I know how to measure myself by my own small standard. I shall merely play the part of a statue of Mercury <note>In antiquity, statues of Mercury (Herms) often served as signposts or road markers.</note>, with my finger pointing toward the best guides of the way, who can show the road and fortify it for younger students striving toward ancient times. These guides are either ancient or more recent; from both groups, which exist in huge numbers everywhere, I will provide only a few, and those the more select. From the ancients, I shall bring forward only two men, but of great name: *Jerome* among the Latins, and *Photius* among the Greeks. Jerome...

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of editorial practices at the Vatican Press, transitioning into a survey of the most significant historical guides and writers of the Church. The author is moving from a critique of modern editing to a bibliography of reliable (and some less reliable) historical sources.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA.

<meta>The text begins mid-sentence: "[...Jerome, who was] most fully equipped with the apparatus of all more refined as well as more recondite literature, held the primary place in sacred and Ecclesiastical matters."</meta>

He treats writings and Ecclesiastical writers throughout his work, both in his *Letters*—specifically in his *Letter to Magnus the Orator*—and in his prefaces to his *Commentaries on Holy Scripture*. However, he deals with them explicitly in his book *On Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *de Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis*, also known as *De Viris Illustribus*</note>, the first part of which he took from the *History* of Eusebius. The latter part, which covers the fourth century up to the year 392, he added by his own effort <term>proprio Marte<note>A Latin idiom meaning "by one's own Mars," or by one's own personal strength and independent labor.</note></term> and almost entirely from his own knowledge. In this work, he reviewed as many writers as he could find, listed their writings, and frequently added an excellent critical judgment of them. 

Photius—than whom Greece, that fertile mother of geniuses, perhaps never produced a more learned man—is remarkably commended by a mind born for everything: solid judgment, the highest keenness, infinite reading, and incredible diligence. He had performed nearly all the duties of the state and had investigated all the monuments of the Church. In the single *Library* <note>The *Bibliotheca* or *Myriobiblon*, a massive 9th-century compilation of summaries of 279 books Photius had read.</note> which we have today, he presents nearly 280 writers, the majority of them Ecclesiastical, diligently read, reviewed, and excerpted by himself. Regarding their books, arguments, style, reliability, and authority, he delivered an accurate judgment, if ever anyone did. 

To these should be subjoined—if not rather placed first—the founders of Ecclesiastical History: **Eusebius**, covering the period from the birth of Christ to the year 325, continued by **Rufinus** to the year 395; and those who followed Eusebius: **Socrates**, **Sozomen**, and **Theodoret**, covering the period from the rise of Arius until approximately the year 439. These were followed by **Philostorgius**, **Evagrius**, and **Theodorus Lector**. There is also the *Tripartite History* compiled from Sozomen, Socrates, and Theodoret, translated into Latin by Epiphanius the Scholar and reduced into a compendium by **Cassiodorus**. Following these at a long, but nearest, interval is **Nicephorus Callistus**, covering from the birth of Christ to the year 625; although he teems everywhere with fables and absurdities, he nevertheless not infrequently wove into his *Ecclesiastical History* some patches <note>original: *pannos*, a reference to "purple patches" or fragments of lost older texts</note> from a better age that are not to be regretted.

I establish two classes of more recent guides <term>ὁδηγῶν<note>Greek: *hodēgōn*, meaning "guides" or "leaders."</note></term>: General and Special. To the General Class are referred: 
1. *Ecclesiastical Historians*, who place before our eyes the state of the Christian Church in every century. In this category, the first place is earned by the **Magdeburg Centuriators**, who with truly incredible diligence gathered an immense mass of Ecclesiastical matters for every century and distributed them into specific chapters in a not ungraceful order. Many defects and more errors ought to be forgiven them, for they were the first to "break the ice" in this area and set out upon a path hitherto untrodden and unattempted. **Lucas Osiander** of Wittenberg reduced the History of the Centuriators into an epitome at the beginning of this century. 
The most illustrious parent of the *Ecclesiastical Annals*, **Cardinal Baronius**, wrote twelve massive volumes of Ecclesiastical History with a heart of adamant and labor almost beyond human strength. It was a truly immense work of thirty years, which was only begun after he had repeated the history of the Church seven times in the Oratory <note>The Oratory of Saint Philip Neri in Rome, where Baronius preached daily for decades.</note>—specifically, every five years. It was written entirely by his own hand, with no assistance from any secretaries (as the author of his *Life* * assures us); 
<margin>Girolamo Barnabeo, *On the Life of Baronius*, book 1, chapter 19, page 45.</margin>
and this was done amidst the daily duties of parish care, amidst frequent hearing of confessions, preaching, and the domestic duties of the congregation

...on other days; it was their task to copy out the Acts of the Councils and the works of the ancient Fathers, and to do so using handwriting that imitated the most ancient manuscripts as much as possible. With what intent this was done, they themselves know; certainly, a certain most noble Englishman <note>The author likely refers to a Protestant scholar or traveler who reported on the secretive practices of the Vatican scriptorium.</note> testifies that he heard the matter himself and viewed these things with his own eyes in the Vatican Library, and he spontaneously offered his oath to confirm his reliability. But whatever may become of the handwritten manuscripts, it is most certain that a printing press was long ago established in the Vatican Palace as a most fortified citadel, not only for defending orthodox truth but for propagating it.

<margin>* See the Preface to Gregory the Great, volumes 1 and 5. Rome, 1588.</margin>
And from this press (so they promise us *) would come the most "corrected" monuments of the Holy Fathers, restored to their former splendor. From this press, they have given us the works of Cyprian, Ambrose, and Gregory the Great, to say nothing of others. In these works, the editors themselves do not deny that many things have been added, many things cut away, and everything "corrected," purged, and restored. Almost every page of their edition of Cyprian is marked by negligence, or rather fraud, as noted by Pamelius <note>Jacques de Pamèle (1536–1587), a Flemish theologian and editor.</note> himself. In the works of Ambrose, they have wretchedly disturbed the order of the Letters and Sermons; they changed the inscriptions and titles; and they added many treatises, letters, and sermons never before printed, without even naming the manuscripts from which they took them. In the works of Gregory the Great—to note nothing else besides the commentaries on the books of *Kings and the Psalms, which were mentioned by none of the ancients and are not sufficiently supported by any manuscripts*—they inserted more than 140 letters. These are missing from all our manuscripts, according to the testimony of James <note>Thomas James (1573–1629), the first librarian of the Bodleian, who famously cataloged "corruptions" in Roman Catholic editions of the Fathers.</note>, who deals copiously with the editions of Gregory and their comparison with ancient handwritten copies in his *Gregorian Vindications, Geneva 1625, quarto*. And lest I be infinite, the works of Augustine were published at *Venice, 1584, in 10 volumes, quarto*. What "good" the editors achieved in this edition, they frankly teach us right at the front of the work:

> *In which, besides the restoration of many passages according to the comparison of older copies, we have taken care, they say, to remove all those things which could infect the minds of the faithful with heretical depravity, or deviate from the Catholic and orthodox faith.*

Thus, such editions, and others like them, should be handled by the hands of students only with the greatest caution.

# SECTION VI.

## Books serving as guides to the study of Ecclesiastical Antiquity.

I do not claim so much for myself here that I would prescribe laws and methods for the study of Church matters to others. Far be it; I know how to measure myself by my own small scale. I shall merely perform the role of a statue of Mercury <term>Mercurialis statuæ<note>Statues of Mercury (Hermes) were used in antiquity as road signs or milestones to guide travelers.</note></term>, with my finger pointing toward the best leaders of the way, who can show and clear the path for younger students striving toward ancient times. These guides are either ancient or more recent; from both groups, which exist everywhere in vast numbers, I will provide only a few, and those the most select. From the ancients, I will bring forward only two men, but of great name: *Jerome among the Latins, and Photius among the Greeks*. Jerome...

->### PROLEGOMENA.<-

...[Jerome], highly learned in both classical and profound literature, held first place in sacred and ecclesiastical matters. He deals with ecclesiastical writings and writers throughout his works, specifically in the *Letter to Magnus the Orator*, and in the prefaces to his *Commentaries on Holy Scripture*; but he treats them as his main subject in the book *On Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *De Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis*, also known as *De Viris Illustribus*</note>. The first part of this work he drew from Eusebius’s *History*, while the latter part—which covers the fourth century up to the year 392—he added by his own effort and almost entirely from his own knowledge. In this work, he reviewed as many writers as he could find, listed their writings, and frequently added an excellent critical judgment of them.

Photius—a man more learned than whom the fertile land of Greece has perhaps never produced—is outstandingly recommended by a genius born for all things: solid judgment, the highest keenness, infinite reading, and incredible diligence. He had held almost all the offices of the state and had thoroughly investigated all the monuments of the Church. In his single work that we have today, the *Bibliotheca* <note>original: "Library"</note>, he presents nearly 280 writers, the greater part of them ecclesiastical, which he diligently read, reviewed, and excerpted; regarding their books, arguments, style, reliability, and authority, he delivered a judgment as accurate as anyone ever has.

To these should be joined—if not rather preceded—the founders of Ecclesiastical History: Eusebius, covering the period from the birth of Christ to the year 325; continued by Rufinus to the year 395; and those who followed Eusebius: Socrates, Sozomen, and Theodoret, from the rise of Arius <note>Arius (c. 250–336), whose teachings led to the Arian controversy and the Council of Nicaea.</note> up to approximately the year 439. These were followed by Philostorgius, Evagrius, and Theodorus Lector. There is also the *Tripartite History*, translated into Latin from Sozomen, Socrates, and Theodoret by Epiphanius Scholasticus and condensed into a compendium by Cassiodorus. At a long interval after these, but next in line, comes Nicephorus Callistus, covering the period from the birth of Christ up to the year 725; although he abounds everywhere in fables and absurdities, he nevertheless frequently wove into his *Ecclesiastical History* valuable fragments <note>original: *pannos*, literally "patches" or "rags"</note> of better ages.

<margin>Girolamo Barnabeo, *On the Life of Baronius*, Book I, chapter 19, page 45.</margin>
Of the more recent guides <note>original: ὁδηγῶν (*odēgōn*)</note>, I establish two classes: the General and the Special. To the General Class belong, first, the *Ecclesiastical Historians*, who set before our eyes the state of the Christian Church in every century. Among these, the first place is earned by the Magdeburg Centuriators <note>A group of 16th-century Protestant scholars who organized history into "centuries" to argue against the papacy.</note>, who with truly incredible diligence gathered an immense mass of ecclesiastical material for every century and distributed it into specific chapters in a not-inelegant order; many defects and several errors in their work ought to be forgiven, for they were the first to "break the ice" in this area and followed a path that had until then been neither worn nor attempted. Lucas Osiander of Wittenberg reduced the history of the Centuriators into an epitome <note>A summary or condensed version.</note> at the beginning of this century.

The most illustrious father of the *Ecclesiastical Annals*, Cardinal Baronius, wrote twelve massive volumes of Ecclesiastical History with an iron heart and labor almost surpassing human strength. It was a truly immense work of thirty years, which was only begun after he had repeated the ecclesiastical history seven times in the Oratory <note>The Oratory of Saint Philip Neri in Rome, where Baronius preached daily sermons on church history for decades.</note>, specifically every five years. The entire work was written by his own hand, with no help from any amanuenses <note>Secretaries or copyists.</note> (as the author of his *Life* assures us); and this was done amidst the daily duties of parish care, frequent hearing of confessions, preaching, and the domestic [duties] of the congregation...

<sig>e</sig>
<meta>catchword: gationis</meta>

It highlights the transition from Patristic chronicles to systematic, century-by-century historical analysis.</summary>

page xxvi

->### PROLEGOMENA.<-

...of his mission, his duties of overseeing these matters were brought to completion <note>original: *ad umbelicum perductum*, literally "led to the navel," an idiom for finishing a scroll or task</note>—not to mention his other occupations and his other writings. In this work, he certainly served the study of Ecclesiastical Antiquity with distinction (I myself freely acknowledge that I have profited not a little from him). However, he would have carried off far greater glory if he had not directed all his sweat, all the sinews of his talent and erudition, toward this single goal: to expand the boundaries of the Papal Majesty. He sought to exalt and establish the Pope's dignity and authority not only above ecclesiastical laws, but even above Royal and Imperial rights and every towering height of power—and this, not infrequently, by using writers who were apocryphal, forged, and altogether foolish and futile. Regarding this work when it was only just beginning, Pierre Pithou <note>original: *Petrus Pithœus*, a French jurist</note> once rightly noted (as reported by Casaubon) that *they should be called not Ecclesiastical Annals, but rather Concerning the Power of the Pope.* Certainly, Baronius had nothing else in mind than to fight against the "innovators" <note>A term often used by Catholic writers for the Protestant Reformers</note> of his time—that is, the Magdeburg Centuriators—*on behalf of the antiquity of sacred traditions and for the preservation of the power of the Roman Catholic Church.* Neither he himself <margin>* Dedicatory letter to Sixtus V, prefixed to the volume.</margin>, nor Girolamo Barnabeo <margin>† Life of Baronius, ibid., chapters 18, 19.</margin>, deny this.

Baronius's *Annals*, from the year 1198 where they left off, were continued by Abraham Bzovius, Odoricus Rainaldus, and finally in France by Henri Sponde <note>original: *Henricus Spondanus*</note>. Although Sponde is smaller in bulk than the others, he far surpasses them in reliability, diligence, and more accurate judgment. To these should be added Isaac Casaubon’s *Exercises on Sacred and Ecclesiastical Matters against Baronius* (London, 1614, folio); Richard Montagu’s *Selected Ecclesiastical Exercises* (London, 1622, folio), which were later enlarged and revised under the title *Preparation for Ecclesiastical Origins* (Oxford, 1635, folio); the same author's *First and Second Parts of Commentaries on Ecclesiastical Origins* (London, 1636, 1640, folio); likewise his *Discourse against Bulinger*. I shall pass over the writings of Rosweyde, Cappel, Eudaemon-Joannis, Prideaux, Bulenger, and others relating to this subject. Among the compendiums of ecclesiastical history, the following stand out: Gabriel Bisciola’s *Epitome of the Ten Volumes of Baronius's Annals* (Lyon and Venice, 1612, 2 volumes, quarto); Johann Micraelius’s *Collection of Church Histories*, with the continuation by Daniel Hartnaccius (Leipzig, 1679, quarto); Hieronymus Kromayer’s *Sixteen Centuries of Ecclesiastical History* (Leipzig, 1666, quarto); J. H. Hottinger’s *Ecclesiastical History of the New Testament*, published at Zurich in 9 volumes from 1651 until 1667, when he died (octavo); and Friedrich Spanheim’s *Introduction to Sacred Christian Chronology and History*, volume 1 (Leiden, 1683), volume 2 (ibid., 1687, quarto); we expect the remaining volumes in due time.

2. ***Editors of the Councils***: Jacques Merlin, 2 volumes (Paris, 1535, octavo); Pierre Crabbe, 2 volumes (Cologne, 1538, 1551, folio); Bartolomé Carranza *in the Summary of the Councils* (Paris, 1624, 12mo; Douai, 1659, octavo); Laurentius Surius, 4 volumes (Cologne, 1627, folio); Domenico Nicolini, 5 volumes (Venice, 1585, folio); *The General Councils*, 4 volumes (Rome, 1608, folio); Severin Binius, 4 volumes (Cologne, 1606, 1618), 10 volumes (Paris, 1636, folio); Francesco Longo a Coriolano *in the Summary of All Councils* with annotations (Antwerp, 1623, folio); Philippe Labbe and Gabriel Cossart (passing over the Royal Louvre Edition for now), together with a double apparatus (Paris, 1672, 18 volumes, folio); and *The Pandects of Canons of the Apostles and Councils received by the Greek Church*, in Greek and Latin with the annotations of William Beveridge (Oxford, 1672, 2 volumes).

->### PROLEGOMENA xxvii.<-

<note>original: e 2; signature mark for the printer</note>

ii. Two volumes of Friedrich Ruess and Hartmann’s *Ecclesiastical History of the Councils* <note>original: *Historia Conciliorum Ecclesiastica*</note> up to the year 1661, published at Nuremberg, 1675, in four quarto volumes. *The British Councils* <note>original: *Concilia Britannica*</note> by Henry Spelman, in two volumes, London, 1639 and 1664. *The Spanish Councils* <note>original: *Hispanica*</note> by Garcia de Loaisa, Madrid, 1595. *The French Councils* <note>original: *Gallica*</note> by Jacques Sirmond, in three volumes, 1629, folio. *The Councils of Narbonne* <note>original: *Narbonensia*</note> by Étienne Baluze, Paris, 1678, octavo. To these may also be added Philippe Labbe’s *Synopsis of the Councils*, Paris, 1661, quarto, and those works placed at the beginning of the volumes of the Councils; David Chytraeus’s *Catalog of Councils* added to his *Theological Nomenclature* <note>original: *Onomastico*</note>, and added by Johann Pappus to the end of the *Synodicon*, Strasbourg, 1601, quarto; and Caspar Heunisch’s *Chronology of the Six Ecumenical Councils*, Rothenburg, 1677.

3. Regarding those who have written on the *creeds* <term>symbols; the formal statements of Christian belief, such as the Apostles' Creed</term> of the ancient Church: Gerard Joan Vossius’s *Dissertations on the Three Creeds*, Amsterdam, 1642 and 1662, quarto. James Ussher <note>Ussher was the Archbishop of Armagh, famous for his biblical chronology</note> of Armagh’s *Discourse on the Ancient Apostles' Creed of the Roman Church and Other Formulas of Faith*, London, 1647, and Oxford, 1660, quarto. Aegidius Strauch’s *History of the Apostles' Creed*, Wittenberg, 1668, quarto.

4. *On the Roman Pontiffs* <note>The Popes</note>: in addition to the ancient writers—Anastasius, Liutprand, etc.—there is Bartolomeo Platina, Cologne, 1610, quarto, and frequently elsewhere. Joannes Stella’s *On the Lives of the Supreme Pontiffs*, Basel, 1507. Onuphrio Panvinio in his *Epitome of the Roman Pontiffs*, Venice, 1557, folio, and Cologne, 1624, quarto. Papirius Masson, a very accurate and learned writer, *On the Lives of the Roman Pontiffs*, Paris, 1586, quarto. Daniel Papebroch’s *Chronological-Historical Attempt at a Catalog of the Roman Pontiffs*, adorned with most elegant figures <note>illustrations</note>, published both separately and before Volume IV of the *Saints of May* <note>part of the massive *Acta Sanctorum* project</note>, Antwerp, 1685, folio. Louis Jacob de Saint-Charles’s *Pontifical Library*, divided into two books. The first deals with the Pontiffs themselves in alphabetical order. The second deals with all the authors who, both in general and in particular, have consecrated their lives, praises, excellence, or authority to posterity, Lyon, 1643, quarto; thus, I refrain from saying more on this matter <note>The author suggests that since Jacob de Saint-Charles has already compiled a full bibliography of the Popes, further listing is unnecessary</note>.

5. *The Persecutions of the Primitive Church* are depicted for us, besides by the general historians, by Johannes Meisner in *On the Persecutions and Martyrdoms of the Ancient Christians*, Wittenberg, 1654, quarto. Christian Kortholt’s *Treatise on the Persecutions of the Primitive Church under the Heathen Emperors, and on the Tortures of the Ancient Christians*, Jena, 1660, octavo. Antonio Gallonio’s book *On the Tortures of the Holy Martyrs*, illustrated with most elegant figures, Cologne, 1602, octavo, and Antwerp, 1668, duodecimo. To these should be added the *Martyrologies* <term>calendars or lists of martyrs and their deaths</term> of Jerome, Bede, Florus, Wandelbert, Rabanus Maurus, Usuard, Ado of Vienne, and Notker; Matthias Friedrich Beck discusses all of these in Chapter 2 of his *Commentary on the Very Ancient Martyrology of the German Church*, which he recently edited, Augsburg, 1687, quarto. I knowingly pass over the *Roman, Gallican, and Spanish Martyrologies*, as they are sufficiently well known.

6. *Heresies* that arose in the Ancient Church are noted throughout by the ecclesiastical historians. They are described separately by Irenaeus, Tertullian, Epiphanius, Philastrius, and the author under the name *Praedestinatus* <note>An anonymous 5th-century work on heresies</note> edited by Sirmond (about whom and his various editions we dealt with under the section on Primasius).

->### PROLEGOMENA.<-

...and of his delegation <note>The author is referring to the duties of Cardinal Caesar Baronius, a key figure in the Catholic Counter-Reformation</note>, to provide for his affairs—not to mention his other occupations and his other writings—was brought to a conclusion <note>original: *ad umbelicum perductum*, literally "brought to the navel," a classical idiom for finishing a book scroll</note>. In this work, he certainly deserved great praise regarding Church Antiquity (I certainly acknowledge, not unwillingly, that I have profited not a little from it). However, he would have gained far greater glory if he had not directed all his sweat, and all the nerves of his wit and learning, toward this single goal: to expand the boundaries <note>original: *Pomœria*, referring to the sacred boundary of Rome</note> of the Papal Majesty, and to elevate and establish its dignity and authority not only above Church rights, but also above Royal and Imperial laws and every high office. This he did, not infrequently, by drawing from writers who were apocryphal, spurious, foolish, and quite futile. Regarding the work when it was only just beginning, Pierre Pithou once rightly noted (as reported by Casaubon) that it should not be called the *Ecclesiastical Annals*, but rather *On the Power of the Pope*. Certainly, neither Baronius himself \* nor Girolamo Barnabeo † deny that he had nothing else in mind than to fight against the innovators of his time—that is, the Magdeburg Centuriators <note>A group of Protestant scholars who wrote a massive history of the church to prove that the Roman Church had deviated from early Christianity</note>—*for the antiquity of sacred traditions and for preserving the power of the Roman Catholic Church*.

<margin>
\* Dedicatory Letter to Sixtus V, prefixed to the volume.
† Life of Baronius, ibid., chapters 18, 19.
</margin>

Baronius's *Annals*, from the year 1198 where they had ended, were continued by Abraham Bzovius, Odoricus Raynaldus, and finally in France by Henri Sponde. Although Sponde is smaller in sheer volume than the others, he was far superior in his reliability, diligence, and more accurate judgment. To these should be joined Isaac Casaubon’s *Exercises on Sacred and Ecclesiastical Matters* against Baronius (London, 1614, folio); Richard Montagu’s *Selected Fragments of Ecclesiastical Exercises* (London, 1622, folio), which later appeared enlarged and revised under the title *Apparatus for Ecclesiastical Origins* (Oxford, 1635, folio); and the same author’s *First and Second Parts of Commentaries on Ecclesiastical Origins* (London, 1636, 1640, folio). I shall pass over his *Diatribe* against Bulinger, as well as the writings of Rosweyde, Cappel, Eudaemon-Joannes, Prideaux, Bulenger, and others relating to this subject. Among the shorter summaries <term>compendia</term> of ecclesiastical history, the following are prominent: Gabriel Bisciola’s *Epitome* of the ten volumes of *Baronius's Annals* (Lyon and Venice, 1612, 2 volumes, quarto); Johann Micraelius’s *Summary of Church Histories*, with a continuation by Daniel Hartnack (Leipzig, 1679, quarto); Hieronymus Kromayer’s *Sixteen Centuries of Ecclesiastical History* (Leipzig, 1666, quarto); Johann Heinrich Hottinger’s *Ecclesiastical History of the New Testament*, published in Zurich in nine volumes from the year 1651 until 1667, when he died (octavo); and Friedrich Spanheim’s *Introduction to Sacred Christian Chronology and History*, volume 1 (Leiden, 1683), volume 2 (ibid., 1687, quarto); we expect the remaining volumes in due time.

2. **The editors of the Councils:** Jacques Merlin (2 volumes, Paris, 1535, octavo); Peter Crabbe (2 volumes, Cologne, 1538, 1551, folio); Bartolomé Carranza in his *Summary of the Councils* (Paris, 1624, 12mo; Douai, 1659, octavo); Laurentius Surius (4 volumes, Cologne, 1627, folio); Domenico Nicolini (5 volumes, Venice, 1585, folio); *The General Councils* (4 volumes, Rome, 1608, folio); Severin Binius (4 volumes, Cologne, 1606, 1618; 10 volumes, Paris, 1636, folio); Francesco Longo a Coriolano in his *Summary of All Councils* with annotations (Antwerp, 1623, folio); Philippe Labbe and Gabriel Cossart (passing over the Royal Edition of the Louvre for now), together with a double apparatus (Paris, 1672, 18 volumes, folio); and the *Pandects of Canons of the Apostles and Councils* received by the Greek Church, in Greek and Latin with the annotations of William Beveridge (Oxford, 1672).

->### PROLEGOMENA.<-

<note>This section continues a list of authors who have cataloged heresies throughout history.</note>

...we include) Theodoret, <note>A 5th-century bishop and influential historian of the early church.</note> John of Damascus, and Nicetas Choniates. Among more recent authors, those who deal with this subject specifically are: Bernard of Luxembourg in his *Catalog of Heretics* <note>original: *Catalogo hereticorum*</note>, 1523, octavo; Alfonso à Castro in fourteen books *Against Heresies* <note>original: *contra hæreses*</note>, found among his works and published separately at Cologne, 1549, folio, and Antwerp, 1565; Gabriel Prateolus, *On the Lives, Sects, and Dogmas of All Heretics* <note>original: *de Vitis, Sectis & Dogmatibus omnium hæreticorum*</note>, Cologne, 1569, folio; Theodorus Petraeus in his *Catalog of Heretics from the Birth of Christ to His Own Times* <note>original: *Catalogo Hæreticorum à Christo nato ad sua tempora*</note>, Cologne, 1629; Daniel Cramer’s *The Tree of Heretical Genealogy* <note>original: *Arbor hæreticæ Consanguinitatis*</note>, Strasbourg, 1623, quarto; Laurentius Laelius’s *Index of Heresies* <note>original: *Index Hæreseon*</note>, Frankfurt, 1614, octavo; and Joannes Pontanus in his *Catalog of Heretics, Both Ancient and Recent, Arranged Alphabetically* <note>original: *Catalogo hereticorum tam veterum, quam recentiorum, methodo Alphabeticâ*</note>, Frankfurt, 1615, duodecimo. Despite these and many other works that could be added, an accurate work concerning heretics—especially ancient ones—dealing with their origins, progress, connections, dogmas, and duration, must still rightfully be considered a missing necessity in our scholarship. In the following work, we have attempted something in this area, even if it does not fully satisfy the abundance and dignity of the subject.

7. *Ecclesiastical Rites and Ceremonies* <term>Ritus & Ceremonias; the formal, repetitive acts and traditional practices used in Christian worship</term> are explained by writers on the divine offices of the Catholic Church, such as the *Roman Order* <note>original: *Ordo Romanus*</note>, Isidore, Alcuin, Amalarius, Rabanus Maurus, and others. These were collected into a single volume and published by Melchior Hittorp, Paris, 1610, folio, and are therefore usually found in the *Library of the Fathers* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Patrum*; a standard collection of writings from early church leaders</note>. Also: William Durandus in his *Rationale of Divine Offices* <note>original: *Rationali divinorum Officiorum*</note>, Lyon, 1592, octavo; Stephanus Durandus *On the Rites of the Catholic Church* <note>original: *de Ritibus catholicæ Ecclesiæ*</note> in three books, Rome, 1591; Cologne, 1592; Lyon, 1606; Paris, 1631, octavo; Bartholomaeus Gavantus in his *Treasury of Sacred Rites* <note>original: *Thesauro Sacrorum rituum*</note> in two volumes, Rome, 1628; Antwerp, 1634; Lyon, 1664, quarto; Giovanni Battista Casalius *On the Ancient Sacred Rites of Christians* <note>original: *de Veteribus sacris Christianorum ritibus*</note>, Rome, 1647, folio; Gabriel Aubespine *Two Books of Observations on the Ancient Rites of the Church* <note>original: *de Veteribus Ecclesiæ Ritibus observationum*</note>, Paris, 1623, quarto; Helmstedt, 1672, quarto; and in the newest edition of Optatus, Paris, 1679, folio.

8. The state of *Ancient Monasticism* is shown, as the occasion arose, by the ancient writers Socrates and Sozomen; and with deliberate effort by Theodoret in his *Religious History* <note>original: *historiâ religiosâ*</note>, Palladius in the *Lausiac History* <note>original: *Historiâ Lausiacâ*; a collection of biographies of early Egyptian desert monks</note>, Cassian in his *Institutes of the Cenobites* <note>original: *Institutis Cœnobiorum*</note> and *Conferences of the Fathers* <note>original: *Collat. Patrum*</note>, John Climacus in the *Spiritual Meadow* <note>original: *Prato spirituali*; though usually attributed to John Moschus, the author here ascribes it to Climacus</note>, and others. Among modern authors: Rudolf Hospinian, *On the Origin and Progress of Monasticism and Monastic Orders* <note>original: *de Origine & Progressu Monachatûs, ac Ordinum Monasticorum*</note> in six books, Zurich, 1588 and 1609, folio; François Modius’s *The Origin, Progress, and Dress of the Ecclesiastical Order* <note>original: *Ordinis Ecclesiasticis Origo, progressus, & vestitus ratio*</note>, Frankfurt, 1614, octavo; Johannes Creccelius’s *Collection from Histories on the Origin and Foundation of Almost All Monastic Orders, Adorned with Figures* <note>original: *Collectanea ex historiis de Origine & fundatione, omnium fere monasticorum Ordinum, figuris ornata*</note>, Frankfurt, 1614, quarto; Laurentius Landtmeter *On the Ancient Cleric-Monk* <note>original: *de Vetere Clerico-Monacho*</note> in three books, Antwerp, 1635, quarto; Antoine Dadin d'Hauteserre’s *Asceticon, or Ten Books on the Origins of Monasticism* <note>original: *Asceticon, sive Originum rei monasticæ*</note>, Paris, 1674, quarto.

9. *Ecclesiastical Chronology* <term>the science of arranging historical events in order of time within the history of the church</term> will be taught to you by Eusebius, Jerome, Prosper, and others. Also: Denis Pétau *On the Doctrine of Times* <note>original: *de Doctrina temporum*</note> in two volumes, Paris, 1627, folio; and in his *Summary of Times* <note>original: *Rationario temporum*</note>, Paris, 1634, 1636, 1652, octavo; Mainz, 1646, octavo; Giambattista Riccioli in his *Reformed Chronology* <note>original: *Chronologia reformata*</note>, Bologna, 1669, folio; Philippe Labbe and Philippe Briet’s *Historical and Technical Chronology* <note>original: *Chronologia Historica & Technica*</note>, Paris, 1670, five volumes, folio; Philippe Briet’s *Annals of the World, or Chronicle...* <note>original: *Annales mundi, sive Chronicon*</note>

<meta>catchword: nicon</meta>

<summary>This page lists major historical and contemporary scholarly works regarding the study of heretical groups, church rites, the origins of monasticism, and the timeline of church history. The author notes that while many catalogs of heretics exist, a definitive and accurate study of ancient heresies is still a major gap in the field.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the bibliographic survey of essential reference works for church historians. It completes a section on chronology before moving into two major categories: Sacred Geography (the physical organization of the church) and Ecclesiastical Lexicography (dictionaries of specialized church terminology and customs).</meta>

page xxix

->### PROLEGOMENA.<-

<note>This is a continuation of a list of chronological works from the previous page.</note>

*...Universal Chronicle, from the creation of the world to the year of Christ 1660* <note>original: *Chronicon Universale, ab orbe condito ad an Chr. 1660*</note>, *three volumes, Paris, 1662, in duodecimo. Egidius Bucherius’s Commentary on the Ancient Writers of the Paschal Canons regarding the Science of Time* <note>original: *de Doctrina temporum Commentarius in Antiquos Canonum Paschalium scriptores*</note>, *Antwerp, 1634, folio. Caspar Heunisch’s Ages of the Principal Fathers accurately demonstrated from certain historical and chronological foundations* <note>original: *ætates Patrum præcipuorum ex certis fundamentis historicis & chronologicis accuratè demonstratæ*</note>, *Rotenburg, 1677, octavo.*

10. The sacred geography of the Ancient Church is best mapped out by the Emperor Leo the Wise’s *Register of Bishoprics* <note>original: *Notitia Episcopatuum*</note>, published in Greek and Latin by the illustrious William Beveridge in his *Annotations on the Canons* <note>original: *Annot. ad Can.*</note>, page 135; and the *Formularies* <note>original: Διατυπώσεις</note> which appear more than once in Leunclavius’s *Greco-Roman Law* <note>original: *Jure Græco-Romano*</note>. More fully, Aubertus Miraeus in his *Register of the Bishoprics of the Christian World* <note>original: *Notitia Episcopatuum orbis Christiani*</note> with notes, *Paris*, 1610; *Frankfurt*, 1614; *Lyons*, 1620, octavo. Augustine Lubin in his *Commentary on the Roman Martyrology* <note>original: *Comment. ad Martyrologium Romanum*</note>, explained with notes and geographical and historical tables, *Paris*, 1611, quarto. Friedrich Spanheim in his *Introduction to Sacred and Ecclesiastical Geography* <note>original: *Introductione ad Geographiam sacram & Ecclesiasticam*</note>, *Leiden*, 1679, octavo; he has promised a far more extensive work on this subject, which I hope he will not eventually begrudge the public. To this point, Charles of St. Paul, Abbot of Feuillant, has snatched the prize from all others in his *Sacred Geography, or Ancient Notice of the Bishoprics of the Universal Church* <note>original: *Geographiâ Sacrâ, seu Notitiâ antiquâ Episcopatuum Ecclesiæ Universæ*</note>, together with geographical tables accurately prepared to reflect the state of the Christian Church, *Paris*, 1641, folio. To this may also be added Father Joseph Cantel’s *Sacred and Civil History of Metropolitan Cities* <note>original: *Metropolitanarum Urbium Historia Sacra & Civilis*</note>, of which the first volume appeared in *Paris*, 1684, quarto.

11. One must also call upon the aid of the Ecclesiastical Lexicographers, who clearly explain the words and phrases peculiar to, or specifically characteristic of, church writers. Ludovicus de la Cerda achieved something in this area in his *Adversaria*; as did Novarinus in his *Sacred and Profane Selections* <note>original: *Electis Sacro-prophanis*</note>. More intentionally, Johann Heinrich Alsted in his *Theological Summaries* <note>original: *Paratitlis Theologicis*</note>, *Frankfurt*, 1626, quarto; Joshua Arnd in his *Lexicon of Ecclesiastical Antiquities* <note>original: *Lexico Antiquitatum Ecclesiasticarum*</note>, *Greifswald*, 1669, quarto; Pierre Crespet in his *Summary of Ecclesiastical Discipline Arranged Alphabetically* <note>original: *summâ Ecclesiasticæ disciplinæ alphabeticè dispositâ*</note>, *Lyons*, 1628, folio. Domenico and Carlo Magri in their *Sacred Lexicon* <note>original: *Hiero-Lexico*</note>, first published in Italian under the title *Account of Ecclesiastical Vocabulary* <note>original: *Notitia de Vocabuli Ecclesiastici*</note> at *Rome*, 1650, quarto; then immensely enlarged and published in Latin at *Rome*, 1677, folio. But the singular labor of Johann Caspar Suicer surpassed the industry of everyone in this field; he published his *Ecclesiastical Treasury from the Greek Fathers* <note>original: *Thesaurum Ecclesiasticum è Patribus Græcis*</note>, arranged in alphabetical order—a work of twenty years—at *Amsterdam*, 1682, in two volumes, folio. It should be noted that all these authors explain not merely bare words and the etymologies and origins of phrases, but also clearly set forth rites, ceremonies, dogmas, heresies, and other such matters. In this category also belong Henry Spelman’s *Archaeological Glossary of Medieval Latin* <note>original: *Glossarium Archaiologicum, Latino-Barbarum*</note>, *London*, 1664, folio; and Charles du Fresne’s *Glossary for Writers of Middle and Late Latinity* <note>original: *Glossarium ad scriptores mediæ & infimæ Latinitatis*</note>, three volumes, *Paris*, 1678, folio. Truly, for those who deal with such matters, what end will there be of writers? In general, therefore, for the illumination of Church antiquities, much will be contributed by the *Magdeburg Centuries* <note>A massive 16th-century Protestant history of the church organized by centuries.</note>, and the *Ecclesiastical Histories* of Hottinger and Spanheim reviewed above; as well as Hornbeek’s *Old and New* <note>original: *Vetera & nova*</note>, *Utrecht*, 1672, quarto; and Johann Conrad Dannhauer’s *Christeis*, exhibiting the three-hundred-year lot of the Church from the time Christ was raised into heaven until Constantine the Great...

The author highlights key scholars like Suicer, Spanheim, and Du Cange, whose works defined the study of church antiquities during this period.</summary>

->### PROLEGOMENA.<-

Strasbourg <note>original: *Argentoratum*</note>, 1646, quarto. Balthasar Bebel’s *Evangelical and Jewish Antiquities* <note>original: *Antiquitates Evangelicæ & Judaicæ*</note>, Strasbourg, 1673, quarto. By the same author: *Antiquities of the Church of the First Three Centuries* <note>original: *Antiquitates Ecclesiæ trium priorum fæculorum*</note>, ibid. <note>meaning "in the same place," referring to Strasbourg</note>, 1669, quarto. Second volume, ibid., 1679, quarto. Joseph Quesnel’s *Dissertations on Leo the Great* <note>original: *dissertationes ad Leonem M.*</note>, Paris, 1675, quarto. Jean Garner’s *Dissertations on Marius Mercator, Theodoret, Liberatus, the Daily Book* <note>original: *Librum Diurnum*—a collection of papal formulas</note>, etc. Louis Ellies du Pin’s *Historical Dissertations on the Ancient Discipline of the Church* <note>original: *dissertationes Historicæ de antiqua Ecclesiæ disciplina*</note>, Paris, 1686, quarto. Those who have plenty of money and leisure might profitably consult Natalis Alexander’s (of the Dominican Order) *Dissertations on Selected Chapters of Ecclesiastical History* <note>original: *dissertationes ad selecta Historiæ Ecclesiasticæ Capita*</note>: an immense work, comprising twenty-two volumes in octavo <note>a medium-sized book format</note>, which were published in Paris starting from the year 1676. What this tireless monk achieved in such a laborious work, let others judge; I confess that, except for the first volume, I have not yet read through it. Finally, since the scope of our plan does not allow for more, I will recommend one more writer to the reader. This is John Forbes, a Scotsman <note>original: *Scoto-Britannus*</note>, who published *Historico-Theological Instructions on Christian Doctrine and the Various States of Affairs, Errors, and Controversies that have arisen* <note>original: *Instructiones Historico-Theologicas de Doctrina Christiana & vario rerum statu, ortisq; erroribus & controversiis*</note>, from the Apostolic times onward, in sixteen books, Amsterdam, 1645, folio <note>a large, prestigious book format</note>. A work of immense value—if I may say so as someone devoted to these studies—and especially useful for students of ecclesiastical antiquity; it would have been much more useful if only God had not denied this most learned author a longer life <note>original: *longiorem lucis usuram*—literally "a longer use of the light"</note> to complete the tapestry he had begun to weave.

Let us now proceed to a special class. This I divide into two parts. The first concerns those who exercise criticism solely regarding the genuine, spurious, or doubtful works of the Holy Fathers, or who have devised rules for better understanding, interpreting, and applying them. In this category, Sixtus of Siena excels above the rest in the last four books of his *Holy Library* <note>original: *Bibliothecæ sanctæ*</note>; as does Andreas Hyperius in his *On the Method of Theological Study* <note>original: *de ratione studii Theologici*</note>, especially in books 3 and 4, Basel, 1559 and 1572, octavo. Whose work was almost entirely transcribed into his own books *On the Proper Formation of Theological Study* <note>original: *de rectè formando Theologiæ studio*</note> by Lorenzo de Villavicencio, a Spaniard and Augustinian theologian, Antwerp, 1565, octavo. William Perkins in his *Preparatory Instruction for Youth on Reading all the Fathers* <note>original: *Προπαιδείᾳ Juventutis ad lectionem omnium Patrum*</note>, prefixed to his *Problem on the False Catholicity of the Roman Faith*, Cambridge, 1604, quarto; Hanau, 1604, duodecimo. Our own countryman Robert Cooke, in his *Censure of Certain Writings cited by Papists under the Names of Saints and Ancient Authors* <note>original: *censura quorundam Scriptorum, quæ sub nominibus sanctorum, & veterum Auctorum à Pontificiis citantur*</note>, London, 1614 and 1623, quarto; Helmstedt, 1655, octavo. Antoine Teissier is mistaken, however, when he distinguishes this work from the same Cooke’s *Censure of the Fathers*, though Philippe Labbe <note>original: *Labbeus*</note> had already led him into this error in this regard. Andreas Rivet in the four books of his *Sacred Critic* <note>original: *Critici Sacri*</note>, and his *Treatise on the Authority of the Fathers, the Causes of Errors, and the Marks of Spurious Works* <note>original: *Tract. de Patrum autoritate, errorum causis & Nothorum notis*</note>, prefixed to these books, Geneva, 1642, octavo. Johann Heinrich Hottinger in his *Introduction to the Reading of the Fathers*; and his *Dissertation on the Use of the Fathers*, among his *Historico-Theological Collections* <note>original: *Analecta Historico-Theologica*</note>, Zurich, 1652, octavo. By the same author: *Dissertation on the Abuse of the Fathers*; as well as a *Catalog of Spurious Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *Catalogus scriptorum Ecclesiasticorum Supposititiorum*</note>, along with others under the title of *Miscellaneous Dissertations*, published ibid., 1654, octavo. And what he contains in the second book of his *Theological Library*, ibid., 1664, quarto. John Forbes, in his *Historico-Theological Instructions*, book 7, chapter 5, provides twelve rules illustrated with examples and observations, which are especially useful for more easily understanding the writings of the Fathers. Richard Holdsworth also has many things on this matter in his *Theological Lectures*, from Lecture 35 to 43. Many things,

&

### PROLEGOMENA.

...and not unworthy of a reading is Johannes Hornbeck’s *Treatise on the Theology of the Fathers* <note>original: *Tract. de Theologia Patrum*</note>, though it ends with Arnobius. It is found among his *Old and New Works* <note>original: *Vetera & Nova*</note>, Utrecht, 1672, quarto. Also Jean Daillé’s second book *On the True Use of the Fathers* <note>original: *de Vero usu Patrum*</note>, Geneva, 1656, quarto; *On the Writings of Dionysius the Areopagite and Ignatius* <note>original: *de scriptis Dionysii Areopagitæ & Ignatii*</note>, ibid., 1666, quarto; and *On Apostolic Pseudepigrapha* <note>original: *de Pseudepigraphis Apostolicis*</note>, Harderwijk, 1653, octavo. Martin Chemnitz in his *Oration on the Reading of the Fathers*, or *of the Ecclesiastical Doctors* <note>original: *Oratione de Lectione Patrum, sive Doctorum Ecclesiasticorum*</note>, which he placed before his explanation of the commonplaces at Wittenberg, Frankfurt, 1653, page 1. Johannes Botfaccus in his *Patrology*, or on the freedom of judging the writings of the Ancient Doctors <note>original: *Patrologia, seu de libertate Veterum Doctorum scripta judicandi*</note>, Stettin, 1663, octavo. Friedrich Gronovius in his *Observations on Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *Observationibus in Scriptores Ecclesiasticos*</note>, Deventer, 1651, duodecimo <note>a small book format</note>.

The second part comprises authors who treat the Ecclesiastical writers and their works <term>κατ’ ἀνδρ’</term> <note>original Greek: *kat’ andra*; meaning "man by man" or individually</note> and by profession. From the number of these, in the first rank stand Jerome, Gennadius of Marseilles, Isidore of Seville, Ildefonsus of Toledo, Honorius of Autun, Sigebert of Gembloux, and Henry of Ghent. Suffridus Petrus published these together and illustrated them with notes, Cologne, 1580, octavo; also with scholia <note>explanatory comments</note> and a vast *Supplement* <note>original: *Auctuario*</note> by Aubertus Miraeus, in the *Ecclesiastical Library* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Ecclesiast.*</note>, Antwerp, 1639, folio, after whose death a second part appeared, ibid., 1649. Johannes Trithemius in his *Collections on Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *Collectaneis de Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis*</note>, Mainz, 1494; and with additions, Paris, 1512, quarto; Cologne, 1531, 1546, quarto; Basel, 1594. Sixtus of Siena in the *Holy Library* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Sancta*</note>, throughout the fourth book. Antonio Possevino in the *Sacred Apparatus* <note>original: *Apparatu Sacro*</note>, Cologne, 1608, two volumes, folio. Abraham Scultetus, in *The Marrow of the Theology of the Fathers, in which he treats at length and accurately the Fathers and the Theology of the ancient Doctors of the primitive Church who flourished before and after the Council of Nicaea* <note>original: *Medulla Theologiæ Patrum...*</note>, though he proceeds no further than the times of Basil and Hilary, Hamburg, 1613; Frankfurt, 1634, quarto. Johannes Schopfius in *The Academy of Jesus Christ* <note>original: *Academia Jesu Christi*</note>, Tübingen, 1593; Speyer, 1616, but he mostly follows in the footsteps of the Centuriators <note>the Magdeburg Centuriators, a group of Lutheran historians</note>. Pierre Halloix in the *Lives, Writings, and Documents of the illustrious writers of the Eastern Church of the first and second centuries* <note>original: *Vitis, Scriptis, & Documentis illustrium Ecclesiæ Orientalis scriptorum primi & secundi seculi*</note>; in which work he deals with them most extensively, Douai, 1633, 1636, two volumes, folio. Cardinal Robert Bellarmine’s *Book on Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *libro de Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis*</note>, Cologne, 1612, octavo; Lyon, 1613, quarto; Paris, 1617, octavo; and with the philological appendix of Philippe Labbe, Paris, 1658, octavo; Lyon, 1675, octavo. André Saussay, Bishop of Toul, gave a supplement to Bellarmine, Toul, 1665, quarto; and another recently by Casimir Oudin. Philippe Labbe in his *Philological and Historical Dissertations on the Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *Dissertationibus Philologicis & Historicis, de Scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis*</note> touched upon by Bellarmine, Paris, 1660, two volumes, octavo. He was indeed a man of infinite reading and of untiring diligence in promoting literary matters, but of a spirit too harsh and bitter toward the Lutherans and the Reformed. Augustus Varenius in his *Theological Ledger of the Ecclesiastical writers of the first and second centuries* <note>original: *Rationario Theologico de scriptoribus Ecclesiasticis primi & secundi seculi*</note>, Rostock, 1669, 1673, quarto. I pass over Gerhard's *Patrology*, as his own closest friends admit it was collected only for his private use, and not with the intention of being published. The first inspection of the book and the matter itself sufficiently teaches this. Johann Gottfried Olearius in his *Patrological Table, or an Alphabetical enumeration of the Fathers and Doctors of the Christian Church, as a supplement to Gerhard's Patrology* <note>original: *Abaco Patrologico, sive Ecclesiæ Christianæ Patrum & Doctorum Alphabetica enumeratione, in Patrologiæ Gerardi supplementum*</note>, Jena, 1673, octavo. Gerhard Meier in his *Abridged Theological Library* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Theologica Contracta*</note>, book 2, Bremen, 1660, duodecimo; but he treats this material with too sparing and meager a hand.

page xxxii

# PROLEGOMENA.

...treatise. Not to mention others, it will be useful for the **Curious Reader** to consult Gisbert Voetius’s *Library for the Student of Theology* <note>original: *Bibliotheca studioso Theologiæ*</note>, Utrecht, 1651, duodecimo <note>a small book format</note>. Johann Andreas Bosius’s *Brief Sketch on Acquiring Knowledge of Ecclesiastical Writers* <note>original: *Schediasma de comparanda notitia Scriptorum Ecclesiasticorum*</note>, Jena, 1673, quarto. Valentin Heinrich Vogler’s *Universal Introduction to the Knowledge of Every Kind of Good Writer* <note>original: *Introductionem Universalem in Notitiam cujuscunque generis bonorum scriptorum*</note>, Helmstedt, 1670, quarto. Philippe Labbe’s *Library of Libraries* <note>original: *Bibliothecam Bibliothecarum*</note>, Paris, 1664, octavo. This was recently reviewed and increased with an immense addition by Antoine Teissier of Nîmes, Geneva, 1686, quarto. Severin Walther Sluter’s *Portal to Christian History* <note>original: *Propylaum Historiæ Christianæ*</note>, Frankfurt, 1680, duodecimo.

Finally, it remains for us to briefly review some of those who have published indices of manuscript books. To this category belongs Possevino’s *Appendix to Volume II of the Sacred Apparatus* <note>original: *Appendix ad Tom. ii. Apparatus sacri*</note>; however, it was made without much discernment and is not sufficiently accurate for the importance of the subject. Philippe Labbe’s *New Library of Manuscript Books* <note>original: *Nova Bibliotheca MSS. librorum*</note>, Paris, 1653, quarto. Gottlieb Spitzel’s *Sacred Secrets of Illustrious Libraries Revealed* <note>original: *Sacra Bibliothecarum illustrium arcana retecta*</note>, Augsburg, 1668, octavo. Thomas James’s *Oxford-Cambridge Selection* <note>original: *Ecloga Oxonio-Cantabrigiensis*</note>, London, 1600, quarto. Giacomo Filippo Tomasini’s *Public and Private Manuscript Library of Padua* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Patavinæ MSS. publicæ & privatæ*</note>, Udine, 1639, quarto. Also his *Public and Private Manuscript Library of Venice* <note>original: *Bibliothecæ Venetæ MSS. publicæ & privatæ*</note>, ibid., 1650, quarto. Alexander Barvoetius’s *Catalog of the Principal Greek Manuscript Authors Preserved in the Escorial Library* <note>original: *Catalogus præcipuorum Autorum Græcè MSS. qui asservantur in Bibliotheca Scorialensi*</note>, placed after Corderius’s *Prolegomena* in Cyril’s *Homilies on Jeremiah*, Antwerp, 1648, octavo; and in Labbe’s *New Library*, page 175. Anton Sanders’s *Belgian Library of Manuscripts, or Universal List of Manuscript Codices* <note>original: *Bibliotheca Belgica MSS. sive Elenchus Universalis Codd. MSS.*</note> etc., Lille, 1641, quarto. The *Greek Theological Manuscripts of the Munich Library* entered into an index by Hervart <note>original: *Monachiensis Bibliothecæ MSS. Græca Theologica ab Hervarto in Indicem relata*</note>, which are found both elsewhere and in Spitzel’s cited work, page 145. The *Theological Manuscripts of the Utrecht Library* <note>original: *Trajectinæ Bibliothecæ MS. Theol.*</note>, Utrecht, 1608, and in Spitzel, page 290. David Hoeschel’s *Catalog of Greek Manuscript Codices in the Augsburg Library* <note>original: *Catalogus Græcorum Cod. MSS. in Bibliothecâ Augustanâ*</note>, Augsburg, 1595 and 1605, quarto. Then by the care of Elias Ehinger, 1630, quarto. The *Theological Manuscripts of the Basel Library* <note>original: *Basileensis Bibliothecæ MS. Theol.*</note> are supplied by Spitzel, page 17. Heinrich Ernst’s *Catalog of Manuscript Books of the Medicean Library in Florence* <note>original: *Catalogus librorum MSS. Bibliothecæ Florentinæ Medicææ*</note>, Amsterdam, 1641. The *Catalog of the Leiden Library* <note>original: *Catalogus Bibliothecæ Lugduno-Batavæ*</note> revised by the most celebrated Spanheim, Leiden, 1674, quarto; especially from page 295, where the printed books end. The *Manuscript Codices of the Thuanian Library* <note>original: *Bibliothecæ Thuanæ Codices MSS.*</note> at the end of the *Thuanian Library*, page 419, Paris, 1679, 2 volumes, octavo. Anton Reiser’s *Index of Manuscript Books in the Augsburg Library* <note>original: *Index librorum MSS. in Bibliothecâ Augustanâ*</note>, Augsburg, 1675, quarto. Joachim Feller’s *Catalog of Manuscript Codices of the Pauline Library in the Academy of Leipzig* <note>original: *Catalogus Codicum MSS. Bibliothecæ Paulinæ in Academia Lipsiensi*</note>, Leipzig, 1686, duodecimo. 

But above all others, **Peter Lambecius** deserves the prize, both for the number and rarity of the manuscript books and for several very curious monuments of antiquity, in his *Commentaries on the Most August Library of Vienna* <note>original: *Commentariis de Augustissima Bibliothecâ Vindobonensi*</note>, Vienna, 1665, folio. From that time, eight volumes appeared until the year 1679; and yet, the most distinguished author died while still engaged in the work <note>original: *immortuus est*; literally "died into the work"</note>, to the great loss of the Republic of Letters <note>The "Republic of Letters" refers to the long-distance intellectual community of late 17th-century Europe</note>, before it was completed.
