# PROLEGOMENA.

## SECTION VII.

->*An Exhortation to the Study of Ecclesiastical Antiquity.*<-

Everything having finally been prepared in this manner, having obtained the best guides for the way, and being equipped on all sides with suitable resources, what stands in our way to prevent us from hastening to unfold the writings of the **Holy Fathers** <note>The "Fathers" refers to the influential theologians and bishops of the first several centuries of the Christian Church.</note>, and applying ourselves to these studies with our whole soul? The Roman Catholics <note>original: *Pontificii*</note> are accustomed to turn it into a vice against us, claiming that we despise and reject the ancient Fathers. In his *Theological Commonplaces*, **Melchior Canus**—a man otherwise of a fair and moderate disposition—reproaches us more than once, saying that *the Lutherans have no commerce with the Fathers, that they cast down their authority from its rank, and offer them up as objects of either ridicule or certainly contempt.* **Possevino** says in his *Select Library, book 5, chapter 26*: *The heretics never formally taught the Fathers in the Schools, nor did they ever publish them willingly or without corruption.* And with these men, **Bellarmine**, **Maldonatus**, **Harding**, **Campion**, and others, nearly all sing the same song. How false and how unjust this calumny is, both the Lutherans and the Reformed <note>The "Reformed" refers to the tradition following Calvin and others, distinct from the Lutheran branch of the Reformation.</note> have long since publicly demonstrated in their writings. Indeed, that the same old story should be forged against us, I think hardly anyone could be so brazen <note>original: *perfrictæ frontis*, literally "of a rubbed forehead," an idiom for shamelessness</note> as to bring such a charge against the sons of our Church. How far we are from being complete strangers to the monuments of the ancients, our adversaries themselves experience daily to their own peril, and even snarling envy itself will not deny it. 

Certainly, with what great care and with what happy success the **Church of England** has always cultivated the study of ecclesiastical antiquity, from the very cradle of our reformed religion, is witnessed by so many of the most illustrious names in the world of letters: **Cranmer**, **Ridley**, **Parker**, **Jewel**, **Nowell**, **Humphrey**, **Whitgift**, **Reynolds**, **Whitaker**, **Hooker**, **Richard and John Cosin**, **Bilson**, **Bancroft**, **Sutcliffe**, **Downame**, **Perkins**, **Andrewes**, **Cooke**, **Robert and George Abbot**, **Field**, **Buckridge**, **Overall**, **White**, **Willet**, **Nicholas Fuller**, **Crakanthorpe**, **Davenant**, the **Most Serene King JAMES**, **Laud**, **Featley**, **Lindsell**, **Junius**, **Mason**, **Ward**, **Brownrigg**, **Chillingworth**, **Holdsworth**, **Hall**, **Hales**, **Ussher**, **Heylin**, **Prideaux**, **Morton**, **Montagu**, **Gregory**, **Sanderson**, **Hammond**, **Taylor**, **Thorndike**, **Isaac Barrow**, **Fell**, **Gunning**, **Pearson**, **Savile**, **Selden**, and others. What men, and how great, O good God!—if I may spare the modesty of those who are still among the living today. Our Church holds, and has always held, that firm and unmoved **Rule of Tertullian**: *That is truer which is earlier; that is earlier which is from the beginning.* <note>original: *Id verius quod prius, id prius quod ab initio.* A foundational principle of the Reformation: looking back to the "primitive" church for truth.</note> The closer we approach the fountain of Truth, the purer flows the stream of **Catholic Doctrine** <note>In this context, "Catholic" refers to the universal, ancient teachings of the whole church, rather than the Roman Catholic Church specifically.</note>. Nor is there any more manifest sign of a failing faith or a collapsing church than a defection from the straight path of genuine antiquity; *While human superstitions are introduced in place of heavenly dogma, while well-founded antiquity is undermined by wicked novelty, while the institutes of our predecessors are violated, while the decrees of the Fathers are rescinded, while the definitions of our ancestors are torn up, while the lust of profane and novel curiosity does not contain itself within the most chaste limits of sacred and incorrupt antiquity,* as **Vincent of Lérins** so excellently put it. In

<meta>This page continues the defense of the Anglican Church's commitment to antiquity. It argues that the English Reformation was not a novelty but a restoration of the primitive Church, citing the scholar Isaac Casaubon and St. Basil the Great. It then pivots to the intrinsic beauty and historical value of studying the early Church, comparing it to the study of ancient ruins and coins.</meta>

# PROLEGOMENA.

It results in the eternal honor and glory of our Church that the **Restorers** <note>original: *Ἐξορθωταί* (Exorthotai); a term for the leaders of the English Reformation, framing them as "correctors" who set things right.</note> of the Anglican Reformation drew their faith not from unwritten and ambiguous Traditions, nor from the muddy pools of the **Scholastics** <note>Medieval theologians who focused on logic and dialectic, often criticized by later scholars for being too abstract.</note>, nor from the disorganized medleys of the **Systematized theologians** <note>Likely referring to those who built rigid theological "systems" rather than relying on historical sources.</note>, but from the sacred page, the venerable monuments of the holy Fathers, and the decrees of the first Councils. They accurately delineated the Liturgy, rites, and ceremonies—and indeed the entire government—not according to the whims of the Papal School, nor of Luther or Calvin, but according to the rule and model of primitive Antiquity. Hence, that **Great Casaubon** <note>Isaac Casaubon (1559–1614), a renowned classical scholar and theologian who moved to England and became a great defender of the Anglican "Middle Way."</note>, a man most well-versed in the writings of the Fathers, did not hesitate to write openly: *The Anglican Church has been so established that no other today approaches more closely to the form of the once-flourishing Church, following a middle path between those sinning either by excess or by defect.* Furthermore, our very laws, enacted in the solemn Assemblies of the Kingdom <note>The English Parliament.</note>, take diligent care that no doctrine among us be held as orthodox unless it agrees perfectly with the Canonical Scriptures and the first four **Ecumenical Councils** <note>The major meetings of the early global church: Nicaea (325), Constantinople (381), Ephesus (431), and Chalcedon (451).</note>. Most gravely and appropriately, St. Basil says in *On the Holy Spirit*, chapter 29: <note>original: "Δυσωπητικὰ γὰρ πῶς τὰ παλαιὰ τῶν δογμάτων, οἱονεὶ πολιᾷ τινι τῇ ἀρχαιότητι τὸ αἰδέσιμον ἔχοντα"</note> *Ancient dogmas are somehow persuasive, possessing something venerable because of their antiquity, as if by a certain graying of hair.*

External things also gain a certain value from their antiquity. Who would not marvel at and gaze with a kind of silent veneration upon the ruins of old Rome—the Temples, Theaters, Porticos, the statues eaten away by age, and the scattered, broken marbles? Who would not handle ancient coins with a certain remarkable pleasure—perhaps those bronze ones, which cannot be redeemed by gold itself, held in such high regard by everyone that they have earned a place among the most precious **treasures** <note>original: *κειμήλια* (keimelia); heirlooms or sacred treasures.</note> of Kings? Who is not moved with wonderful delight by the memory of past centuries? To turn over the annals of times gone by; to contemplate the image of the world at its birth; to investigate the origins, customs, and migrations of nations; to recall the rise and fall of empires; and to trace the fates of peoples and the various changes of the human race across the whole world? Now truly, according to the Prophet’s utterance, *What is the chaff to the wheat?* <note>Jeremiah 23:28.</note> — If *glass is worth so much, how much is a pearl worth?* <note>original: "tanti vitrum, quanti margaritum?"</note> as I might say with **Tertullian** <note>An early Christian author from North Africa (c. 155–220 AD).</note> in *To the Martyrs*, chapter 4. The antiquities of cities and nations lead us sweetly—I might almost say, they seize us; how much greater the pleasure that floods the mind when contemplating the birth and beginnings of the Christian Church, its progress and growth, the founding of its sees, the established metropolitan centers, the successions of Bishops, and the rights of the Patriarchs?

Who is there (unless he has a **pumpkin for a heart**) <note>A Latin idiom (*peponem pro corde*) for being dull-witted or lacking feeling.</note> who does not most greedily desire to read again the acts, travels, and struggles of the blessed Apostles? To touch the victory-palms of the Holy Martyrs, who triumphed over the very rack, the iron claws, and the heaviest tortures? To enter the synodal assemblies, to survey the venerable gatherings of the Fathers with one's eyes, and to drink in their prayers and decrees with one's ears? To whom is it not a joy to look upon our faith as it was emerging and struggling out of the waves of so many difficulties, and by a certain power sent from heaven, subduing almost the entire world to itself? Finally, what could be more pleasant than to spend one's life with men of an honest heart and unfeigned piety, to live among those who, by their character, teaching, erudition, and magnificent monuments, adorned and built up the Church, and whose names...

# PROLEGOMENA

...who will wonder if errors have crept in for me while working on such a long and difficult task? For the work itself was written at widely different times, and was born almost entirely amidst the various burdens of failing health. Being but a man, I consider error to be in no way foreign to me <note>The author is paraphrasing the Roman playwright Terence: "I am a man, I consider nothing human foreign to me."</note>; whatever mistake I have made, I will gladly correct it, even upon the slightest warning. If the reader finds the writing style somewhat uneven in places, I would wish them to consider that I have perhaps contracted some blemish from the countless writers I have spent nearly all my time studying. I certainly applied all my diligence to the facts themselves, being less concerned with the style. 

I am truly sorry that so many printing errors <term>typographical errors: mistakes made during the manual setting of type</term> are found; may the kind reader not disdain to apply a "healing hand" to them. I have noted many; the reader will perhaps observe others. Indeed, it is surprising that the pile of errors did not grow larger: for besides the fact that I had to deal with lazy printers who were unaccustomed to printing Latin or Greek, during the first part of the work my eyes could hardly—scarcely at all—endure the strain of proofreading and correcting, while my scribe <term>Amanuensis: a secretary or assistant who copies manuscripts</term>, to whom I had partially entrusted this task, was often far too drowsy. But this is a misfortune I share with almost everyone else: one might only hope for a book to come forth without flaws when the author himself is entirely free and immune from every error.

->*Who is burdened by the fewest faults.*<- <note>A quote from the Roman poet Horace: "Optimus ille, qui minimis urgetur."</note>

# THE APOSTOLIC AGE.
## Overview of the Century.

<margin>1st CENTURY AD</margin>
**A**s the advent of the Messiah—so often promised and so long expected—approached, *John the Baptist* was sent ahead. He was established as a kind of boundary between the old and the new, at whom *Judaism* was to end and from whom *Christianism* was to begin. He is quite elegantly called by Tertullian in *Against Marcion, Book 4, Chapter 33*, "The herald who points the hand to the first manifestation" <note>original Greek: Χεῖρα τῆς πρώτης φανερώσεως κῆρυξ</note>. Justin Martyr <note>A 2nd-century Christian philosopher and apologist</note> in his *Dialogue with Trypho, page 268*, and Clement of Alexandria in his *Exhortation, page 7*, call him: "The voice running before the Word, a voice of exhortation, preparing for salvation, a voice urging towards the inheritance of the Heavens" <note>original Greek and Latin: 'Η φωνὴ πρόδρομος τοῦ Λόγου... Vox Verbum præcurrens...</note>. Soon after John follows our Lord, the true Messiah, sent from God <note>original Latin: θεόθεν</note> to announce the Gospel and redeem the human race. By him were appointed the twelve APOSTLES, ambassadors destined for the conversion of the nations. To these were added the 70 Disciples, whom Pseudo-Dorotheus <term>Pseudo-Dorotheus: The name given to the anonymous author of a list of the seventy disciples, now considered largely legendary</term> lists by name; he fancifully trifles by claiming they were all bishops and assigning an Episcopal seat to each. Finally, after Christ was taken up into Heaven, the Apostles—adorned with the wondrous gifts of the Holy Spirit—having preached to the Jews for several years, traveled to various provinces of the world. By them the Gospel was preached and *Churches* were founded, not only in *Syria*, but in *Rome*, *Alexandria*, etc. St. Paul even reached "the limit of the West" <note>original Greek: ἐπὶ τὸ τέρμα τῆς δύσεως</note>, according to the testimony of Clement to the Corinthians (Number 5), hinting at Britain or at least Spain. For as to the claim of certain very learned men who contend that Rome is to be understood by this phrase, I can hardly bring myself to agree. For who would call Rome the "limit of the West" in Clement's sense, when it is located in the nearer part of the West? Certainly, our famous Bishop of Chester <note>John Pearson (1613–1686), a renowned English theologian and scholar</note> stands with me; he does not merely state this but proves it at length, both in his *Pauline Annals for the year AD 63* and in his *Dissertation 1, Chapter 8, Section 9*. Furthermore, so that the rule of faith and morals might be rendered more certain, the books of the New Testament, divinely inspired <note>original Greek: θεόπνευστοι</note>, were handed down in writing, partly by the Apostles themselves and partly by their associates and ministers. In AD 47, at the Council of Jerusalem, a decree was issued by the Apostles that Christians converted from the Gentiles were not bound to observe Mosaic rites. Notwithstanding this, heretics—the unhappy offspring of Simon Magus <term>Simon Magus: A practitioner of magic mentioned in the Book of Acts, traditionally regarded as the first heretic</term>—later mixed Jewish observations with Christianity. In AD 64, seizing the opportunity from the burning of the city of Rome, Nero initiated the first persecution against the Christians; as Tertullian once noted from the very records of the Roman Senate in his *Apology, chapter 5*: "There," he says, "you will find that Nero was the first to rage with the imperial sword against this sect, when it was especially rising in Rome." In AD 70, after Jerusalem was captured and burned by Titus, the entire Jewish polity, both sacred and civil, was torn up by the roots, the nation itself being nearly destroyed. In the following year, Ebion began to peddle his heresy among the Christians then living in *Pella*, asserting that Christ was a mere and simple man; he soon reaped a significant harvest of disciples. It was against these especially that St. John directed his Gospel. Perhaps at the end of AD 95, Domitian excited the second persecution—"a portion of Nero in his cruelty," as Tertullian not unskillfully described him. Eusebius <term>Eusebius of Caesarea: A 4th-century historian known as the "Father of Church History"</term> treats this persecution in *Book 3, Chapters 17, 18, etc.*, and the Neronian persecution in *Book 2, Chapter 25*.

<summary>This page provides a chronological overview of the 1st century AD, tracing the transition from John the Baptist to the establishment of the early Church, the spread of the Gospel by the Apostles, and the first major persecutions and heresies.</summary>

## WRITERS.

<margin>
1st CENTURY AD
</margin>

<margin>
|| Justin Martyr, Dialogue with Trypho, p. 316.

JESUS CHRIST, our Lord, the eternal Son of God, from the royal lineage of the Hebrews according to the flesh, born of the mother Mary <gloss>ever-virgin</gloss> <note>original Greek: ἀειπαρθένου</note> in Bethlehem of Judea, in the year 4000 from the creation of the world and 4004 BC according to the common era, by the calculation of Ussher <note>James Ussher was a 17th-century Archbishop famous for calculating the date of creation as 4004 BC</note>. While still a boy of twelve, he publicly debated with the experts of Hebrew Law in the Temple of Jerusalem. During his private life, he devoted himself to the craft of carpentry under Joseph, his father (as he was then regarded). Saint Mark suggests this in Chapter 6:3: *Is this not the carpenter, the son of Mary?* Justin Martyr explains it a bit more clearly: || *For he performed these works of carpentry while among men, making plows and yokes, teaching by these both the symbols of righteousness and an active life.* <note>original Greek and Latin: Ταῦτα γὰρ τὰ τεκτονικὰ... Fabrilia namq;...</note> Baptized at the age of 30, he prepared himself to undertake his office; he openly announced the doctrine he had brought down from heaven, confirmed it with miracles, and appointed Apostles and other preachers of the Gospel. In the year 33, nailed to the cross for the salvation of the human race, he rose from the dead on the third day; after spending forty days with the Apostles, surrounded by a guard of angels, he returned to heaven.

Among the ecclesiastical writers, he holds the leading position—not so much because of the <gloss>divinely inspired</gloss> <note>original Greek: θεόπνευστα</note> Gospels, much of which were spoken from his own mouth and written down entirely at the dictation of his Spirit, as for that famous letter to Abgar of Edessa, <gloss>short in lines, but great in power</gloss> <note>original Greek: ὀλιγόστιχον μὲν, πολυδύναμον δὲ</note> (to use the words of Eusebius <note>A 4th-century historian often called the "Father of Church History"</note>), which was taken from the archives of Edessa. This exists in Greek and Latin along with Abgar’s letter to the Lord in Eusebius, Book 1, Chapter 13. Quite a few modern writers argue these letters are forgeries: among the Roman Catholics, Erasmus, Coster, Canus, and Bellarmine; among the Reformed, Coke, Rivet, Hoornbeeck, Spanheim the Younger, and others. But I believe such a monument of venerable antiquity should not be cast aside so rashly. First, in the entire context of the letter, no trace of fraud or <gloss>spuriousness</gloss> <note>original Greek: νοθεύσεως</note> appears; there is nothing that does not perfectly suit the wisdom, goodness, and dignity of our Lord. Even Rivet himself does not deny <margin>* Sacred Criticism, Book 1, Chapter 1</margin> that there is nothing in them to justify their rejection. Second, the great faith and authority of Eusebius carries much weight with me; he copied these letters from the actual archives of the people of Edessa and translated them from the Syriac language into Greek. Since he could not easily be deceived himself, he should not be judged as wanting to deceive us. Pearson <margin>† Defense of Ignatius, Part 1, Chapter 8</margin> truly notes of him: *Good God, what a judge! I truly contend that Eusebius used such diligence and judgment in examining the writings of early Christian antiquity, in which he believed the Apostolic tradition was contained, that no one has ever afterward doubted his faith or the writings he held as certain.* Third, Saint Ephrem—himself a Syrian, highly skilled in Syriac writings, and more importantly, a Deacon of Edessa—gives credit to Eusebius. I will present what he has in his *Testament*, translated from the Syriac language into Greek, and then into Latin by the translator Gerard Vossius, where he addresses his fellow citizens of Edessa as follows: || *Blessed be your city in which you live. For Edessa itself is a city and mother of the wise; indeed, she was openly and clearly blessed by the mouth of Christ the Lord through his disciples, our Apostles. For when King Abgar, who built this city, asked that he might receive the Savior of all and Lord Christ, who had appeared as a stranger on earth, he said: "I have heard all that has been done by you, and whatever you have suffered from the Jews who reject and despise you. Come here therefore and live with us; for I have this small city for myself which will suffice for you and me." The Lord, admiring his faith, sent word there through messengers and perpetually blessed that city, strengthening its foundations.* Count Darius also cites these letters in a letter to Saint Augustine <margin>* Letter 263, Louvain edition, in Baronius, year 31</margin>, to say nothing of more recent writers.

<margin>
* Sacred Criticism, Book 1, Chapter 1.
<br>
† Defense of Ignatius, Part 1, Chapter 8.
</margin>

<margin>
|| Works, Volume 3, and in Surius, February 1, Number IV.
</margin>

</margin>

As for the fact that writers older than Eusebius do not mention them, that happened because

<header>The Apostolic Century.</header>
<sig>B 2</sig>

<margin>
1st CENTURY from the BIRTH OF CHRIST
〰〰〰〰
</margin>
<margin>* John 20:30, 21:25.</margin>
<margin>† Exercises against Baronius 13. chapter 31.</margin>
<margin>* Origins of the Church volume 1. latter part. page 63.</margin>
<margin>‡ On the Harmony of the Evangelists book 1. chapters 9, 10. volume 4. 31.</margin>

because the Greek Fathers, being for the most part ignorant of the Syriac language, had almost no commerce with the people of Edessa. Nor are the arguments brought forward to the contrary of almost any weight; I will recall the primary ones for examination. **Objection 1.** No mention of these letters, or of the mission from Abgar, is found among the Gospel writers. I admit this; for it was not their intention to commit to writing everything that Christ did. Indeed, Saint John clearly testifies <gloss>*</gloss> *that Jesus did many other things, which are not written in this book, and which if they were written one by one, I suppose that even the world itself could not contain the books that should be written.* 21:25. **Objection 2.** A certain passage from the Gospel of Saint John is cited in this letter, which was not composed until many years later. I respond: A similar short sentence is found in both places, yet not in the same words, nor in exactly the same sense. For our Lord seems to have used such <note>original: Apophthegmatibus</note> pithy sayings frequently. But even if the sentiment were the same, it is not necessarily cited from there. Indeed, a passage from the oracles of the prophets seems to be cited, which the most learned Valesius <note>Henri Valois, a 17th-century historian of the early Church</note> refers to the sixth chapter of Isaiah; others look for it elsewhere. But I would judge that the word <term>it is written</term> <note>original Greek: γέγραπται</note> is to be understood not so much as referring to a particular place, but as the common voice of the prophets; in which sense it often occurs in Clement of Rome and others. **Objection 3.** The chronological account does not hold up. For these things are said (according to the common reading of Eusebius) to have been done in Edessa in the forty-third year of Christ. But the famous Valesius happily removed this entire difficulty; he showed, partly from more corrected manuscript copies of Eusebius and partly from the true Edessene Era, that it was the three-hundredth and fortieth year of the Edessenes (for so it was to be read), in which Christ both suffered and rose from the dead. Having set forth and confirmed our opinion in this manner, I nevertheless submit everything to the judgment of the more learned, nor will I "pull the rope of contention" with anyone on this matter. I gladly embrace the wonderful modesty of the great Casaubon and his successor in the <note>The "Baronian arena" refers to the intellectual battles over the *Annales Ecclesiastici* written by Cardinal Caesar Baronius</note> Baronian arena, Richard Montague. *Concerning the Letter of Abgar, Governor of Edessa, to the Lord,* says Casaubon <gloss>†</gloss>, *I would prefer to hear the judgments of the learned than to pronounce anything myself.* R. Montague says: <gloss>*</gloss> *Nor is there anything in them why they should be rejected. All things are pious, modest, and joined with Christian piety. But however they seem to be estimated to me, let each one abound in his own sense.* Finally, the reader should be warned that certain letters of Christ addressed to Peter and Paul, explaining those arts by whose power he had performed his miracles, were still accustomed to be circulated in the age of Augustine; but he rightly rejected them as the most stinking dreams and ravings of the foolish <gloss>‡</gloss>.

### SAINT PETER

SAINT PETER, previously called *Simon*, son of Jonah or John; a Galilean by country, a native of Bethsaida, called from the craft of fishing to the Apostolic office by the Lord in the Year of Christ 31. After Christ was taken up from human affairs in the Year of the Common Era 33 (the 19th year of Tiberius not yet completed), he himself, having delivered a speech, converted three thousand of the Jews. He healed the lame man in the portico of the Temple; he pleaded his cause twice before the Sanhedrin. In the following year, as it seems, being sent by the Apostles to Samaria, he overthrew Simon Magus and confirmed the Samaritans in the faith. In the year 37, peace having now been restored to the churches, passing through all regions, he came down to Lydda, healed Aeneas the paralytic, raised Tabitha of Joppa from the dead, and stayed for many days at the house of Simon the Tanner. In the following year he converted Cornelius, and returning to Jerusalem (on account of the Passover solemnity, as the famous Dodwell <note>Henry Dodwell, a scholar of ancient chronology</note> conjectures), and being accused because he had preached to the Gentiles, he vindicated himself by giving an account before the Church. In the year 44, having been cast into chains by Herod, he was freed by an Angel. In the year 47, a question having arisen concerning circumcision, he took part in the Council of Jerusalem and was the first to deliver an opinion. In the year 48 he came to Antioch, where he was most sharply rebuked by Saint Paul because of his pretended agreement with the Jews and because he did not walk with a straight foot according to the truth of the Gospel. What became of him after that is not clear: for the Sacred Scriptures make no further mention of him; and indeed, after the Apostolic History falls silent about him, almost all things that are handed down are uncertain. Concerning his double journey into the city <note>Rome</note> (the first of which is said to have happened in the year of Christ 42, the 2nd year of Claudius), and of his 25 years in it...

<summary>This page evaluates the authenticity of the Christ-Abgar letters, defending them as pious if not strictly canonical, and begins a chronological biography of Saint Peter's ministry up to his rebuke by Paul at Antioch.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the chronological account of the Apostles, specifically addressing the historical debates surrounding Saint Peter's travels and his eventual arrival and martyrdom in Rome.</meta>

<header>The Apostolic Century.</header>

<margin>1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST</margin> Regarding his <note>Referring to Saint Peter.</note> Bishopric in the City <note>Rome</note>, and his travels into Africa and the furthest shores of the West, as well as other stories commonly circulated, the reliability of the historical annals suffers miserably. I will freely offer my own opinion on this matter, which I published fourteen years ago. After Christ’s Ascension into Heaven, it appears the Apostles did not depart from the borders of Jerusalem for twelve years. For Apollonius, 
<margin>|| In Eusebius, book 5, chapter 18.</margin> a writer of the second century, expressly testifies that the Lord commanded this to his Apostles, based on ancient tradition. This tradition is supported by a matching passage in Clement of Alexandria, a contemporary of Apollonius, in the *Stromata*, Book 6, page 636. There, he cites this testimony of Peter, namely that the Lord said to the Apostles: *If anyone of Israel wishes to repent and believe in God through my name, their sins will be forgiven after twelve years. Go out into the world, so that no one may say, "We did not hear."* <note>original Greek: "Ἐὰν μὲν οὖν τις θελήσῃ τοῦ Ἰσραὴλ μετανοῆσαι... μετὰ δώδεκα ἔτη. Ἐξέλθετε εἰς τὸν κόσμον..."</note> And indeed, all the editions I have seen so far present this passage punctuated in this way. In my judgment, this is most flawed and yields no clear sense. For I ask you, what sense do these words make: *their sins will be forgiven after twelve years*? Let the words be punctuated in this manner instead, and everything flows smoothly: *If anyone of Israel wishes to repent and believe in God through my name, their sins will be forgiven. After twelve years, go out into the world, so that no one may say, "We did not hear."* <note>The author is correcting a likely transcription error where a period was misplaced, changing the meaning from "forgiveness after 12 years" to "wait 12 years before preaching to the world."</note> This was precisely the command the Lord gave the Apostles: that they should first preach Gospel forgiveness to the Jews, and having performed this duty among the Israelites for twelve years, they should then go out to the Gentiles and announce the Gospel to the whole world. Therefore, once this twelve-year period had passed, the Apostles began to be dispersed. After his meeting with Paul held at Antioch, Peter propagated the Gospel through various provinces of the East and Egypt for several years, especially among the Jews. Finally, under Nero, perhaps around the year 63, he came to Rome. He organized the faithful he found there, established and grew the Church, and soon ennobled it with his own blood. I have witnesses of noble antiquity, Lactantius and Origen, stating that Peter came to Rome around this time. 
<margin>* On the Deaths of the Persecutors, Section 2.</margin> Lactantius *, after stating that the Apostles had laid the foundations of the Church throughout all provinces and cities for twenty-five years up to the beginning of Nero's reign, immediately adds: *And when Nero was already reigning, Peter came to Rome, and having performed certain miracles by the power of God himself... he converted many to justice and established a faithful and stable temple to God. When this matter was reported to Nero... he was the first of all to persecute the servants of God, and he fixed Peter to a cross and killed Paul.* Origen, after discussing Peter's preaching to the Jews in the <term>Diaspora</term> <note>The "scattering" of Jewish people outside of Israel.</note>, has these words, 
<margin>† Volume 3 on Genesis, in Eusebius, book 3, chapter 1.</margin> which he mentions receiving as handed down by his ancestors: *Who, having finally arrived at Rome, was crucified head-downwards; for he himself had asked to suffer in this way.* <note>original Greek: "ὃς καὶ ἐπὶ τέλει ἐν Ῥώμῃ γενόμενος ἀνεσκολοπίσθη κατὰ κεφαλῆς..."</note> Saint Peter died, therefore, in the year of Christ 64, the 10th year of Nero, at the beginning of the Neronian Persecution, as we have discussed more at length in our work in the common tongue <note>A reference to the author's English publication, "Lives of the Apostles."</note> regarding the lives of the Apostles. For when Nero had incurred the hatred of all because of that great fire in the city of Rome, Tacitus 
<margin>* Tacitus, Annals 15, page 279.</margin> says *that to abolish the rumor, he shifted the blame onto the Christians, and for this reason afflicted them with the most exquisite punishments.* No one of sound mind can doubt that the Apostles were snatched away to martyrdom on this occasion, especially since no other persecution occurred in Rome between the times of Nero and Domitian. And perhaps, not without some shadow of plausibility, that capital crime <note>Arson</note> could be held against the Christians, and especially the Apostles, who had so often publicly declaimed in speech and writing about the future conflagration of the world and the punishment of the wicked by fire. Our Peter had done this extensively in his recently written second epistle. Indeed, 
<margin>† On True Wisdom, book 4, chapter 21.</margin> as Lactantius † witnesses, among other things that Peter and Paul preached at Rome, was this: that after a short time the entire Jewish nation was to be utterly destroyed, and finally everything would be laid waste by fire and sword. Therefore, since that Roman fire occurred when Gaius Lecanius and Marcus Licinius were Consuls, on July 19th, it must be judged that Peter underwent martyrdom in that very same year as it was drawing to a close. He was buried in the Vatican, where...

<meta>The text cuts off mid-sentence, transitioning to the next page with the catchword "witnesses" (teste).</meta>

<summary>This page discusses the timeline of Saint Peter's ministry, arguing that the Apostles stayed in Jerusalem for twelve years before dispersing. It concludes with Peter's arrival in Rome around 63 AD and his martyrdom during the Neronian Persecution following the Great Fire of Rome in 64 AD.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the historical analysis of Saint Peter’s life, moving from his ministry to the physical evidence of his burial in Rome and the scholarly debate over the origins of his letters and other writings attributed to him.</meta>

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST</margin>
According to the testimony of Gaius †, a Presbyter <note>An elder or minister.</note> of the Roman Church who lived at the beginning of the third century, his tomb or "trophy" <note>original: "tropæum"—a monument or shrine marking the site of a martyrdom.</note> was visible to anyone.

<margin>† In Eusebius, book 2, chapter 25.</margin>
He left behind two Epistles <note>Letters.</note>, addressed chiefly to Jewish Christians. The first of these was written in *Babylon*; many ancient and modern writers alike contend that this should be understood figuratively to mean Rome. However, considering both the facts and the timing, it is more fitting that the word <gloss>Babylon</gloss> <note>be understood literally</note>. This refers either to the Babylon in Parthia <note>Modern-day Iraq.</note>, where the name Babylon persisted even among its ruins (as is clear from Strabo, Pliny, Stephanus, and others), or to the Babylon located in Egypt, mentioned by Strabo, Ptolemy, Stephanus, and others. The Reverend Bishop of Chester <note>John Pearson (1613–1686), a famous English theologian.</note> gathered many details about this city in his *Dissertations on Succession*, Book 1, chapter 8, section 7. However, I embrace the former opinion as being far more likely, both because there was a great multitude of Jews in Parthian Babylon who maintained their "Captivity" <note>The Jewish community living in exile.</note> there, and because it was an easier and more convenient journey for Peter when departing from Antioch. No one, I think, will easily deny that he preached there for a long time; whereas not even the slightest shadow of antiquity can be offered to suggest he ever stayed in Egypt. This epistle was written, in the opinion of the illustrious Mr. Dodwell, in the year of Christ 54, after the conversion of the Asians and after Saint Paul’s three-year stay in Asia, at a time when the converted Jews in those parts were suffering persecution, which Peter himself discusses in 1 Peter 1:6, and which Saint Paul perhaps hints at in Hebrews 10:32.

<margin>Book 3, chapter 3.</margin>
The second Epistle, written in Rome (as it seems) shortly before his death, was for a long time not admitted into the Canon of Holy Scripture due to the difference in style from the first (as Jerome reports). <note>original Greek: "ὅμως δὲ πολλοῖς χρήσιμος φανεῖσα, μετὰ τῶν ἄλλων ἐσπουδάσθη γραφῶν"</note> "Nevertheless," says Eusebius, "appearing useful to many, it was studied diligently alongside the other scriptures."

There follow now the *Apocrypha* <note>Writings of "hidden" or doubtful authorship.</note> which were formerly circulated under Peter’s name. These are indeed forgeries, but ancient ones, seemingly from the Apostolic age or at least the one immediately following, which Eusebius discusses in the passage cited above. Such was the *Preaching* [Kerygma], fragments of which can be seen in Clement of Alexandria’s *Miscellanies*, Book 6; and the *Selections* from the same published by Combefis; as well as in the *Summary of Theodotus*; and in Origen’s commentaries on Matthew and John. It is also cited by Gregory of Nazianzus, John of Damascus, and Maximus. It is even cited by the author <margin>* Among the works of Cyprian, p. 142.</margin> of the *Book on the Rebaptism of Heretics*, a contemporary of Cyprian; so that, along with Valesius, we may rightly wonder why Eusebius says it was cited by none of the ancient or recent Church writers. Furthermore, the same passage that we mentioned John of Damascus brought from the *Preaching of Peter* is presented to us in Greek by Cotelerius, cited from Peter's *Doctrine*, so that the *Preaching* and the *Doctrine of Peter* seem to be one and the same book. There are the *Acts of Peter*, mentioned by Origen; the *Gospel*, which Serapion, Bishop of Antioch, proved to be false against the heretics who used it (according to Eusebius); and the *Apocalypse* or Revelation, which Sozomen reports <margin>* Book 7, chapter 19.</margin> was still accustomed to be read once a year in certain churches of Palestine in his time, specifically on the day of Preparation <note>Good Friday.</note>. Certain fragments from this book are shown in the later excerpts of Theodotus at the end of Clement of Alexandria’s works. Ruffinus also mentions the *Judgment of Peter* in his *Exposition of the Creed*, though he identifies the same booklet with the *Hermas* or *The Shepherd*. Finally, the reader should note that the passage about our Lord appearing to his disciples after the Resurrection and speaking of an <note>original Greek: "δαιμονίῳ ἀσωμάτῳ"</note> "incorporeal demon," which Ignatius brings forward in his *Epistle to the Smyrnaeans*, was taken from the *Gospel according to the Hebrews* according to Jerome †; <margin>† On Illustrious Men, ch. 16.</margin> however, Origen cites it from a booklet called the *Doctrine of Peter* (though he denies it is by Peter or anyone else inspired by the Spirit of God). Perhaps it was contained in both. To note this in passing: in the first centuries of the Church, many such <note>original Greek: "ψευδεπίγραφα"—falsely attributed writings</note> pseudepigrapha were circulated, mostly fabricated by heretics who were busy trying to win authority for their trifles by using the venerable name of some Apostle. Hence, there were fabricated everywhere "Gospels," "Traditions," "Revelations," and "Teachings" of the Apostles and Apostolic men; some of these we have noted above, and others we shall note below.

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>
1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST
</margin>

...certain things even here in this place, for which a more convenient spot was lacking. For in former times, there were circulated the *Gospels of the Twelve Apostles, of Thomas and Matthias*, and the *Traditions of Matthias*, a fragment of which is shown by Clement of Alexandria in his *Stromata*, book 2, page 380. There were also the *Acts of Andrew and John*, and finally the *Gospels of Bartholomew, Barnabas, Jude, and Philip*; of these, besides their names, hardly anything has survived. Furthermore, there exists a *Mass inscribed with the name of St. Peter*, published in Paris, 1595, in octavo, and in the Supplement to the Library of the Church Fathers, 1624, Volume 2; however, it is clearly evident that it is a work of a more recent age.

<margin>33.</margin>
ST. PAUL, previously called Saul, a Cilician by birth from the city of Tarsus, a Jew by descent, of the tribe of Benjamin, a Pharisee by sect, and a <term>tentmaker <note>original: "skēnopoios"—someone who crafts tents, a trade Paul used to support his ministry.</note></term> by trade. He was well-educated in secular literature and trained in Hebrew law at the feet of Gamaliel <note>A celebrated Jewish teacher and Pharisee mentioned in the Acts of the Apostles.</note>. While he burned with an immense zeal for his ancestral religion, he was converted to the faith of Christ in a truly miraculous manner. He became a Disciple and Apostle at the end of the year 33 AD, or at least at the beginning of the next. To escape the animosity of the Jews, he soon withdrew into Arabia and remained there for nearly three years; in the year 36, he returned to Damascus. Having escaped from a Jewish ambush there, he went to Jerusalem and met with Peter, as noted in Galatians 1:18. The following year, driven out by the Hellenists <note>Greek-speaking Jews.</note>, he set out for Tarsus, from where in the year 42 he was brought to Antioch by Barnabas; they stayed there for an entire year, according to Acts 11:25–26. In the year 44, during a pressing famine, they carried alms to Jerusalem, and soon after, by the command of the Holy Spirit, they were ordained to preach the Gospel to the Gentiles. In performing this duty, they traveled through various places during the two following years, which are discussed in Acts 13 and 14. In the year 47, they took part in the Council of Jerusalem and returned from there to Antioch. In the following year, Paul, taking Silas with him, again prepared himself for the work of the Gospel; St. Luke teaches us where he traveled in Acts 15, 16, and 17. In the year 49, he came to Corinth and there found Aquila and Priscilla, as seen in Acts 18:1–2. Having stayed in Corinth for a year and a half, in the year 50 he sailed to Syria, then sought Jerusalem, and soon returned to Antioch. At the beginning of the year 51, he came to Ephesus, where he spent three years (Acts 18:21, 31). From there he went into Macedonia and Achaia (Acts 19:21; 20:1). In the year 54, having set out for Corinth again, he then went to Jerusalem; St. Luke discusses this journey in Acts 20. While he was at the temple in Jerusalem, he was seized by the Jews and accused before Felix <note>Marcus Antonius Felix, the Roman procurator of Judea.</note>, by whom he was cast into chains and detained for two years. In the year 56, when he had appealed to Caesar, he was sent to Rome by Festus (the successor of Felix), and as winter had already set in, he landed at the island of Malta; St. Luke accurately describes the entire course of this voyage in Acts 27. In the year 57, he was led to Rome and kept in "free custody" <note>A form of house arrest where the prisoner could receive visitors.</note> for two years under the care of Burrus, the Prefect of the Praetorian Guard, a very good man full of kindness. In the year 59, released from prison, he returned to his usual duty of preaching the Gospel, and in the following years he traveled through Asia, Crete, and perhaps some places in the West. Finally, in the year 63, or at the beginning of the next, he returned to Rome, where on the occasion of the Great Fire of Nero <note>A fire in 64 AD which Nero blamed on the Christians, leading to their persecution.</note>, he was beheaded in that same year. He suffered martyrdom, as St. Clement says in his letter to the Corinthians, <term>under the rulers <note>original: "tōn hēgemonōn"—this Greek phrase is debated by scholars.</note></term>; that is, interpreted by Patrick Young <note>A 17th-century Scottish scholar and librarian.</note> as *under the Emperors*; but perhaps more correctly by our countryman Henry Dodwell <note>A famous English theologian and historian (1641–1711).</note> as *under the Praetorian Prefects*. This is because in place of Burrus, who died in the year 62, two Praetorian Prefects were appointed, Faenius Rufus and Sophonius Tigellinus, as witnessed by Tacitus in his *Annals*, book 14, page 257.

There are *14 Epistles* written by him which are extant: nine addressed to Churches gathered from the Gentiles, four concerning pastoral care and office, and one to the Hebrews. We shall review these in the order in which they are held in the sacred canon.

The *Epistle to the Romans* was written during Paul's second arrival at Corinth in the year 54, in which mention is made of Narcissus, who died in this very year.

The *First Epistle to the Corinthians* was written while he was staying at Ephesus in the year 53, before Pentecost and before he came into Macedonia, as noted in chapter 16, verses 5 and 8.

The *Second Epistle to the Corinthians* was sent, as it seems, at the end of the same year while he was lingering in Macedonia; it was sent by Titus, whom he had sent there to stir up the diligence of the Corinthians in collecting alms for the second time.

The *Epistle to the Galatians*, in which he strongly defends himself and the Gospel doctrine against the slanders of Judaizing heretics. The famous Henry Dodwell conjectures that it was written in the year 53, and indeed before the first epistle to the Corinthians, while the Apostle <term>stayed for a time <note>original: "epeche chronon"—a reference to Paul's delay or stay in Asia.</note></term> in Asia.

<meta>catchword: Epi-</meta>

It also begins a scholarly review of the dating and context of Paul's epistles, referencing historical figures like Burrus and Tacitus.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the chronological survey of the Apostolic age, specifically focusing on the timeline and authenticity of the letters (Epistles) attributed to the Apostle Paul, transitioning from his general biography to a detailed bibliography.</meta>

# The Apostolic Century.
<meta>catchword: Style</meta>

<margin>
1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST
~~~
</margin>

*The Epistle to the Ephesians*, delivered, as it seems, at Rome in the year 59; around the same time that he was released from his first Roman imprisonment.

*The Epistle to the Philippians*, written in the same place and at the same time.

*The Epistle to the Colossians*, whom Epaphras, sent by Paul, had converted to the faith; he therefore sent this Epistle through him from Rome in this same year, 59.

*The First Epistle to the Thessalonians*, written at Corinth in the year of Christ 49, immediately after Silas and Timothy had arrived there. See Acts 18:5 and 1 Thessalonians 3:6.

*The Second Epistle to the Thessalonians*, written a little after the first, though the location is not quite so certain.

*The First Epistle to Timothy*, whom he had left at Ephesus, written in the year 63, while he was traveling from Macedonia to Rome for the second time, or at least toward the West.

*The Second Epistle to the Timothy*: the illustrious <note>Henry Hammond (1605–1660) was a prominent English churchman and pioneering biblical critic.</note> Hammond contends it was written at Rome during Paul's first imprisonment. But that most learned man is entirely mistaken; for anyone who seriously weighs the context of the whole epistle and all the circumstances of this time cannot doubt that it was written in the year 64, shortly before the Apostle's martyrdom.

*The Epistle to Titus*, whom he had ordained as the primary Bishop of Crete, given in the year 63 from some location between Macedonia and Nicopolis, for he had decided to spend the winter there.

*The Epistle to Philemon*, a man of no small reputation among the Colossians, to whom Paul wrote in the year of Christ 59, just as he was about to leave Rome.

*The Epistle to the Hebrews*, written in the year 59 after a two-year custody in Rome, while he was still staying in Rome or at least in Italy. Indeed, it does not bear the name of Paul on its face like the others; nevertheless, almost all learned men agree it is his. Certainly, the Ancients—cited in Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea was a 4th-century historian often called the "Father of Church History."</note> book 3, chapter 38, and Jerome <note>St. Jerome was the 4th-century scholar who translated the Bible into the Latin Vulgate.</note> in *On Illustrious Men*, chapter 5—judged that it was composed by Saint Paul in Hebrew, and translated into Greek by Luke the Evangelist, or Saint Clement, or, as others say, by Barnabas. However, it was rejected by some, as the same Eusebius testifies (book 3, chapter 3), because it was not held as a certain and genuine epistle of Paul by the Roman Church.

---

## SPURIOUS WORKS.

The *Epistle to the Laodiceans*: Tertullian, in *Against Marcion* (book 5, chapters 11 and 17), understands this to be nothing other than the *Epistle to the Ephesians*. From this, Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century bishop and counter-heresy writer.</note> in *Heresy 42* also presents a certain passage cited by Marcion <note>A 2nd-century heretic who rejected the Old Testament and much of the New Testament.</note> from the "Epistle to the Laodiceans" which is found in our current Epistle to the Ephesians. Indeed, a Greek *Epistle to the Laodiceans* exists today, but it is obvious to everyone that it is a forgery. It is found in Hutter's *Twelve-Language New Testament* (Nuremberg, 1599) and from there in Johannes Leusden’s *The Hebrew-Greek Philologist, Dissertation 4*, and perhaps more than once elsewhere.

The *Anabatikon* <note>original: "Ἀναβατικὸν"—meaning an "Ascension" or "Going up."</note>, or *Ascension*, which the Caian heretics <note>The Cainites, a Gnostic sect.</note> falsely attributed to Paul on the occasion of his <term>rapture <note>original: "harpagēs"—referring to Paul's account of being "caught up" to the third heaven in 2 Corinthians 12.</note></term>. The Gnostics made much of it; regarding this, see what Epiphanius has in *Heresy 38*, number 2.

<margin>† Book 7, ch. 19.</margin>
The *Apocalypse*, which Sozomen <note>A 5th-century historian of the Christian Church.</note> mentions † as being greatly praised by credulous monks even in the fourth century; it was falsely said to have been found in a marble chest dug up in Saint Paul's house at Tarsus by divine warning, and it gained extraordinary veneration among the Easterners. Augustine not undeservedly mocks it at the end of *Tractate 98 on John*.

<margin>
|| *On First Principles*, book 1, ch. 2.
* Book 3, ch. 3.
</margin>
The *Acts of Paul* are mentioned by Origen || and Eusebius *; however, they do not count them among the writings of undoubted faith.

*Six Epistles to Seneca*, together with *Eight from Seneca to Paul*: held in some value in the age of Augustine and Jerome, they are found in Sixtus of Siena’s *Holy Library*, book 2, and with the treatise *On the Four Cardinal Virtues* (London, 1523, quarto; Venice, 1588, octavo). That these are mere rubbish is evident even at first glance; indeed, to linger over refuting such trifles would be the work of a man abusing both his own and his reader's leisure. Furthermore, Clement of Alexandria, in his *Stromata* (page 636), cites certain sayings of Paul which are not found in his Epistles.

It also lists and dismisses various apocryphal or forged works attributed to Paul, such as the Ascension of Paul and his supposed correspondence with the philosopher Seneca.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Apostle Paul’s literary legacy, specifically focusing on the debate surrounding his writing style, before transitioning into a biographical and bibliographical account of St. Matthew and his Gospel.</meta>

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>
1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST
* Book 3, chapter 24.
† Book 3, chapter 7.
* Heresies 64, number 29.
</margin>

The Pauline style is judged by the Ancients to be quite unrefined and simple; nevertheless, it breathes a certain gravity and sometimes even eloquence. <note>original: "Πάντων ἐν παρασκευῇ λόγων δυνατώτατος, νοήμασί τ' ἱκανώτατος"</note> "He was the most powerful of all in the preparation of discourse, and the most capable in his thoughts," as Eusebius <note>Eusebius of Caesarea (c. 260–339 AD) was a pioneering Christian historian.</note> says of him. Among all the Apostles, he easily excelled in both the richness of his vocabulary and the weight of his sentiments. Regarding his manner of writing, Irenaeus <note>A 2nd-century bishop and theologian.</note> once wrote: He frequently uses <term>hyperbata</term> <note>A rhetorical device where the natural order of words is transposed for emphasis or due to strong emotion.</note> because of the speed of his words and the spiritual impulse that is within him. Epiphanius <note>A 4th-century bishop known for his "Panarion" against heresies.</note> says of him: <note>original: "Ὥσπερ ἀκονητὴς..."</note> "Just as the Apostle, like a most skilled hurler of javelins, flinging the piercing words of his speech into the midst, has produced a work that seems obscure and hidden in depth—yet it is most true, orthodox, and contains nothing careless or ill-omened. For to those who approach his words without diligence or struggle, he seems to say things that are disconnected and inconsistent; but to those who apply themselves with a sober and wakeful mind, his words are full of order and truth." Indeed, the Apostle, like a most skilled archer, sending forth twisted sentences of words, established an oration that, while obscure and concealed in the deepest senses, is most true and in agreement with the right faith, containing nothing said negligently or absurdly. For those who encounter his speeches not studiously but with a yawn sometimes think he says things that are inconsistent and incoherent. But those who apply themselves with a sober and alert mind judge that everything was pronounced in proper order and with the truth of the matter. For how boldly Jerome <note>St. Jerome (c. 347–420 AD), the translator of the Vulgate, was a famously sharp critic.</note> wields his censorial rod against the Pauline style, you may see if you wish in his letters to Algasia and Hedibia, and his commentaries on Ephesians and Galatians.

<margin>
* Page 22.
* Book 3, chapter 24.
† Book 2, chapter 45.
</margin>

ST. MATTHEW, also called Levi, was perhaps a Galilean by birth; and if we are to trust the Arabic author of his life published by Kirstenius <note>Peter Kirstenius (1577–1640) was a German scholar of Oriental languages.</note>, he was a native of the city of Nazareth, born to a father named Ducu and a mother named Karutia, from the tribe of Issachar. From being a <term>publican</term> <note>A tax collector, a profession often despised by the Jewish population for its association with Roman occupation.</note>, Christ called him to the Apostleship. After Christ was taken up into the heavens, Matthew preached the Gospel doctrine throughout Judea for a full twelve years; from there he went to Asiatic Ethiopia <note>In ancient geography, "Ethiopia" often referred broadly to regions in Africa or parts of southern Asia like India.</note>, and there in the city of Naddaver (or as others say, Hierapolis in Parthia), he suffered martyrdom around the year 60. He was the first who committed the Gospel to writing. For when he had first preached the faith to the Hebrews and was about to set out to other nations, he wrote his Gospel in his native language <note>Hebrew or Aramaic</note>, supplying by writing to those he was leaving behind that which seemed to be missing from his physical presence, as Eusebius teaches us. And indeed, the Ancients hand down with a truly wonderful consensus that he wrote his Gospel history in Hebrew; so much so, that it would be a crime to go against the testimony of almost all of antiquity in this matter. Concerning this, see what we have noted elsewhere in his Life. He is commonly said to have written his Gospel in the 8th year after Christ's resurrection; however, the author of the *Alexandrian Chronicle* and Nicephorus refer this to the 15th year after Christ's assumption. And surely, since the Apostles were not permitted to leave the borders of Judea before the 12th year after Christ's Passion, they departed for their respective lots only after the 15th year (the year 48 AD), once the Council of Jerusalem had finished; thus it seems Matthew composed his Gospel shortly before that time.

<margin>
* On Illustrious Men, chapter 3.
† Ecclesiastical History, book 2, near the beginning.
</margin>

This Gospel—though foully corrupted in many places—was later used almost exclusively by the Nazarenes, Ebionites, etc. <note>Early Jewish-Christian groups who often rejected certain Pauline doctrines and used variant versions of Matthew.</note>. Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a Dutch legal scholar and theologian.</note> presents fragments of it collected from ancient writers in his *Annotations on Matthew*, chapter 1; to these may be added those omitted by him which exist in Origen <note>A 3rd-century theologian from Alexandria.</note>, one quite long in his *Treatise on Matthew*, and another shorter one in his *Commentary on John*. From this same source seems to be taken that saying of Christ: <note>original: "Ἐν οἷς ἂν ὑμᾶς καταλάβω, ἐν τούτοις καὶ κρινῶ"</note> "In whatever things I find you, in those I will also judge you," which is found in Justin Martyr's *Dialogue with Trypho*, in a certain homily of Origen on Jeremiah, and likewise in Augustine. The martyr Pamphilus placed a copy of this Gospel in his library at Caesarea; the Nazarenes also gave Jerome the opportunity to transcribe another copy at Beroea in Syria. I will finally add what Theodore the Reader says: that under the reign of Emperor Zeno, the relics of the Apostle Barnabas were found in Cyprus, and upon his chest was placed the Gospel of Matthew, copied by Barnabas's own hand. Zeno deposited it in the palace in the Church of St. Stephen. Sebastian Münster <note>A famous 16th-century Hebraist.</note> boasted that he had published the Hebrew Gospel of Matthew. However, it is well enough established that the entire work came from his own workshop (or at least from some impostor's), disguised under a false title. Who [translated] the Gospel of Matthew into Greek...

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>
1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST
* P. 493.
† Preface to Matthew, p. 2.
</margin>
...since he translated the Discourse <note>Referring to the Gospel of Matthew.</note>, whether it was some Apostle or a companion of the Apostles who did so remains uncertain (the author of the *Athanasian Synopsis* <note>A summary of Holy Scripture traditionally attributed to Athanasius, though its authorship is debated.</note> attributes it to St. James the Less, while Theophylact attributes it to St. John). Certainly, the Catholic Church has consistently used the Greek copy.

Finally, there is a Liturgy inscribed to St. Matthew found in Latin in the *Library of the Fathers* (under the name of the *Universal Canon*); this work, if any is, is spurious—the offspring of a more recent century. Also among the *Works of Jerome*, Volume IV, is the *Book on the Origin or Nativity of the Blessed Virgin*, the preface of which grandly but falsely claims it was translated into Latin by Jerome from a work written by Matthew in Hebrew.

## ST. JAMES the Apostle, called the *Minor* <note>Known as James the Less.</note> to distinguish him from James the Great who was killed by Herod; surnamed the *Just*, he was the "brother of the Lord," being the son of Joseph from a previous wife, or as it pleases Chrysostom and Jerome, the son of Mary, the sister of the Lord's mother.

<margin>
31.
* On Famous Men, ch. 2.
† Epistle 117.
</margin>
After Christ’s ascension into heaven, he was consecrated as the first Bishop of Jerusalem by the Apostles (<note>original: "θεοποιῷ τῇ χειρὶ"</note> "by the deifying hand," says Photius, meaning Christ directed the hands of the Apostles). At the instigation of Ananus the younger, the High Priest, he was condemned by the Sanhedrin <note>The supreme council and tribunal of the Jews.</note> and cast down from the pinnacle of the temple. Still half-alive, he died after being struck on the head with a fuller’s club <note>A heavy wooden tool used by cloth-washers or "fullers" to beat fabric.</note> in the year 61 AD. A man venerable even to the Jews themselves for his piety and the supreme holiness of his life—so much so that Josephus <note>Flavius Josephus (c. 37–100 AD), a Romano-Jewish historian.</note> himself admits that those evils which afflicted the Jews during the siege of Jerusalem happened for no other reason than the crime committed against James.

<margin>
|| In Jerome, On Famous Men, ch. 2.
* Book 7, ch. 19.
† Place cited.
</margin>
Eusebius reports that his Episcopal throne was preserved until his own time and held in great veneration. Jerome includes certain details about him from the *Gospel of the Nazarenes*. Shortly before his death, it seems, he wrote a *Catholic Epistle* <note>The "General" or "Universal" Epistle of James in the New Testament.</note> to the Jews <note>original: "ἐν τῇ διασπορᾷ"</note> "in the dispersion" who had primarily professed Christian doctrine; he did this to teach that the profession of the Christian faith is utterly useless unless it is joined with purity and holiness of life. Regarding this work, Eusebius says: <note>original: "ἱστορητὴ, ὡς νοθεύεται"</note> "it is recorded that it is considered spurious," etc.

<margin>
* Book 2, ch. 23.
</margin>
Indeed, it should be known that it was rejected as if it were *spurious*. Certainly, very few of the ancient writers made mention of it; but gradually, as time went on (as Jerome says), it obtained authority. There also exists under his name a *Proto-evangelium* <note>An early "Gospel of James" focusing on the birth and childhood of Mary and Jesus.</note>, which deals chiefly with the births of our Lord and the Blessed Mary; at its end, he testifies that he withdrew into the desert during the riot stirred up in Jerusalem by Herod, and wrote it there. Guillaume Postel <note>A 16th-century French polymath and orientalist.</note> first brought it from the East, strenuously contending it was a genuine work of James and boasting that it was read publicly in the Eastern Churches as being of indubitable faith; indeed, he did not hesitate to stitch its nonsense to the beginning of the Gospel of St. Mark, as noted by Ussher in his *Theological Library MS*, p. 11. But anyone can see that the work is supposititious <note>Fraudulently substituted; not genuine.</note>. It is available in Greek and Latin in the *Orthodoxographa*, Vol. 1, p. 71. Finally, it is cited by the Sixth Council in Trullo <note>The Quinisext Council of 692 AD, held in the domed hall (Trullo) of the palace in Constantinople.</note>, Canon 32; and there still exists under the name of our James a *Liturgy in Greek* (Paris, 1560; and the 1624 Supplement). However, it seems to have been fabricated not long before that Council. That it is not by James, nor from the Apostolic age, is clearly shown by the words frequently used in it: *incense*, *altar*, *sacrificial gates*, *Trisagion* <note>A standard hymn in the Eastern Orthodox liturgy.</note>, the *Cherubic hymn*, *Mother of God*, *consubstantial*, *Temples*, *Monasteries*, *Confessors*, and other terms born and devised long after the time of St. James. This is further demonstrated by the rituals and formulas of prayer, which are entirely foreign to the Apostolic age, as will be obvious to anyone looking closely. It must seem truly remarkable that men who are otherwise most learned—such as Leo Allatius in his published dissertation on this matter, and Cardinal Bona in *Liturgical Matters*—defend this liturgy as a genuine work of St. James, although they do not deny it was interpolated by later hands, with some things added or removed or changed as the occasion required.

<margin>
* Greco-Roman Law, Book 5, Answer 1, p. 363.
</margin>
Theodore Balsamon <note>A 12th-century canonist and Patriarch of Antioch.</note> rejects this Liturgy, as well as the one by St. Mark, in his *Inquiry of Mark, Patriarch of Alexandria*, where he notes that they are mentioned neither in the 85th Apostolic Canon nor the 59th Canon of the Synod of Laodicea (which list the writings of both Testaments and the Apostolic books we ought to use), nor are they recognized by the Church of Constantinople. "We declare, therefore," he says, "that these are not to be received. For even if they had been made by them, <note>original: "παντελῶς ἀπραξίας κατεψηφίσθησαν, ὡς καὶ ἄλλα πολλὰ"</note> they were condemned to be entirely without use, just as many other things were," which he demonstrates with many points in the following sections.

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>
1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST

**ST. JUDE**, the Apostle, also known as *Thaddaeus* and *Lebbaeus*, was the brother of St. James the Less and the son of Joseph <note>Some traditions identify the "brothers of the Lord" as sons of Joseph from a previous marriage.</note>. See Matthew 13:55. He taught not only among the Jews, Galileans, and Samaritans, but also among the Idumeans, Syrians, Arabs, and those of Mesopotamia. Finally, in Persia, because he freely criticized the superstitions of the Magi <note>Followers of the ancient Persian religion, Zoroastrianism.</note>, he was crowned with martyrdom around the year 61, as some believe. It is well established that he wrote his Canonical Epistle after the Second Epistle of Peter, as he incorporated many elements from it into his own, often retaining the very same words. *Eusebius*† <note>A 4th-century historian often called the "Father of Church History."</note> testifies that few of the ancients mentioned this Epistle, nor that of James. *Jerome*† adds that it was rejected by many because of the testimony cited in it from the apocryphal Book of Enoch; nevertheless, he notes it deserved authority through its antiquity and long usage, and it is counted among the Holy Scriptures. The illustrious *Grotius*† <note>Hugo Grotius (1583–1645), a famous Dutch jurist and scholar known for his critical approach to the Bible.</note> departs from the consensus, attributing this Epistle to a certain Jude "the Younger," the 15th Bishop of Jerusalem, who flourished in the time of the Emperor *Hadrian*. To make his conjecture seem more plausible, he claims that the words appearing at the beginning of the Epistle—[*Brother of James*]—were added by later scribes. However, he asserts this without the evidence of any ancient manuscript and without the support of any ancient witness; therefore, he is by no means to be listened to in this matter. Indeed, he is openly contradicted by *Epiphanius*\* <note>A 4th-century bishop known for his massive catalog of heresies.</note>, whose authority *Grotius* elsewhere† praised marvelously.

<margin>
31.

† Book 3, ch. 1.

* On Prescription against Heretics, ch. 36.

† Sermon on St. John, Vol. 6, p. 505.
* Synopsis on St. John, p. 157.

† Observations, Book 2, ch. 11.

* Commentary on the Apocalypse, ch. 1.

† Heresies, 30.
</margin>

**ST. JOHN**, Apostle and Evangelist, a *Galilean* by birth, the son of *Zebedee* and *Salome*, and the younger brother of *James the Greater*. He was called by Christ from the fishing boat in which his father had raised him to the "Fishing of Men" (that is, the Apostolate) in the year 31. He was the disciple whom the Lord loved above all others, who leaned against His chest, and to whose care and protection Christ commended His mother while He was dying. *Eusebius*†, citing *Origen*, records that in the distribution of the world's provinces made by the Apostles, *Asia* <note>Asia Minor, roughly modern-day Turkey.</note> fell to him; there, he successfully fulfilled the duties of his office with the greatest care and Apostolic diligence. At last, when persecution broke out in the year 95 (the 14th year of *Domitian*), he was accused before *Domitian*, sent to *Rome* by the proconsul, and there cast into boiling oil. According to *Tertullian*\*, he emerged unharmed. After this event, he was banished to the island of *Patmos* in the Aegean Sea at the end of that same year. Following the death of *Domitian*, he returned to *Ephesus* in the year of Christ 97. There, and in the surrounding regions, he spent the remainder of his life. Near the end of the Apostolic century, in the year of Christ 100 (the 3rd year of *Trajan*), according to *Eusebius* and *Jerome*, he peacefully fell asleep in the Lord, being a year or two over one hundred years old (though *Chrysostom*† and *Dorotheus*\* say he died at age 120). However, the *Alexandrian Chronicle*—perhaps much more correctly—dates his death to the year 72 <note>This likely refers to 72 years after the Ascension, or perhaps a different chronological era.</note>. Regarding the time of his death, *Henry Dodwell* <note>An influential 17th-century theologian and scholar.</note> has noted many things accurately and eruditely, as is his custom, in his *Additions to Pearson's Dissertations*. I pass over what some of the ancients hand down regarding his burial and tomb, which are to be counted as fables rather than history. While he was on *Patmos* in the year of Christ 96 (the 15th year of *Domitian*), he wrote the *Apocalypse* <note>The Book of Revelation.</note>, in which he depicts the future state of the Christian Church as shown to him in many visions. In a certain monastery on this island, according to *Bellonius*†, the hand of his corpse is preserved to this day, the nails of which grow back whenever they are cut. The Greeks assert this is the hand of St. John with which he wrote his *Apocalypse* there. Regarding this book and its author, many of the ancients long doubted; some even rejected and refuted it entirely, contending it was neither a true Revelation nor that John was its author. Indeed, they argued that no Apostle, nor even any holy or ecclesiastical man, was the creator of this book (on this matter, see *Dionysius of Alexandria* in *Eusebius*, book 7, ch. 25). However, it was eventually received by the universal consent of the whole Church as the genuine offspring of the Apostle John. The conjecture of the celebrated *Grotius*\* seems not improbable: that the work was ascribed by some to *John the Presbyter* <note>A figure mentioned by early writers like Papias, whose identity as distinct from the Apostle is a long-standing scholarly debate.</note>, a disciple of St. John, because it was committed to his safekeeping by the Apostle. At the request of the Bishops of Asia, near the end of his life (around the year 100), he wrote his Gospel, partly to refute the emerging blasphemies of *Cerinthus* and the *Ebionites* <note>Early groups who held "heretical" views; Cerinthus taught a distinction between the human Jesus and the divine Christ, while Ebionites often denied Christ's divinity.</note>, who asserted that Christ did not exist before Mary (which is why he treats primarily those things which serve to demonstrate the divine nature of Christ); and partly to supply the deficiencies of the other Evangelists, which he indeed did (concerning which, see *Eusebius*, book 3, ch. 24). I shall add only what *Epiphanius*† has, the Gos-

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>
1st CENTURY FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST

* Questions on the Gospels, book 2, ch. 39. See the index of Possidius.
† Found in Eusebius, at the cited location.
</margin>

The Gospel of John, translated from the Greek language into Hebrew, was preserved in the treasuries of the Jews of Tiberias, stored away among the hidden books <note>original Greek: *en apokryphois*</note>. He also wrote three Epistles, the first of which—which according to St. Augustine * was once addressed to the Parthians <note>The Parthian Empire was a major Iranian political and cultural power in ancient Persia.</note>—is called "Catholic" <note>Meaning "Universal," intended for the whole Church rather than a specific congregation.</note>; and concerning this one, there was never any controversy. The latter two were formerly ascribed to a certain "John the Younger," who also lived in Ephesus. However, common consensus and the mature judgment of the Church later approved them as his, although they are not yet held as genuine by the modern Syriac Church. He seems to have written these three Epistles around the year 97 AD, after his return from Patmos; the first while he remained at Ephesus; the second when he already intended a visitation of Asia; and the third while he was still on that very journey. Regarding the style of St. John, Dionysius of Alexandria † once noted that in the Gospel and the first Epistle, he not only spoke correctly, but also applied the greatest elegance in his words, his arguments, and the entire composition of his speech; it is understood that the Lord granted him these two gifts: knowledge and eloquence together. But in the Apocalypse <note>The Book of Revelation.</note>, his diction is found not to be very Greek, but rather stained with barbarisms and occasionally solecisms <term>Barbarisms and solecisms: grammatical errors or the use of non-standard, unrefined linguistic structures</term>. Notwithstanding the judgment of this most holy man, it is known to everyone that his diction abounds with Syriacisms <term>Syriacisms: idioms or sentence structures derived from the Syriac or Aramaic language</term>, and that he employs certain peculiar and exotic words which are not found in the other sacred writers.

<margin>34.</margin>
**NICODEMUS**, a Pharisee <term>Pharisee: a member of a Jewish sect known for strict adherence to traditional law</term>, famous among the leaders of the Jews, and later a disciple of Christ, whom he first approached by night (John 3:1–2). Later, he defended Him in the Sanhedrin <note>The supreme judicial and administrative council of the Jews in Jerusalem.</note> when He was accused by the Pharisees (John 7:50), and he brought the body of the crucified Lord, seasoned with spices, into the tomb (John 19:39). He flourished from the year 34 AD. Nothing certain is known regarding his death. There exists under his name a *Gospel* concerning the passion and resurrection of Christ, which was said to have been found in the Praetorium of Pontius Pilate in Jerusalem during the reign of Emperor Theodosius the Great, and translated from the Hebrew into the Latin language (though it is easily established that this is a forgery). It is found in Latin in the *Orthodoxographa*, p. 643. It had appeared earlier in Leipzig in 1516, in quarto format. Whelock, in his notes to the *History of Bede* (pp. 77–78), teaches us that it exists in the Saxon language <note>Old English.</note> in the Library of St. Benedict at Cambridge.

<margin>
34.

* Theodorus Lector, book 2.
† On Modesty, ch. 20.
* Book 3, ch. 25.
† Second Lecture on the Acts of the Apostles, §. 10.
* Introduction to *Sacred Varieties*, vol. 1.
</margin>

**ST. BARNABAS**, previously called Joses or Joseph, was a Cypriot by birth and a Levite by descent. He was one of the seventy disciples, who sold the field he possessed as his own property, and offered the money, laying it at the feet of the Apostles for the use of the nascent Church. Having been ordained as an Apostle to the Gentiles together with St. Paul (and thus his deeds, which are mentioned in the Apostolic History, are mostly recounted in the life of Paul), he traveled through various provinces of the East, founded churches, and established those already founded. There are those who maintain that he preached at Rome and even laid the foundations of the Church at Milan, becoming the first Bishop of that see. Others maintain that he taught in Alexandria. It is probable that toward the end of his life he was crowned with martyrdom in Cyprus, his native island; where later, during the reign of Zeno, his remains were found *. Formerly, the *Epistle to the Hebrews* was ascribed to him, as is clear from Tertullian †. There also exists today under his name an Epistle in Greek and Latin with a style quite obscure and involved, which is almost entirely concerned with questions and arguments against the Jews; but it is counted by Eusebius * among the "spurious" works <note>original Greek: *ta notha*</note>. Here, as the illustrious Valois rightly notes, the "spurious" books are not entirely fraudulent, but only those which are of ambiguous authority and were not admitted into the Canon of Holy Scripture; these he had previously called the "disputed" books <note>original Greek: *tas antilegomenas*</note>. This is also clearly evident from what he says in the same place concerning the Epistles of James, Jude, 2 Peter, 2 and 3 John, the Apocalypse, etc. Certainly, whatever modern critics may say about this Epistle, the Ancients constantly ascribe it to our Barnabas. "There was certainly no one," says our great Bishop of Chester † <note>John Pearson (1613–1686), Bishop of Chester and a renowned theologian.</note>, "who did not attribute this Epistle to Barnabas, nor does anything appear in it that does not fit that age." The arguments brought to the contrary by the illustrious Stephen Le Moyne * are light and of no weight. "If it were the genuine offspring of the Blessed Barnabas," he says, "I do not see why it should not have been included among the Canonical Scriptures: the dignity of the parent—namely the Apostle Barnabas, the companion of the Apostle Paul, who deserved so well of the Church just like the other Apostles—would have [given] it a place..."

<summary>This page discusses the authorship and linguistic style of St. John's works, the life and "spurious" Gospel of Nicodemus, and the missionary travels and disputed Epistle of St. Barnabas.</summary>
