<meta>This page continues the discussion regarding the authorship and textual history of the early Christian writings, specifically focusing on the Epistle of Barnabas, before transitioning to the history of the heretic Simon Magus.</meta>

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>
1ST CENTURY
FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST
</margin>

...would undoubtedly have claimed a place among the sacred books. But could not the same be said of St. Clement’s Epistle to the Corinthians, which no one, as far as I know, has ever denied to be genuine? He objects, secondly, that the Epistle was written after the destruction of Jerusalem, as it mentions the temple being overturned and destroyed, long after Barnabas had been removed from human affairs. I confess the author of the Epistle hints at this, but obscurely. But how does he know for certain that Barnabas did not prolong his life until the year 70 AD, when that Jewish total destruction <note>original Greek: *panolethria*</note> occurred? Certainly, the ancients do not deny it, and among them there is deep silence regarding the time of his death; nor does the man’s age prevent us from believing that he reached his eightieth year. Thirdly, he objects that many things in it are recounted as being derived from Apocrypha and Jewish gaps <note>referring to non-canonical traditions or oral legends</note>, in a manner entirely unusual for the Apostles. However, this objection strikes equally at the Epistle to the Hebrews and the Epistle of Jude, where many things are taken from the Apocryphal Book of Enoch; to say nothing of the second Epistle to Timothy, in which *Jannes and Jambres* <note>Two magicians mentioned in Jewish tradition who opposed Moses; they are named in 2 Timothy 3:8 but not in the Old Testament.</note> are mentioned. Finally, as for the opinion of a very learned man that this Epistle should be attributed to Blessed Polycarp, it is indeed a new but plainly weak conjecture. The Epistle of Barnabas clearly reveals its author to be of Hebrew origin and imbued with no small knowledge of his religion, and therefore it fits Barnabas the Levite perfectly; and indeed, in both subject matter and writing style, it differs from that of Polycarp as much as "the heaven is from the earth" <note>original Greek: *hoson ouranos est’ apo gaies*</note>. Nor does he have any other foundation for his opinion than that in most manuscript codes <note>handwritten copies</note> it is attached to the Polycarpian Epistles without any author's name. Hence, having finally abandoned his own conjecture, he admits that he presents this only as a hesitant suggestion, and does not command his conjecture to be accepted as an "authoritative opinion" <note>original Greek: *kyrian doxan*</note>. But whatever happens with the author, whether it was Barnabas or someone else, almost everyone agrees that the Epistle itself is very ancient and was born in the Apostolic age; for this reason, it was frequently cited as genuine by Clement of Alexandria and Origen. <margin>* Book 1, *Against Celsus*.</margin> Indeed, Origen * did not hesitate to distinguish it by the title "The Catholic <note>Universal</note> Epistle of Barnabas." The Reverend James Ussher <note>An influential Anglo-Irish Archbishop and scholar known for his biblical chronology.</note> was the first to rescue this Epistle from darkness and submit it to the Oxford Press in 1643. However, before the printing reached its completion <note>literally "the navel," an idiom for the finishing touches</note>, the entire copy, along with the pages already printed, perished in a great fire. A significant part of the Greek manuscript that Ussher used can still be seen in his *Theological Library*, p. 530, where whatever could be found cited in Greek from the Epistle of St. Barnabas by ancient writers is compared with the Greek text of the manuscript placed opposite it; an old Latin version drawn from a manuscript was also added at the side so that a more accurate comparison could be established. In the year 1645, Hugues Ménard published it in *Paris (in quarto)* along with a very ancient version and most learned notes. The following year, the celebrated Isaac Vossius appended it to his Epistles of Ignatius, at *Leiden. In the year 1672*, Jean Cotelier inserted it into his *Apostolic Fathers*, published in *Paris*. It also appeared at Oxford in 1685 in *duodecimo*, in which edition whatever was missing in the Greek text was presented in Latin by an ancient translator, and whatever was missing in the translator was supplied from the Greek, marked in red ink <note>original: *minio*, referring to red lead or vermilion used for rubrication</note>. Most recently, Stephen Le Moyne, a theologian from Leiden, presented it illustrated with very extensive commentaries among his *Sacred Miscellany*, *Leiden, 1685, in quarto.*

---

<margin>35.</margin> SIMON, called *the Magician* <note>Simon Magus</note>, a Samaritan by birth, originating from the village of Gitton, flourished in the year 35 and thereafter. He achieved such great authority among his countrymen through trickery and magic arts that they thought him to be the great power of God. Finally, struck with amazement by the miracles of Philip the Deacon preaching among the Samaritans, he pretended to profess the Christian faith and was initiated through Baptism. Soon, intending to bid for the gift of miracles by offering money to St. Peter, he was sharply rebuked by the Apostle on that account and rejected with these words: *May your money perish with you.* Taking this rejection badly, Simon defected from the Christian faith and returned to his usual poison. As time went on, he set out for Rome, where through trickery and sorcery he turned the faces and eyes of everyone toward himself. But having been followed to Rome by St. Peter, he was convicted of imposture in a debate and reduced to silence: and (if some later historians are to be believed) he was punished with a wretched death through Peter’s prayers. Among the Jews he boasted that he was the son of God, or the Messiah; among the Samaritans he took on the persona of God the Father;

# The Apostolic Century.

<margin>
1ST CENTURY
FROM THE BIRTH OF CHRIST
</margin>

...among the Gentiles he claimed for himself the title of the Holy Spirit. He devised many other portents of error and blasphemies, which Irenaeus, Epiphanius, Theodoret, and others enumerate one by one. To put it briefly, he led the way for the Valentinians, the Gnostics, and the entire plague of heretics, as much by his doctrines as by his example. He wrote (according to the author of the *Apostolic Constitutions*, Book 6, Chapter 16) most wicked books, filled everywhere with his poisonous opinions, which he falsely attributed to the venerable names of Christ and the Apostles. Cleobius did the same (according to the same witness), being himself the founder of a heresy not much different from that of Simon Magus. However, the nonsense of both has long since perished.

---

<margin>
40 AD.
\* Book 2, ch. 17.
† On Illustrious Men, ch. 11.
|| Codex 105.
\* Antiquities of the Jews, Book 18, ch. 10.
† Book 2, ch. 18.
\* Codex 105.
</margin>
PHILO, a Jew by birth, an Alexandrian by nation, and of priestly descent, was the brother of Alexander the Alabarch <note>The Alabarch was the chief magistrate or official of the Jewish community in Alexandria, often responsible for collecting customs duties.</note> (who was the highest magistrate of the Jews at Alexandria). He flourished in the year 42 of the common Christian Era, during the reign of Gaius Caligula. He was sent to Rome as the leader of an embassy to defend the cause of his people against Apion <note>Apion was a grammarian and critic of the Jews who had incited Caligula against them.</note>. But the somewhat hostile Gaius forbade him to speak; indeed, he even brought him into danger of his life. When Philo turned to his people, he told them they ought to be of good heart; for while Gaius was indeed angry with them, he had in fact made God his own enemy. When he came to Rome a second time to see Claudius (if the reports of Eusebius \*, Jerome †, and others are true), he held a conversation with the Apostle Peter and cultivated his friendship. Photius || adds that he was even initiated into the Christian sacred rites, but eventually fell away from them out of some grief and anger. He devoted himself to the school of Plato, and so thoroughly internalised his philosophy and eloquence that it became a common saying: *Either Plato speaks like Philo, or Philo speaks like Plato* <note>original Greek: Ἢ Πλάτων φιλωνίζει, ἢ φίλων πλατωνίζει</note>. He was a man "illustrious in all things," as Josephus says \*. "He was truly great in speech, and broad in thought, reaching high and lofty heights in the contemplation of the divine scriptures; he produced a varied and multifaceted guidance on the sacred words," as Eusebius says of him †. He was, as he says, a man copious in speech, rich in ideas, sublime and exalted in his understanding of divine scripture; he published a varied and manifold exposition of the sacred volumes. He especially pursued the allegorical sense <note>The allegorical method interprets the literal text of scripture as a set of symbols for deeper spiritual or philosophical truths.</note>; indeed, every allegorical discourse on sacred scripture, in the opinion of Photius \*, flowed into the Church from him. He wrote excellent and innumerable works, which were held in such high esteem by everyone that they were judged worthy to be consecrated in the public libraries of the Romans. His works survive in Greek from the edition of Turnebus (Paris, 1552, folio); in Latin by Gelenius (1561); in Greek and Latin (Geneva, 1613, folio); and in an enlarged edition with clearer type (Paris, 1640, folio), in this order:

| | |
|:---|:---|
| On the Creation of the World. | On the Life of Moses; namely, on Theology and the Prophets, in 3 books. |
| Allegories of the Laws, in 2 books. | On Charity. |
| On the Cherubim, the Flaming Sword, and Cain. | On the Judge. |
| On the Sacrifices of Abel and Cain. | On the Creation of a Prince. |
| That the Worse is Wont to Plot against the Better. | On Fortitude. |
| On Agriculture. | On the Decalogue. |

...virtues. In this book, he treats the customs and institutions of certain Ascetics whom he calls the *Therapeutae* <note>A Jewish sect of the Hellenistic period similar to the Essenes, living near Lake Mareotis in Egypt.</note>; Eusebius contends through a long series of arguments that these were actually Christians in Egypt converted from Judaism by the work of Saint Mark. How correctly he does so, others may judge. To me, certainly, it seems otherwise; I have set forth my reasons more extensively elsewhere <margin>\* Life of the Apostles, in the Life of St. Mark, Number 3.</margin>.

It is found in Latin in the "Micropresbyticon," Basel, 1550, page 295, among trifles of this sort, Lyon, 1560, 12mo, and elsewhere.*
*A Brief Account of Times, divided into two parts. It begins from Adam and ends in the year of Christ 78. Philo supposedly wrote it, but Joannes Annius of Viterbo <note>A notorious 15th-century scholar known for forging "missing" ancient texts.</note> illustrated it with commentaries and took care to have it published in Latin among other writers of the same sort, Rome 1498, folio; reprinted at Paris, 1515, folio, and frequently elsewhere. Of the same character is the book On the Genealogy of Christ, which exists in Latin with the commentaries of Annius, Paris, 1612.*

<margin>
40 AD.
\* On the Life and Death of the Saints, ch. 84.
</margin>
SAINT MARK, the Evangelist (whom some mistakenly confuse with John Mark, mentioned in Acts 12:12), was a Levite by birth, and became famous after the year 40. He was a disciple and interpreter of Peter, and according to the testimony of Isidore of Seville <margin>\*</margin>, his son in baptism, and thereafter his constant companion, having been sent by him into Egypt. Having been made the first Bishop of Alexandria, he underwent martyrdom there, though in what year remains unknown to me. At the request of the brothers in Rome, he wrote a Gospel approved by Peter (whence it is also called the Gospel of Peter), and did so in the Greek language, which was familiar enough to the Romans. This was done around the year 65, after Peter and Paul had already been taken by death; for since Paul had summoned him to Rome in the second epistle to Timothy, written not long before his martyrdom, it is probable that Mark came there either in the same year or at least the following, and there either first composed his Gospel or published what had been composed earlier. Certainly Irenaeus (Book 3, ch. 1, and cited in Eusebius Book 5, ch. 8) expressly records that Saint Mark wrote his Gospel <term>μετὰ τὴν τούτων ἔξοδον</term> <note>original Greek: "meta tēn toutōn exodon," meaning "after their departure" or death.</note>, after the death of Peter and Paul. However, as for Eusebius saying the Gospel was approved by Peter, that should perhaps be understood regarding the plan of writing and the evangelical matters communicated to him by Saint Peter while he was still among the living. In composing his Gospel, he follows the order of Matthew and, having added a few things, follows in his footsteps almost everywhere. Jerome testifies <margin>† To Hedibia, question 3, vol. 3, p. 143.</margin> that the last chapter of this Gospel was formerly missing in nearly all the Greek manuscripts. Regarding his style, Chrysostom observed <margin>\* Homily 3 on Matthew, near the beginning.</margin> that he studied to imitate his master Peter, who delighted in a brevity of words. There are those who maintain that Mark wrote in Latin; others add <margin>|| Cornelius à Lapide, preface to Mark, p. 562.</margin> that his Latin autograph is preserved to this day at Venice in the Library of St. Mark. Both claims certainly deserve equal credit from me <note>The author is being sarcastic; he believes neither claim.</note>. More worthy of notice is what Jerome narrates <margin>\* Against the Pelagians, Dialogue 2.</margin>, namely that in certain copies, especially Greek ones, a hardly orthodox conclusion <note>original: "coronidem"</note> was added to the end of Saint Mark’s Gospel, which he presents to us in writing. As for the Liturgy attributed to Saint Mark and published under his name in Greek and Latin at Paris in 1583 and in the Supplement of 1624, Vol. II, anyone can see it is spurious and far later than the Apostolic age. Otherwise, that John Mark, of whom Acts 12 speaks, is said by Sigebert of Gembloux to have written on the passion of the Apostle Barnabas...

## SAINT LUKE

A native of Antioch and a physician by trade (Saint Metaphrastes, Gretser, Nicephorus, and others relate wonderful stories about his skill in painting <note>Church tradition frequently identifies Luke as the first iconographer, credited with painting the first portrait of the Virgin Mary.</note>), he was converted by Saint Paul, likely while he was staying in Antioch. He was thereafter Paul’s follower and companion on every journey; once Paul was released from his Roman chains, Luke preached in Dalmatia, Galatia, Italy, Macedonia, and perhaps elsewhere. He lived a celibate life and died at the age of 84, around the year 70 (as some believe), though the specific manner of his death is uncertain. Nicephorus writes that he was hanged from a fruitful olive tree; Dorotheus says he died at Ephesus; the Roman Martyrology records that he suffered martyrdom in Bithynia; others say Rome, others elsewhere. Certainly, the Arabic writer of his life found in Kirstenius <note>Peter Kirstenius (1577–1640), a scholar of Arabic and medicine.</note> maintains that he suffered in Rome, and that not long after Saint Paul's first release from prison; he gives the reason that if Luke had lived longer, he would have extended the history of the Acts of the Apostles further. Whether this reasoning is sufficiently solid, let the learned judge. He learned the Gospel doctrine from Saint Paul and the rest of the Apostles, and from their accounts he composed his Gospel. In this work, he describes many things more fully and abundantly than the other Evangelists, treating especially those matters pertaining to Christ’s priesthood. He also wrote—but here as an <term>eyewitness</term> <note>original Greek: "αὐτόπτης" (autoptes)</note>—the Acts of the Apostles, which encompasses the origins and growth of the Gospel Church, especially the labors, struggles, and travels of Saint Paul (whose inseparable follower he was), up to the two-year imprisonment in Rome, where he concludes his history. He dedicated both works to Theophilus, a nobleman (as is reasonable to believe) of Antioch; Theophylact calls him a <term>man of senatorial rank and perhaps a ruler</term> <note>original Greek: "συγκλητικόν ἄνδρα, καὶ ἄρχοντα ίσως" (synklētikon andra, kai archonta isōs)</note>. He seems to have written both in the year 59 AD, while Saint Paul was not yet released from prison. Regarding his style, Jerome, reflecting the mind of the ancient writers, says this: "The ancient commentators of the Church hand down that the Evangelist Luke was most skilled in the art of medicine, and knew Greek letters better than Hebrew. Therefore, his language in both the Gospel and the Acts of the Apostles is more elegant and smacks of secular eloquence." And elsewhere: "Luke, who among all the Evangelists was the most learned in the Greek tongue, being a physician and one who wrote his Gospel for the Greeks; because he saw he could not translate the property of the language [from Hebrew idioms], he thought it better to remain silent than to put down something that would raise questions for the reader." Jerome also mentions in his entry on *Luke* a certain book entitled <term>The Journeys</term> <note>original Greek: "Περιόδων" (Periodōn)</note> of Paul and Thecla; he relates, on the authority of Tertullian, that a certain Asian presbyter, moved by love for Saint Paul, forged the work and confessed this to Saint John, for which reason he was removed from his office. Whether these *Journeys* were forged under Luke’s name, Jerome does not explicitly say.

<margin>
Year 50.
* Under the entry for "Dionysius."
* Cited in Eusebius, book 3, chapter 4, and book 4, chapter 23.
† See October 9.
</margin>

## DIONYSIUS,

An Athenian by birth, of whom Suidas says: <term>a most eloquent man, having reached the very peak of Greek education</term> <note>original Greek: "ἀνὴρ ἐλλογιμώτατος, καὶ τῆς ἑλληνικῆς παιδείας εἰς ἄκρον ἐληλακώς" (anēr ellogimōtatos, kai tēs hellēnikēs paideias eis akron elēlakōs)</note>, a most illustrious man, advanced to the highest summit of Greek discipline. He devoted himself to the study of the humanities first at Athens, then at Heliopolis in Egypt; where (if the writers of his life are to be believed), having witnessed that miraculous eclipse of the sun which happened at the time of Christ’s passion, he is said to have exclaimed: <term>either the deity is suffering, or he suffers with the sufferer</term> <note>original Greek: "ἢ τὸ θεῖον πάσχει, ἢ τῷ πάσχοντι συμπάσχει" (ē to theion paschei, ē tō paschonti sympaschei)</note>. Having returned home, he was elected to the senate of the Areopagus <note>The high court of Athens, named for the hill where it met.</note>, from which he took the name "Areopagite." Around the year 50 AD, while Paul was preaching at Athens, he embraced the Christian faith. Moreover, Dionysius, the Bishop of the Corinthians, records that he was appointed the first Bishop of the Athenians. Indeed, if Suidas, Nicephorus, Syncellus, and others are to be believed, he was ordained by the hands of Paul himself. Finally, after a most famous confession of faith and the most severe types of torture, the Athenian philosopher Aristides reports in his Apology that he was crowned with a glorious martyrdom—if indeed Usuard and Ado cite it truly in their Martyrology. At what time he died—whether under Domitian, Trajan, Hadrian, or (as seems likely) under Domitian—is not clear. Concerning the two Dionysiuses, one of Paris and the other of Athens—or as others say, one and the same Dionysius <note>A common medieval conflation between the Athenian convert and Saint Denis, the martyred bishop of Paris.</note>—I will do nothing here. Whether our Dionysius left anything in writing behind him, whatever others may say, [is] not sufficiently...

<margin>Year 50.</margin>
## MARCELLUS

A Roman, son of Marcus, the city prefect; he was a disciple of the Blessed Peter, having formerly been a listener of Simon Magus. He flourished, so it is said, around the year 50 and thereafter, finally being crowned with martyrdom. However, these accounts are entirely fabulous, supported by no ancient author. Sigebert of Gembloux, Trithemius, Possevinus, and others record that he wrote *The Conflict between Saint Peter and Simon Magus*; *The Acts of Peter and Paul*; *The Magical Arts of Simon Magus*, addressed to Saints Nereus and Achilleus; *The Death of Saint Petronilla, daughter of the Lord Peter the Apostle*; *The Passion of Saint Felicula*; *The Passion of Saint Nicomedes*; and certain other things concerning the deeds of the Saints. A fragment of the booklet *The Conflict of Saint Peter with Simon Magus* <note>original: "Conflictu S. Petri cum Simone M."</note> exists at the end of the *Library of the Popes* by Louis Jacob de Saint-Charles, page 487. For my part, if it were worth the effort to indulge in conjectures, I would think this booklet should rather be attributed to Marcellus I, Bishop of Rome from the year 304, who died in the year 309.

<margin>Year 59.</margin>
## THE PRIESTS OF ACHAIA

The Priests of Achaia, who were present at the martyrdom of Saint Andrew the Apostle in the year 59, are said to have written a letter concerning his passion. It survives in Latin in the works of Lipomannus and Surius under the entry for November 30th. Although it is ancient, and usually defended as legitimate by Bellarmine, Possevinus, Labbe, and others—as it is somewhat favorable to their dogma concerning the Eucharist—it is nevertheless rightly rejected as spurious by the far greater part of the learned world. Indeed, in ancient times, it was relegated to the rank of the Apocrypha by Saint Philastrius of Brescia, by Innocent I, and by Pope Gelasius. Certainly, Baronius seems to condemn it in more than one place: namely at the year 44, section 42, 43, and at the year 69, section 34. Nor do Bellarmine and Labbe themselves, even if they defend it, dare to pronounce it as certainly genuine.

<margin>Year 59. * On Greek History, book 2, chapter 9.</margin>
## ABDIAS

Abdias of Babylon is usually numbered among the ecclesiastical writers of this age. By birth he was a Jew and one of the seventy disciples; he saw our Lord in the flesh (if indeed his word is to be trusted), was present at many of the deeds and passions of the Apostles, and, having accompanied Simon and Jude as they departed for Persia, was there appointed by them as the first Bishop of Babylon. But these are all trifles, not mentioned even once by any of the Ancients. Even granting that Abdias ever existed in the nature of things, it is still much more uncertain that he wrote anything. For those ten books on the *History of the Apostolic Conflict* <note>original: "Historia Certaminis Apostolici"</note> which Wolfgang Lazius published at Basel in the year 1551 (in folio), under Abdias's name—taken from very old copies showing an age of nearly 700 years—and which were supposedly written by Abdias in Hebrew, translated into Greek by his disciple Eutropius, and finally translated into the Latin tongue by Julius Africanus, are so foul and fabulous that they hardly deserve a place even among the "wooden legends" <note>A term of derision for crude, unconvincing, or poorly written hagiographies.</note>. Hence, it should seem a wonder that these scraps so deceived Wolfgang Lazius, a man not unlearned and most diligent, that he did not hesitate to write in his preface that such a great similarity exists between the Acts of the Apostles and the first part of these books that one must believe either that Abdias took these things from Luke, or Luke from Abdias—since both lived at the same time and were disciples of the Apostles. Nor can I agree with the illustrious Vossius, who suggests that these books of Abdias were designated by Blessed Augustine in his work *Against Adamantius*, chapter 17, where he relates a certain short story about the Apostle Thomas from the Apocryphal Scriptures which is found in Abdias's ninth book. For there were many fables about the Apostles fabricated in Augustine's age and long before, partly written and partly passed down by word of mouth, which the stitcher-together of this history could have transferred into his own medley. Furthermore, at the very beginning of that book, the impostor affirms that he described what he has regarding the deeds of Saint Thomas from a certain older book which he happened upon, omitting the superfluous parts. This *History of Abdias* was also published at Cologne in 1569 (12mo), at Paris in 1571 (8vo), and is commonly found in the *Library of the Fathers*.

<margin>Year 64.</margin>
## LINUS

An Italian by nation, Tuscan by birth, and son of Claudia; he is mentioned by Saint Paul in the Second Epistle to Timothy written at Rome. After Paul and Peter...

<meta>This page continues the chronology of the first-century Roman bishops, specifically focusing on Linus and Clement. The previous page concluded the entry for the Priests of Achaia and began the discussion of Linus.</meta>

### The Apostolic Age

<margin>1st Century from the Birth of Christ. * Book 2, chapter 3.</margin>
"of the church of the Romans, he was the first to be allotted the bishopric," says Eusebius <note>original: "ἡ Ῥωμαίων ἐκκλησίας πρῶτος κληροῦται τὴν ἐπισκοπὴν"</note> *. He held the seat, according to our Cestrensis <note>John of Chester, a 14th-century English chronicler</note>, from the year of Christ 55 to the year 67. To me, however, it seems far more probable that the Roman Church, founded by Peter and Paul in the year 63 or 64, was first given Linus as bishop by them. "The blessed apostles, having founded and built up the church, entrusted the office of the bishopric to Linus," says Irenaeus || ; <margin>|| Book 3, chapter 3; and in Eusebius, book 5, chapter 6.</margin> *The Church having been founded and built, the most blessed Apostles handed over the office of the episcopate to Linus.* <note>original: "Fundata jam & ædificata Ecclesia, beatissimi Apostoli episcopatus officium Lino tradiderunt."</note> But he seems to have held the seat for only a very short time, and perhaps, as the author of the *Apostolic Constitutions* states <margin>* Book 7, chapter 47.</margin>, he died while Peter was still living, or at least died not long after the martyrdom of the Apostles, during the height of the Neronic Persecution. There is no need to remind the learned reader that in arranging the dates and succession of the first Pontiffs of Rome, there is a wondrous and almost irreconcilable discrepancy among Ecclesiastical writers, both ancient and modern. How great this confusion is, and where it originated, is dealt with extensively and professionally by Pearson and Dodwell in their recently published *Chronological Dissertations*. But I return to Linus. Chifflet * and others tell many fables about him. <margin>* Besancon part 2, chapter 1.</margin> He has a place here because of two small books on the passion of Saints Peter and Paul; although Sigebert <margin>† On Ecclesiastical Writers chapter 5.</margin> says Linus wrote them in Greek, those which we now have seem to be the work of some barbarous and <margin>* Greek History, Book 2, chapter 9.</margin> ancient monk, in the judgment of the illustrious Vossius *. Indeed, even as they now exist in print, they have been corrupted by heretics and have gathered some soot <note>figuratively: stains or distortions</note>; although Chifflet † testifies that they are still preserved intact in the very ancient Bifontine manuscripts. <margin>† In the place cited.</margin> They appear in the *Library of the Fathers*, volume 2, and in various other editions, to say nothing of the *Christian History* of Laurentius Barrius and the commentaries of Jacques Lefèvre d’Étaples on the Pauline Epistles, into both of which they are inserted. That they are spurious and of no credibility cannot be doubted. This is admitted even by Baronius in the year 69, Possevino, Giovanni Maria Brasichellano in the Roman Index he compiled, Bellarmine, and Labbe, not to mention others.

<margin>65.</margin>
## CLEMENT

Clement is said to have had Rome as his birthplace, at the Caelian Hill, born to his father Faustinus and mother Mattidia, though this is uncertain. He was a companion and fellow-worker <note>original: "synergos"</note> of Saint Paul, along with others, whose names *are written in the book of life*, Philippians 4:3. Origen calls him a disciple of the Apostle Peter; and it is probable that he labored together with Peter in building up the Roman Church. Concerning the time he arrived at the Roman see, there are various opinions of the learned, which I do not have the time to list here. I confess that, led chiefly by the authority of Tertullian and Eusebius, I was formerly of the opinion that Clement was ordained by Saint Peter and admitted to a part of the episcopal duty—namely, to preside over the Church of the Jews living in Rome—until Linus (whom Saint Paul had appointed Bishop of the Church of the Gentiles there) died, followed by Cletus; then, the Roman Church of both Jews and Gentiles being joined together, Clement succeeded to the care of the whole Church around the year 91; and finally, as Eusebius testifies, died in the third year of Trajan, the year of Christ 100, a matter which we have discussed at length elsewhere *. <margin>* Lives of the Fathers, volume 1, in the Life of Clement, number 4.</margin> But having now reconsidered the matter better, I rather agree with the illustrious Dodwell: namely, that Linus and Anacletus having been removed within a very short time, Clement succeeded to the Roman see from the year 64 or 65, and held it until the year 81, or as Pearson’s calculation has it, 83. Eusebius indeed, as we noted above, when he wished to make the third year of Trajan (100 AD) notable for the persecution raised at that time, also assigned the death of Clement to this year. Modern writers have added that he was exiled to Cherson in Crimea and crowned with martyrdom. But Eusebius does not mention the martyrdom of Clement, nor do Irenaeus, Clement of Alexandria, Tertullian, or others older than Eusebius; regarding this matter before the fifth century, there is not even a syllable <note>original: "ne γρῦ quidem", literally "not even a grunt."</note>. The origin of the whole fable seems to have flowed from this: that the first fabricators of this martyrdom rashly confused our Clement with Flavius Clemens the Consul, cousin of the Emperor Domitian, who underwent martyrdom for the faith of Christ around these times, as the illustrious Dodwell rightly observes. From this, the fable of his martyrdom, imperial lineage, and kinship with Augustus Domitian was born; which we also observed once before. I will add only what Irenaeus has to say,

->D<-

# The Apostolic Age

<margin>1st CENTURY from the BIRTH of CHRIST</margin>
...Irenaeus, in the place cited above, states that Clement was allotted the Pontificate <note>The office of the Pope or Bishop of Rome</note> in the third place from the Apostles. He had indeed seen the Blessed Apostles and lived familiarly with them, and still had the preaching of the Apostles ringing in his ears and their tradition before his eyes.

### GENUINE WORKS.

<margin>
* In Eusebius, book 5, chapter 6.
† Book 3, chapter 16.
* Codex 112.
|| On Illustrious Men, chapter 15.
† Place cited.
* Codex 126.
</margin>
The **Epistle to the Corinthians** was written in the name of the Roman Church on the occasion of a very grave schism spreading in the Corinthian Church. In it, he called them back to peace and harmony, and renewed their faith and the tradition they had recently received from the Apostles. Truly, this Epistle is adorned with wonderful praise by the Ancients; by Irenaeus * it is called "a most sufficient writing" <note>original: "ἱκανωτάτη γραφὴ"</note>, a most brilliant document; by Eusebius † "great and wonderful" <note>original: "μεγάλη τε καὶ θαυμασία"</note>, and "acknowledged by all" <note>original: "ἀνωμολογημένη παρὰ πᾶσι"</note>; by Photius * "noteworthy" <note>original: "ἀξιόλογος"</note>, to be highly valued; and by Jerome || "very useful." It was held in such high value by everyone that, as Eusebius † testifies, it was read publicly in most churches, both in his own time and in earlier memory. Regarding this work, Photius * judges thus: "his speech is simple and clear, approaching the ecclesiastical and unelaborated style of speaking" <note>original: "ἁπλῆ τις κατὰ τὴν φράσιν, καὶ σαφὴς ἐστὶν, καὶ ἐγγὺς τῆ ἐκκλησιαστικῆ, καὶ ἀπεριέργω χαρακτῆρος"</note>. Among scholars, there is not yet agreement on the time this epistle was written. Setting aside the opinions of others, I will bring forward only the conjecture of Dodwell <note>Henry Dodwell, a 17th-century scholar of church history</note>: namely, that it was written before Clement was established as Bishop of the city of Rome. For he refutes, in many words, the idea held by most that it belongs not to the time of Nero, but to the time of Domitian. He adds this in his *Additions to Pearson's Dissertations*, chapter 2, section 6, paragraph 24. Since, therefore, in the year of Christ 64, the Corinthian Church was suffering from a grave schism on account of certain "spiritual men" <note>original: "πνευματικῶν"</note> who, distinguished by extraordinary gifts of the Holy Spirit, were rising up against the ordinary governors of the Church, they sent Fortunatus (whom St. Paul calls the "first-fruits of Achaia" in 1 Corinthians 16:15), Claudius, and other delegates to the Romans, imploring their help and aid. Finally, in the following year, the Roman Church replied to the Corinthians—the seat perhaps not yet being filled because of the persecution and the recent slaughter of the Apostles. Hence, Clement writes not in his own name, but in the name of the Roman Church; hence, at the beginning of the epistle, he excuses himself for having turned his mind to their requests somewhat late, "on account of the calamities and adverse events that have happened to us" <note>original: "διὰ γενομένας ἡμῖν συμφορὰς καὶ περιπτώσεις"</note>. He means (as I believe) the persecution of Nero, which was even then scarcely settled. These points could be strengthened by more evidence, but I do not wish to dwell on them longer. These things seem most probable, yet we leave them to be discussed by the learned.

<margin>
* Book 3, chapter 38.
† Place cited.
|| Codex 112.
</margin>
There is a **Fragment of a Second Epistle**, or rather a Homily <note>A sermon or religious discourse</note>, which contains an admonition and instruction for a better life. (Cotelerius <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier, a French scholar</note> also presents another fragment of this Homily in his notes, taken from the parallels of John of Damascus, in Greek). However, Eusebius *, Jerome †, Photius ||, and others explicitly testify that this was not received and approved in the same way as the first, nor did the older writers ever use its testimony. Although there are some who strenuously defend it for our Clement, among them Gottfried Wendelin (who also added a Latin translation to this fragment) in his *Divination on the Epistles of Clement*, inserted into the most recent edition of the Councils, Volume 1. These Epistles, lost for so many centuries—after the learned world had despairingly mourned them—were first brought to light and illustrated with very learned notes by Patrick Young <note>Patricius Junius, librarian to King Charles I</note>, Oxford, 1633, in quarto. They were taken from the end of a very ancient Greek manuscript of the Holy Bible, described as being written by the hand of the Virgin Thecla (as they say), and sent as a gift by the Patriarch Cyril <note>Cyril Lucaris, Patriarch of Constantinople</note> to the Most Serene King of England <note>The famous Codex Alexandrinus</note>. They are also extant in an Oxford edition of 1677 in duodecimo (but with Young's notes omitted), in the first volume of the Councils (Helmstadt, edited by Joachim Mader, 1654), and with a collection of notes by Paul Colomiès (London, 1687). William Burton of London, author of the *History of the Greek Language*, translated this Epistle to the Corinthians into the English tongue and illustrated it with notes that are indeed not unlearned (London, 1647). Furthermore, it is not sufficiently clear what Jerome * means when, speaking of the Epistles written by Clement to the Corinthians, he says that Clement directed almost his entire discourse toward the purity of virginity...

<margin>
* Book 1, against Jovinian.
</margin>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Clement of Rome’s literary legacy, specifically addressing claims made by Saint Jerome regarding his writings on virginity, before transitioning into a detailed catalog of "spurious" works—writings attributed to Clement but likely composed by others in the early Church.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>

<margin>
1st CENTURY
from the BIRTH of CHRIST
</margin>
...[Jerome] wrote that [Clement] had woven together [such praises]. For it cannot be believed that Clement gave other Epistles to the Corinthians than these which now exist, nor in these do praises of virginity fill every page <note>literally "make both pages," an idiom meaning to be the dominant theme</note>. It seems rather to be said that Jerome, carried away by an excessive zeal for praising virginity, used hyperbolic language; and since Clement devotes only a few periods to teaching the purity of mind and body, Jerome wished to suggest that the whole discourse was dedicated to an encomium <note>a formal speech or piece of writing that highly praises someone</note> of virginity.

# SPURIOUS WORKS.

<margin>
* Book 6, chapter 13.
|| On Illustrious Men, chapter 15.
† Codex 111.
</margin>
**T**he **85 Apostolic Canons**. These Canons seem to have arisen from the common discipline, institutions, and decrees of the Primitive Church, and were reduced into a single <term>Synodicon <note>a collection of Church decrees or canons</note></term> around the end of the second century. They are called "Apostolic" not because they were composed by the Apostles themselves, but because they flowed from Apostolic doctrine and practice, or were published by Apostolic men. Whether they were first collected by Clement of Alexandria, who wrote <term>Concerning Ecclesiastical Canons <note>original: περὶ κανόνων ἐκκλησιαστικῶν</note></term>, as Eusebius \*, Jerome ||, and Photius † assure us (as our most learned William Beveridge quite sharply conjectures), let it be for others to judge. The same Beveridge explained these Canons individually and defended them against Jean Daillé and his defender, both in his *Annotations on the Pandects of Canons* and in his *Defense of the Code of Canons of the Primitive Church*, published in *London, 1678*. These Canons are contained in Greek and Latin in the *Collection of Councils*, Volume I, page 25.

The **Eight Books of Apostolic Constitutions** were written at roughly the same time as the Canons. They seem to have been first composed from various <term>teachings <note>original: διδασκαλίαις</note></term> and <term>ordinances <note>original: διατάξεσι</note></term> which Apostolic men were accustomed to publish at that time. It is well established that by the age of Epiphanius, these Constitutions had been heavily corrupted by heretics and were far different from those which once existed, which could easily have happened through additions, truncations, and interpolations. Francisco Torres was the first to publish them in Greek, adding his own introduction and scholarly notes in Greek (*Venice, 1563, in quarto*). They were published in Latin together with the notes of Carlo Borromeo (*Paris, 1564, in octavo*). The Greek and Latin version by Fronton du Duc, the most learned of the Jesuits in unearthing the monuments of the Greek Church, was published at the end of the *Commentaries of Zonaras* (*Paris, 1618, in folio*). From there, the illustrious Philippe Labbe transferred them into his newest edition of the *Councils*, volume I.

<margin>
† Philocalia, chapter 23, pages 81, 82.
* Preparation for the Gospel, book 6, chapter 10.
Lives of the Primitive Fathers, in Clement, volume 1, number 11.
</margin>
The **Ten Books of Recognitions** have been given various names: sometimes the *Acts*, *History*, or *Chronicle* of Saint Clement; sometimes the *Acts*, *Itinerary*, <term>Journeys <note>original: Περιόδοι</note></term>, or *Dialogues with Appion* of Saint Peter. The antiquity of these books is sufficiently proven by the fact that Origen † cites from them under the name of Clement’s *Journeys*, and these quotes are found word-for-word in today’s copies. There also exists a very large fragment of Bardesanes the Syrian from his *Book on Fate* in Greek in Eusebius \*, which is found today in the same words in the ninth book of the *Recognitions*. It is not entirely certain who received it from whom. What if Bardesanes himself was the compiler of these *Recognitions*? We have elsewhere set forth at length our conjecture on this matter, bolstered by our own reasons. They exist in Latin from the ancient version of Rufinus (who perhaps suppressed the blemishes in them formerly noted by Jerome and Photius), by the care of Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples (*Paris, 1541, 1544; Cologne, 1570*) and elsewhere more often with the rest of Clement’s Works.

**The Clementina**, or **Nineteen Clementine Homilies**, seem to be the same as the second edition of the *Recognitions* mentioned by Rufinus. Certainly, whatever he reports of that edition fits these perfectly. The most learned Jean-Baptiste Cotelier was the first to publish them in Greek along with a Latin translation.

<term>Clement’s [work] concerning the deeds, etc. <note>original: Κλημέντος περὶ τῶν πράξεων</note></term>, or the **Epitome of the deeds, travels, and preachings of Saint Peter the Apostle**: this was formerly collected from the *Recognitions* and *Homilies* by Simeon Metaphrastes (if I may be allowed a conjecture in so uncertain a matter). This can be established by comparing its latter part with the *Martyrdom of Saint Clement* published by Metaphrastes. Joachim Périon published it in Greek and Latin (*Paris, 1555, in quarto*). The Latin version is usually joined to the rest of Clement’s Works.

# The Apostolic Age.
<margin>CATCHWORD: con-</margin>

<margin>1st CENTURY from the BIRTH of CHRIST</margin>
Irenaeus, in the place cited above, records that Clement obtained the Bishopric in the third place after the Apostles. Clement had indeed seen the Blessed Apostles and had lived familiarly with them; he still had the preaching of the Apostles ringing in his ears and their tradition before his eyes.

---

### GENUINE WORKS.

<margin>
* See Eusebius, Book 5, chapter 6.
† Book 3, chapter 16.
* Codex 112.
|| On Illustrious Men, chapter 15.
† Place cited.
* Codex 126.
</margin>
**T**he **Epistle to the Corinthians**, written in the name of the Roman Church on the occasion of a very serious <term>schism <note>a formal split or division within a religious body</note></term> raging in the Corinthian Church. In this letter, he recalled them to peace and concord, and renewed their faith and the tradition they had recently received from the Apostles. This Epistle is indeed adorned with wonderful praises by the Ancients. To Irenaeus \* it is "a most powerful writing" <note>original Greek: ἱκανωτάτην γραφήν</note>; to Eusebius † it is "great and wonderful" <note>original Greek: μεγάλη τε καὶ θαυμασία</note> and "acknowledged by all" <note>original Greek: καὶ ἀνομολογουμένη παρὰ πᾶσι</note>; to Photius \* it is "noteworthy" <note>original Greek: ἀξιολόγου</note>; and to Jerome || it is "very useful." It was held in such high value by everyone that, as Eusebius † testifies, it was read publicly in most churches both in his own time and in earlier times. Photius \* judges it thus: "Its style is simple and clear, approaching the ecclesiastical and unelaborated form of speech" <note>original Greek: ἁπλῆς δὲ κατὰ τὴν φράσιν, καὶ σαφὴς ἐστὶν, καὶ ἐγγὺς τὴν ἐκκλησιαστικὴν, καὶ ἀπερίεργον χαρακτῆρος</note>. Among the learned, there is not yet agreement on the time when this epistle was written. Setting aside the opinions of others, I will bring forward the single conjecture of <term>Dodwell <note>Henry Dodwell (1641–1711), an Anglo-Irish scholar and theologian</note></term>; namely, that it was written before Clement was appointed Bishop of the city of Rome. He himself refutes with many arguments what many others suggest—that it belongs not to the time of Nero, but to the time of Domitian. (See Additions to Pearson’s Dissertation 2, chapter 6, § 24). Therefore, when in the year of Christ 64, the Corinthian Church was suffering from a heavy schism caused by certain "spiritual" men <note>original Greek: πνευματικῶν</note> who, distinguished by extraordinary gifts of the Holy Spirit, rose up against the ordinary rulers of the Church, they sent Fortunatus (whom Saint Paul calls the "firstfruits of Achaia" in 1 Corinthians 16:15), Claudius, and other legates to the Romans, imploring their help and aid. Finally, in the following year, the Roman Church replied to the Corinthians, though the <term>see <note>the seat of a bishop's office or jurisdiction</note></term> was perhaps not yet filled due to the persecution and the recent slaughter of the Apostles. Hence, Clement writes not in his own name, but in the name of the Roman Church. Thus, at the beginning of the epistle, he excuses himself for having turned his mind to their requests somewhat slowly "on account of the calamities and adverse events that have happened to us" <note>original Greek: διὰ γενομένας ἡμῖν συμφορὰς καὶ περιπτώσεις</note>. He refers (as I believe) to the persecution of Nero, which had scarcely yet subsided. These points could be supported by more evidence, but I do not wish to dwell on them any longer. These things seem very likely, yet we leave them to be discussed by the learned.

<margin>
* Book 3, chapter 38.
† Place cited.
|| Codex 112.
</margin>
**A fragment of a Second Epistle**, or rather a **Homily** <note>a religious discourse or sermon</note>, which contains an admonition and instruction for a better life. (Cotelerius also presents another fragment of this Homily in Greek in his notes, taken from the *Parallels* of John of Damascus). Eusebius \*, Jerome †, Photius ||, and others explicitly testify that this was not received and approved in the same way as the first, nor did the older writers ever use its testimony. However, there are some who strenuously defend it for our Clement, and among these is Gottfried Wendelin (who also added a Latin version to this fragment) in his *Divination on the Epistles of Clement*, inserted in the most recent edition of the Councils, Volume 1. After the learned world had despairingly mourned these Epistles as lost for so many centuries, they were recovered from the end of a very ancient Greek manuscript of the Holy Bible (<term>Codex Alexandrinus <note>A 5th-century manuscript of the Greek Bible, one of the most important for biblical scholars</note></term>), which is said to have been transcribed by the hand of Thecla the Virgin. This manuscript was sent as a gift from Patriarch Cyril to the Most Serene King of England. Patrick Young <note>Patricius Junius</note> was the first to publish them and illustrate them with very learned notes (Oxford, 1633, in 4to). They also exist in Oxford editions of 1677 in 12mo (but with Young's notes omitted), in Volume 1 of the Councils, in the 1654 Helmstedt edition by Joachim Mader, and with a collection of notes by Paul Colomiès (London, 1687, in 8vo). William Burton of London, author of the *History of the Greek Language*, translated this Epistle to the Corinthians into the English language and illustrated it with notes that are certainly not unlearned (London, 1647, in 4to). Furthermore, it is not sufficiently clear what Jerome \* means when, speaking of the Epistles written by Clement to the Corinthians, he writes that Clement had woven almost his entire discourse on the purity of virginity...
<margin>* Book 1, against Jovinian.</margin>

# Apostolic Age.
<margin>D 2</margin>
<meta>catchword: Epistle</meta>

<margin>
1st CENTURY from the BIRTH of CHRIST
</margin>
...he [Jerome] wrote that he [Clement] had woven together [praises of virginity]. For it cannot be believed that Clement sent any other Epistles to the Corinthians than those which now survive, and in these, the praises of virginity do not "fill every page" <note>original Latin: utramq; faciunt paginam — an idiom meaning to be the sole or dominant subject</note>. It seems rather to be said that Jerome, carried away by an excessive zeal for praising virginity, used hyperbolic language; and since Clement devoted a few brief periods to teaching the purity of mind and body, Jerome wished it to seem as if the entire discourse were dedicated to a formal eulogy of virginity.

## SPURIOUS WORKS.
<note>Works attributed to Clement but generally considered by scholars to be written by others or compiled later.</note>

<margin>
* Book 6, chapter 13.
|| On Illustrious Men, chapter 15.
† Codex 111.
</margin>
**Eighty-five Canons of the Apostles.** These Canons seem to have arisen from the common discipline, institutions, and decrees of the Primitive Church, and were reduced into a single <term>Synodicon <note>a collection of church decrees or canons</note></term> around the end of the second century. They are called "Apostolic," not because they were established by the Apostles themselves, but because they flowed from Apostolic doctrine and practice, or were published by Apostolic men. Whether they were first collected by Clement of Alexandria, who wrote "concerning ecclesiastical canons" <note>original Greek: περὶ κανόνων ἐκκλησιαστικῶν</note>—as Eusebius \*, Jerome ||, and Photius † assure us (and as our most learned Beveridge <note>William Beveridge (1637–1708), a prominent English bishop and scholar of church law</note> quite sharply conjectures)—let others judge. The same Beveridge explained these Canons individually and defended them against Daillé <note>Jean Daillé (1594–1670), a French Protestant scholar who questioned the antiquity of these texts</note> and his champion, both in his *Annotations on the Collection of Canons* and in his *Vindication of the Code of Canons of the Primitive Church*, published in London, 1678. These Canons are found in Greek and Latin in the *Councils*, Volume I, page 25.

**Eight books of the Apostolic Constitutions**, written around the same time as the Canons. They seem to have been first composed from various "teachings and ordinances" <note>original Greek: διδασκαλίαις & διατάξεσι</note> which Apostolic men were accustomed to publish at that time. It is most certain that these Constitutions were heavily corrupted by Heretics in the age of Epiphanius, and are far different and most diverse from those which once existed; this could easily have happened through additions, truncations, and <term>interpolations <note>later insertions or alterations made to a text</note></term>. Francisco Torres <note>Franciscus Turrianus</note> was the first to publish them in Greek, adding his own introduction and notes in Venice, 1563, in quarto. In Latin, they were published with the notes of Carlo Bovio, Paris, 1564, in octavo. In Greek and Latin by Fronton du Duc <note>Fronto Ducæus</note>, the most learned of the Jesuits in unearthing monuments of the Greek Church, at the end of the *Commentaries of Zonaras*, Paris, 1618, in folio. From there, the celebrated Philippe Labbe transferred them into his newest edition of the *Councils*, Volume I.

<margin>
† Philocalia, chapter 23, pages 81, 82.
* Preparation for the Gospel, Book 6, chapter 10.
Lives of the Primitive Fathers in Clement, volume I, number 11.
</margin>
**Ten books of Recognitions**, which have received various names: sometimes *The Acts of Saint Clement*, *History*, or *Chronicles*; sometimes *The Acts of Saint Peter*, *The Itinerary*, *The Travels* <note>original Greek: Periodi</note>, or *Dialogues with Apion*. The antiquity of these books is sufficiently proven by the fact that Origen † quotes from them under the name of *The Travels of Clement*, and those quotes are found word-for-word in today's copies. There exists a very full fragment of Bardesanes the Syrian from his *Book on Fate* in Greek in Eusebius \*, which is held in the same words today in the ninth book of the *Recognitions*. It is not entirely certain who received it from the other. What if Bardesanes himself was the compiler of these *Recognitions*? We have elsewhere set forth at length our conjecture on this matter, fortified by our own reasons. They exist in Latin from the ancient version of Rufinus (who perhaps suppressed the blemishes in them once noted by Jerome and Photius) by the care of Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples <note>Jacobus Stapulensis</note>, Paris, 1541, 1544; Cologne, 1570; and often elsewhere with the rest of Clement’s works.

**The Clementines** <note>original Greek: Τὰ Κλημέντια</note>, or **Nineteen Clementine Homilies**, seem to be the same as the second edition of the *Recognitions* mentioned by Rufinus. Certainly, whatever he reports of that edition fits these perfectly. The most learned Cotelerius <note>Jean-Baptiste Cotelier (1629–1686)</note> was the first to publish them in Greek along with a Latin translation.

**Clement's Epitome concerning the deeds, etc.** <note>original Greek: Κλήμεντος περὶ τῶν πράξεων, &c.</note> *An Epitome of the deeds, travels, and preachings of Saint Peter the Apostle*: collected long ago from the *Recognitions* and *Homilies* by Simeon Metaphrastes (if I may be allowed a conjecture in so uncertain a matter). This can be established by comparing its later part with the *Martyrdom of Saint Clement* published by Metaphrastes. Published in Greek and Latin by Joachim Périon <note>Joachimus Perionius</note>, Paris, 1555, in quarto. The Latin is usually added to the rest of Clement's Works.

# Apostolic Age.

<margin>
1st CENTURY
from the BIRTH of CHRIST.

* Codex 112.
</margin>

The **Epistle to James**, the Brother of the Lord, was formerly placed at the beginning of the *Acts of Saint Peter*, just as it is found today in the *Clementine Homilies*. This epistle varied in the two editions of the *Recognitions* (which were mentioned previously): in one edition, it was attributed to Saint Peter; in the other, to Saint Clement, as noted by Photius *. From the Greek edition of this Epistle, it is now clear how dishonestly the compilers <note>original Latin: confarcinatores — literally "those who stuff together," referring to the forgers of the Pseudo-Isidorian Decretals</note> of the **Decretal Epistles** acted; they stitched onto their Latin version a section nearly twice as long as the Epistle itself.

All these works of Clement, both genuine and spurious, were collected into one volume and clarified with very learned annotations by Jean-Baptiste Cotelier in the *Apostolic Fathers, Paris, 1672,* in folio.

Regarding Clement’s **Liturgy** and the **Decretal Epistles**, it is enough to advise once and for all that we shall not deal with them, as they have now been rejected and condemned by everyone.

---

<margin>
Year 65.
</margin>

## HERMAS, called *The Shepherd*. 
Who this Hermas was, or to whom he belonged (a name which Grotius <note>Hugo Grotius, the famous Dutch jurist and scholar</note> believes is a contraction of *Hermodorus*), is not well agreed upon by either the Ancients or the Moderns. Many maintain that this Hermas was the disciple of Paul mentioned in *Romans 16:14*, who is said to have died in the year 80 AD. The author himself seems to suggest this at the end of the *Second Vision*, where he speaks of Clement and Grapta. This opinion seems to have been supported (to say nothing of later writers) by Irenaeus, Tertullian, Clement of Alexandria, Origen, and other writers of the second and third centuries. Others, however—though not with equal reasoning, being led only by the authority of the *Liber Pontificalis* <note>The "Book of the Popes," a medieval biographical record</note>—identify him with Hermas, the brother of Pope Pius of Rome, who flourished in the following century. But the former opinion is far more consistent with the truth; certainly, the Author is very ancient and clearly belongs to the Apostolic age. He seems to have lived under Clement of Rome while the latter was Bishop; Henry Dodwell <note>A prominent 17th-century scholar of church history</note> claims Clement held the Roman See from 64 or 65 to 81 AD, while the Bishop of Chester <note>John Pearson, author of 'Annales Paulini'</note> places it from 69 to 83 AD. Indeed, in the *Second Vision*, Hermas is commanded to write two copies of his Vision; one is to be shared with <margin>Book 1, Vision 2, § 3.</margin> **Grapta** (apparently a widow of the Roman Church) so that she might admonish the widows and orphans; the other is to be sent to **Clement**, so that he might send it to foreign cities. Here (as the most learned Bishop of Oxford <note>John Fell, Bishop of Oxford, described here by the Greek phrase "he who is now among the saints"</note>, now among the saints, rightly notes), he undoubtedly means Clement, the Bishop of Rome, who, by virtue of his office, frequently sent out encyclical, introductory, and formal letters to neighboring Churches and looked after their affairs. **"That great tribulation about to come,"** which he discusses in the *Fourth Vision*, § 3, is quite aptly referred by Hammond and Dodwell to the destruction of Jerusalem—that <gloss>great tribulation <note>original Greek: θλίψιν ἐκείνην μεγάλην</note></gloss> and <gloss>coming wrath <note>original Greek: ὀργὴν μέλλουσαν</note></gloss> predicted so often in the New Testament. Consequently, he must have written before the year 70 AD, when the city of Jerusalem was captured and devastated by Titus.

He wrote three books, which still exist today in Latin. The first, titled **The Church**, contains 4 Visions granted to the Author. The second, called **The Shepherd**, contains 12 Mandates <note>or Commandments</note> delivered by a guardian Angel in the appearance of a venerable Shepherd. The third has 10 Similitudes <note>or Parables</note>, through which the author is instructed toward a blessed life worthy of a Christian. This work first appeared in *Paris*, edited by Jacques Lefèvre d'Étaples in 1513; soon after in *Strasbourg* in 1522; then among the *Orthodoxographa* of Basel and the *Library of the Fathers*, volume 2; and finally, Cotelier gave it a place, prepared with greater care and diligence, among the *Apostolic Fathers, Paris, 1672*. Caspar Barthius (who attributes the version we have to Anastasius the Librarian) published it separately with notes and appended it to his 1655 edition of Claudianus Mamertus. Most recently, the most learned Bishop of Oxford released an edition in 1685, corrected according to two manuscripts and adorned with brief scholarly notes, together with the *Epistle of Barnabas*. That these books are the same as those that existed in the first centuries of the Church is evidenced by the fact that passages cited from them by Irenaeus, Tertullian, and others are found in them today; although I would not deny that some things have been added or attached to them. Setting aside the judgments of others, I will present only <margin>* Book 3, chapter 3.</margin> what Eusebius says of this writer *: <gloss>"It should be known that this book (of Hermas) has been spoken against by some, on whose account it cannot be placed among the universally accepted works. By others, however, it is judged most necessary for those who especially need an introductory elementary instruction; hence we know it has already been read publicly in churches, and I have understood that some of the most ancient writers have made use of it." <note>This is a translation of the Greek passage from Eusebius's Ecclesiastical History, which the author also provided in a Latin translation.</note></gloss> *It should be known that this book has indeed been called into doubt by some. For which reason, among the re-*

# Apostolic Age.

<margin>
1st CENTURY AD.
</margin>

...cannot be counted among the books of accepted authority. By others, however, it is judged highly necessary, especially for those who are to be instructed in the first elements of our religion. Hence we find that it was even read publicly in the Churches, and that some of the most ancient writers used its testimony. Even Jerome <margin>† On Famous Men, chapter 10.</margin> seems to imply that in his time it was still read publicly in some Churches of Greece. It was, moreover, once of such great authority that it was often cited under the name of Scripture by Irenaeus, Tertullian, and Origen. Nevertheless, it was rejected by many of the Ancients, whose opinion the Reverend Ussher <note>James Ussher, the 17th-century Archbishop known for his biblical chronology</note> explains thus in his *Theological Library MS.* page 23: “Tertullian <margin>* On Modesty, chapters 10, 20.</margin> calls it an apocryphal and false scripture, condemned by every council of our people. Gelasius, with a Council of 70 Bishops, in his Decree <margin>† Distinction 15.</margin> on apocryphal books, rejects it among apocryphal scriptures of doubtful faith. Prosper of Aquitaine <margin>|| On Free Will.</margin> repudiates its testimony. Athanasius <margin>† Book on the Decrees of the Nicene Synod.</margin> and Theodoret <margin>* History, Book 1, chapter 18.</margin> say that the Arians <note>followers of a movement that denied the full divinity of Christ</note>, who sought testimony from this book, sought it from a non-canonical book. Indeed, that which we read in Book 3, *Similitudes*, seems to favor the heresy of the Arians: that the human seed was enriched by God along with the Son of God. It seems to have given a beginning to the heresy of the Novatians <note>a sect that refused to readmit those who had fallen away from the faith during persecution</note>, because it says more than once in Book 1, *Mandate 4*, that there is no second repentance. Clement <margin>Clement in the Stromata, Book 1.</margin> in his *Stromata* confirms from this that the Apostles preached Christ even to those in the underworld <note>original: inferos — referring to the Harrowing of Hell</note>. Origen <margin>* On First Principles, Book 3, chapter 2. † Doctrines of Principles, Book 9, chapter 14.</margin> says that two angels accompany every individual human being. Stapleton <note>Thomas Stapleton, a prominent English Catholic controversialist</note>, nevertheless, declares it possible that this book may be judged Canonical by the Roman Church.” Thus far the illustrious Ussher, who treats of this book at greater length in the same place. The Magdeburg Centuriators <note>authors of a massive 16th-century history of the Church</note> also have more in Century 2, chapter 4. But above all, the illustrious Pearson treats of it most extensively in *Vindication of Ignatius*, part 1, page 39, etc.

<margin>
67.
</margin>
**FLAVIUS JOSEPHUS**, a Noble Hebrew, had Matthias, or Mattathias, as his father. Born in Jerusalem in the year 37 AD. He traced his paternal lineage through a long series from the priestly order, and that from the first course of the <term>priestly shifts <note>original Greek: ἐξ ἐφημεριῶν (ex ephemeriōn) — referring to the weekly rotations of service for priests in the Temple</note></term>, and his maternal lineage from the royal Hasmonean family. By sect he was a Pharisee, but educated in liberal studies. In the year 63, he went to Rome to plead the cause of certain priests. Having returned to his homeland, then suffering from war and sedition, he was made leader of the Galileans, where he conducted himself nobly and bravely; until, after the capture of Jotapata by the Romans, he himself was captured. At length, released from chains by Vespasian, he presented to him the books on the *Jewish War*; for these, and on account of his reputation for learning, and especially because he had predicted the empire for Vespasian, he was honored with a statue in the city of Rome; indeed, the books written by him were placed in the public Library. Whoever wishes for more about him may consult his *Life*, written by himself. He was still among the living in the year 93 AD, but what became of him thereafter is not clear. Of his style, Photius, <term>the most critical <note>original Greek: ὁ κριτικώτατος (ho kritikōtatos)</note></term>, renders such a judgment <margin>† Codex 47.</margin>: <term>“His speech is pure, and he possesses the dignity of language combined with pleasure and clarity; he is powerful in speaking, persuasive and skilled in public oratory, and whenever the occasion calls for the use of speech, he is productive in words of reflection on both sides of an issue. He is as skilled in maxims as anyone, most capable of showing emotion in speech, and most expert at both stirring up and calming passion.” <note>original: Greek text of Photius's critique</note></term> He has a <term>candid style <note>original Latin: candidus sermo</note></term> which can express the weight and momentum of arguments with purity and delight. In his speeches, he is endowed with the power of persuasion and is graceful, even when opportunity persuades him to turn the discourse in the opposite direction. He also uses enthymemes <note>a type of logical argument where one premise is implied</note> dexterously and copiously on both sides and employs maxims as well as anyone. Moreover, he is most skilled in impressing emotion upon a speech, and in exciting movements of the soul, and then quickly soothing them. There are some among modern critics who are accustomed to pluck at his faith and learning on any given occasion; and among these, the first is Salian, the author of the *Annals of the Old Testament*; but against these I shall oppose the judgment of the one great Scaliger <note>Joseph Justus Scaliger, a premier scholar of the Renaissance</note>. “Josephus,” he says <margin>† Prolegomena to the book On the Emendation of Time.</margin>, “is the most diligent and <term>most truth-loving <note>original Greek: καὶ φιλαληθέστατος (kai philalēthestatos)</note></term> of all writers; of whom we boldly say, that he is to be trusted more safely than all the Greeks and Latins, not only in Jewish matters, but also in foreign ones”; for his faith and learning shine forth in all things.

-> WORKS. <-

**Jewish Antiquities, 20 books.** In Book 18, chapter 4, there appears that most debated and famous passage concerning Jesus Christ <note>Known as the *Testimonium Flavianum*</note>, which many among the community of critics contend is a forgery, while no fewer contend it is genuine. After such a rich harvest of work by the most learned men, it is not my place to add any further gleanings.

**The Jewish War, 7 books.** These he had previously published in his native language <note>Aramaic or Hebrew</note>, as he himself testifies in the preface.

***His own Life.*** <note>An autobiography often appended to the Antiquities</note>

***Against Apion, 2 books.*** In these, he defends the antiquity of the Jewish people against the monuments of the Greeks with the greatest wit and erudition.

<margin>† Book 3, ch. 10.</margin>
<margin>* On Famous Men, ch. 13.</margin>
**On the Dominion of Reason, or a Panegyric on the Maccabees.** Called *On Virtue* <note>original: "εἰς ἀρετὴν"</note> by Eusebius †; Jerome * calls it a *very elegant work*. Nevertheless, many learned men deny it is the work of Josephus.

These works exist in Greek, *Basel* 1544; in Latin at the same place, 1567; and in Greek and Latin, *Geneva* 1611, 1634, in folio. Certain fragments of Josephus which do not appear in the printed editions are cited by Isaac Vossius in *On the Septuagint*, pp. 37, 46, and elsewhere more than once; Suidas also has some fragments under the entry *Josephus*, p. 331; but whether Suidas has erred here, let the learned consider. Hippolytus’s treatise *On the Universe* <note>original: "περὶ τοῦ παντὸς"</note> was published in Greek under the name of Josephus by Andreas Schott in his notes on Photius's *Library*, p. 10. Yet there are almost no critics who do not judge that it is not his work.

Furthermore, it will not be useless at this point to inform the reader of a few things regarding Josephus ben Gorion <note>The medieval Hebrew chronicle known as *Josippon*</note>. This Josephus, or Josippon, wishing to sell his trifles to his countrymen as the genuine work of Flavius Josephus, clumsily erred by calling himself the son of Gorion. Scaliger <margin>* Refutation of the Three Sects, ch. 4, 5.</margin> <margin>† On Greek Historians, Book 2, ch. 8.</margin> believes he was a Jew of the French nation from the territory of Tours, and that he wrote after the year 600 AD; Vossius † agrees with him in almost every point. He wrote in Hebrew a sort of compendium of the histories of Flavius Josephus, containing the *Acts of the Seventy Translators* <note>The translation of the Septuagint</note>, the deeds of the Maccabees, and the account of the Fall of Jerusalem, which he excerpted from the Latin of Rufinus. He is a very dull and unpolished writer who betrays his ignorance and deception almost everywhere; yet he is the sole source from whom Jewish writers take whatever they have concerning the Maccabees and the Fall of Jerusalem. The Jews first published it at *Constantinople* in Hebrew; then Sebastian Münster (omitting most of the more offensive fables) published it in Hebrew and Latin with notes at *Worms* 1529 and *Basel* 1541. It also appeared in Hebrew at *Venice* 1544 and *Kraków* 1589. The celebrated Ussher treats him very extensively in his *Theological Library MS.* p. 116, etc.

<margin>70.</margin>
MARTIALIS, Bishop and Apostle of the Limousin in Gaul, came from the East to Rome (as they say) together with St. Peter, and was sent by him from there into the Gauls. There, having converted those of Limoges, Tours, and others to the faith, he died (as appears from his *Acts*) in the year 74. I disregard what they have written about him as being entirely uncertain and legendary, such as the *Acts* forged by Aurelian, the pupil and successor of Saint Martial; for they do not seem to have even the slightest shadow of truth. For several centuries now, there has been sharp dispute among French writers concerning his era; some assign him to this century, others push him back to the third, and even now (says Labbe) the case is still before the judge. The first, as far as I know, to mention him is <margin>† History of the Franks, Book 1, ch. 30.</margin> Gregory of Tours †, drawing from the history of the passion of Saturninus; he writes that Saint Martial was sent into the Gauls and appointed Bishop of Limoges only during the reign of the Emperor Decius and his consulship, that is, in the year 254 AD. Two Epistles are attributed to this Martial, one to the people of Bordeaux, the other to the people of Toulouse, found in the sacristy of the basilica of St. Peter in the city of Limoges during the reign of Philip, King of the Franks, as is claimed by the Elmenhorst manuscript; which further falsely asserts they were hidden there during the heat of Domitian’s persecution. They were first published by Badius Ascensius, *Paris* 1521; then frequently reprinted in the *Library of the Fathers*; and also edited with notes by Geverhard Elmenhorst at the end of Gennadius's *On Church Dogmas*, *Hamburg* 1614. There are many signs in these Epistles of a much later age,

This has been demonstrated at greater length by both the Reformers and the Catholics <note>original: "Reformati, quàm Pontificii"</note>, namely Possevino, Bellarmine, Aubert Miraeus, Labbe, and others. Those who care for such matters may consult them; for the matter is not of such great importance that we should linger on it any longer. <margin>70.</margin>
### PROCHORUS,
said to be the cousin of Saint Stephen the Protomartyr, was one of the seven Deacons established by the Apostles in Acts 6. He was also a listener and disciple of Saint John. He flourished around the year 70. He is said to have written the *Life of Saint John the Evangelist and Apostle*, which exists in Greek and Latin in the *Orthodoxographa* (Basel, 1569). A more complete version in Latin only is found in the *Library of the Fathers* <note>original: "Biblioth. Patr."</note>, volume 2. However, the style, vocabulary (entirely unknown in that age), and the narratives and fables found throughout such a short book clearly proclaim it to be a forgery <note>original: "supposititiam"</note>. This is also admitted by Baronius (year 92), Lorinus in his commentary on Acts 6, Brissot, Bellarmine, Labbe, and others. I would readily agree with Bellarmine, Vossius, and others that this book is the same as the *Circuit of John* <note>Known today as the *Acts of John*</note>, which is listed among the Apocrypha by Athanasius.

---

<margin>80.</margin>
<margin>Irenaeus, Book 3, ch. 11.</margin>
<margin>Eusebius, Book 3, ch. 28. Epiphanius, Heresy 28.</margin>
<margin>Theodoret, *Compendium of Heretical Fables*, Book 2, ch. 3.</margin>
### CERINTHUS,
a Jew by birth, it seems, flourished around the year 80. Having lived for a long time in Egypt and being learned in philosophical sciences, he finally embraced the Christian faith, yet in such a way that he retained his zeal for the Jewish religion and resented the admission of Gentiles into the Church. For this reason, he brought charges against Saint Peter for the baptism granted to Cornelius. In Antioch, he caused disturbances by teaching the necessity of observing the Mosaic law and insisting that proselytes be circumcised; he also accused Saint Paul of profaning the Temple of Jerusalem by bringing in Greeks. Later, however, he withdrew from the Church, spread his heresy in Asia, and established a sect named after himself. He taught that the world was created not by God, but by certain separate powers ignorant of God. He claimed that Jesus was born of Joseph, and that finally, at his baptism, "the Christ" descended upon him from above in the form of a dove, only to fly away again before the Passion—so that Jesus was crucified as a mere man. He taught that after the end of the age, Christ would reign in Jerusalem for a thousand years, granting the resurrected saints an abundance of every carnal pleasure. He devised other monstrous errors which the Valentinians <note>A later Gnostic sect</note> later organized into a system of absurdities. Irenaeus records that Saint John published his Gospel primarily to refute this man’s heresy. The holy Apostle held him in such hatred that, when he was about to enter a bathhouse in Ephesus and heard Cerinthus was inside, he immediately turned back, saying to his companions:
> *Let us flee, lest the bathhouse collapse, in which Cerinthus, the enemy of truth, is staying.*

<margin>† *Dialogue against the Luciferians*.</margin>
Jerome adds † that the bathhouse collapsed immediately, crushing Cerinthus in the sudden ruin. However, other historians are silent on that point. According to Caius (cited in Eusebius, Book 3, ch. 28), Cerinthus wrote certain "Revelations" which he falsely claimed were revealed to him by an angel, in which he dreamed up many things about the millennium. Indeed, according to Dionysius of Alexandria (cited in the same place), there were those who claimed the *Apocalypse* <note>The Book of Revelation</note> was forged by Cerinthus under the name of Saint John—an idea perhaps born from the fact that the book seems to favor the doctrine of the Millenarians <note>original: "Chiliastarum"</note>, which Cerinthus strenuously defended.

<margin>90.</margin>
<margin>* In *His own Life*.</margin>
### JUSTUS,
a Jew and the son of Pistus, was born in Tiberias in Galilee. He was a man of great reputation among his people, and "how distinguished his family was" <note>original: "καὶ τὸ αὐτοῦ γένος πῶς ἐπιφανής"</note>, as Josephus testifies of his father *. Great and serious enmities existed between him and Josephus; Josephus writes much on this in his autobiography, where he detects and refutes Justus's lies and wards off his slanders. Justus flourished in the year 90. He wrote a *Chronicle of the Kings of the Jews* who were crowned, beginning from Moses and leading down to the death of Agrippa, the last of the Jewish kings (that is, to the year 100). He also wrote *Commentaries on the Jewish War*, which he wanted to keep hidden for twenty years so that his lies would not be proven wrong; this is a charge Josephus brings against him. I would clearly conjecture that these...
