# Apostolic Age.

<margin>
1st CENTURY AD
† Under the entry "Tiberias."
* Codex 33.
</margin>
...I would consider these commentaries to have been that part of his *Chronicle* in which he touched upon the affairs of the Jews under Vespasian and Titus, unless I were forced to think otherwise by Stephanus of Byzantium <note>original: "Stephanus de Urb."</note> †, who says that Justus "chronicled the Jewish war against Vespasian" <note>original Greek: "Τὸν Ἰουδαϊκὸν πόλεμον τὸν κατ’ Οὐεσπασιανοῦ ἱστορῆσαι"</note>. Photius says he used a "smooth and flowery" style <note>original Greek: "ἀνθομαλάτῳ"</note>, and that he omitted many of the most necessary details in his report; he adds that Justus suffered from the common vice of the Hebrews, in that, being a Jew, he made absolutely no mention of the coming of Christ, the things that happened to him, or the miracles he performed. Josephus himself admits that Justus was not unlearned in Greek disciplines, but that he was tainted by a kind of popular eloquence and verbal trickery, by which he could easily overcome those advising more sound ideas. The writings of this Justus either remain hidden somewhere, or, what is more likely, perished some centuries ago.

## Gentile Writings Touching Upon Christ in This Century.

ACTA PILATI, or the *Report to Emperor Tiberius* concerning our Lord and his Death, Resurrection, and Ascension, and the crimes of which he was accused before him. No one who is not a stranger to Roman history is unaware that it was a fixed Roman custom for Proconsuls and Governors of Provinces to create Records (*Acta*) of the things done under them and transmit them to the Emperor or the Senate. Eusebius long ago noted this in this very matter (Book 2, Chapter 2): "An ancient custom prevailed among the governors of the nations" <note>original Greek: "Παλαιὸν κεκρατηκὸς ἔθος τοῖς τῶν ἐθνῶν ἄρχουσι"</note>. It was an old custom, he says, for the Governors of Provinces to report whatever new thing occurred among them to the Emperor, so that nothing would be hidden from him. Therefore, Pilate reported to the Emperor Tiberius concerning the Resurrection of our Savior Jesus Christ, whose fame was celebrated throughout all Palestine; he also indicated that he had heard of many other miracles of his, and how, having been restored to life, he was already considered a God by many. Justin Martyr and Tertullian, who are older than Eusebius, also testify to this. The latter, in his *Apology against the Gentiles* (Chapter 21, page 20), mentions this report to Tiberius and says that Pilate himself was already a Christian in his conscience. Justin, in his *Second Apology to Antoninus
<margin>
† P. 76. C. 81. C.
</margin>
Pius* †, appeals to these Acts of Pilate more than once: "And that these things happened, you can learn from the acts recorded under Pontius Pilate" <note>original Greek: "Καὶ ταῦτα ὅτι γέγονε, δύνασθε μαθεῖν ἐκ τῶν ἐπὶ Ποντίου Πιλάτου γενομένων ἄκτων"</note>. Both heretics and Gentiles corrupted these Acts, or at least created others in imitation of them. Epiphanius tells this of the heretics (*Heresies*, Book 1), implying he had used more than one copy of those Acts. The Gentiles, however, during the reign of Diocletian, forged Acts under Pilate’s name out of hatred for Christians; Maximinus Augustus <note>Maximinus Daza, an emperor who persecuted Christians</note> finally published these and ordered them to be posted publicly everywhere, both in the countryside and in the cities. He even ordered schoolmasters to give these to boys as lessons to be declaimed and committed to memory, as reported by Eusebius (Book 9, Chapter 5; See also Book 1, Chapter 9). These Acts, both the genuine and the forged, perished long ago. There is indeed a Letter from Pilate to Emperor Claudius on this very subject found in the *Summary* <note>original: "Anacephal."</note> of Pseudo-Hegesippus (page 683). But it betrays itself immediately by its own vanity, and is therefore deservedly rejected by everyone.
